# 🤖 database, LLM and agent systems that write specifications or proofs
(authored by agents unless marked 🧑)

370 sources, best-read first, then newest first. Generated from `sources.jsonl`; edit the reviews' notes, not this page.

- AeroReq2LTL (2026)
    - title: Automated LTL Specification Generation from Industrial Aerospace Requirements
    - what: Before translating requirements to LTL (a logic for statements about sequences of events), it adds a domain data dictionary and controlled requirement templates. It compares the results to formalizations written by experts for aerospace requirements.
    - number: 85% precision and 88% recall
    - limits: It covers one industrial domain, and its authors overlap with those of Req2LTL. It is not shown to work for the intent of distributed services.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.21715); [notes: Natural language to temporal logic and TLA+](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_6_intent_to_spec.md)
    - record id: `aeroreq2ltl-2026`
- AGENT LTL procedural compliance (2026)
    - title: AgentLTL: A Trace-Verification Framework for Measuring, Enforcing, and Training Procedural Compliance in Tool-Using LLM Agents
    - what: Introduces AGENT LTL, a first-order linear temporal logic for typed tool traces. The same constraints yield deterministic judge-free compliance scores, online tool-call blocking or warnings, and dense reinforcement-learning rewards, including grounding checks tying answer entities to tool outputs. Defines six weighted constraint layers over ordering, branching, argument extraction, pair ordering, global sequence, and grounding; builds 12 workflow templates and held-out transfer splits; compares baseline, soft-block, and block-and-warn harnesses; and finetunes Qwen3-4B with the compliance score. A trace-grounding predicate exposes answers based on parametric memory rather than retrieved evidence.
    - number: Block-and-warn improves compliance on five of seven models and raises accuracy by +0.019 on average (§5.1)
    - number: Finetuning yields +38 and +17.5 percentage-point gains in accuracy and compliance on held-out patterns (Abstract)
    - number: Benchmark has 12 workflow templates; 1,917 valid traces collected for grounding (§4.3–4.4)
    - number: Production benchmark contains 360 instances; held-out splits are 83/100/39/39 instances (§4.3)
    - limits: Compliance is relative to authored constraints, typed tool signatures, trace logging, and regex/AST grounding extraction. Block-and-warn can alter trace length and strong models may regress; grounding is a sufficient evidence predicate, not complete semantic truth. Finetuning and held-out results cover seven models and the supplied templates rather than arbitrary agent tasks.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    -
    - record id: `agent-ltl-procedural-compliance-2026`
- Agentic-IC3: Enabling Semantic Proof Search in IC3 Model Checking (2026)
    - what: Integrates a language-model agent into a persistent word-level IC3 model checker so RTL semantics and solver feedback guide lemma generalization, derived observations and backtracking. Specifies an agent-oriented interface over Pono/Bitwuzla proof state, with SAT and UNSAT batches, solver-checked lemma admission, observation definitions and revision of earlier proposals. Detailed trajectories show semantic equalities for multiplier completion, occupancy observations relating shift-register and ring FIFOs, and state-guarded backtracking for floating-point adders. The evaluation and ablations separate proof success from interaction cost and demonstrate where semantic actions help or fail.
    - number: 10/14 Agentic-IC3 cases solved within 3,600 seconds; rIC3 and A-IC3 each solve 5, Pono-IC3Bits solves 0
    - number: four cases are unsolved by all three baselines
    - number: agent responses account for 87% of aggregate completed-run time
    - number: batching reduces lemma-proposal generalizations 515 to 137 (73%) and solves all ten
    - limits: The backend remains the soundness boundary: proposed lemmas are admitted only after SAT/SMT checks. Experiments use Codex CLI GPT-5.6-Sol, one CPU core, 16 GiB, one-hour timeout, 14 selected RTL benchmarks and fresh conversations, so stochastic repeatability and model dependence are unresolved. Current observations are stateless; quantified lemmas and stateful history require future backend extensions. Agent waiting dominates runtime.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    -
    - record id: `agentic-ic3-enabling-semantic-2026`
- Agentic Proving for Program Verification (Claude Code on CLEVER, Lean 4) (2026)
    - what: Runs Claude Opus 4.6 in Claude Code, with Lean tools, on the 161 CLEVER algorithm tasks. The agent writes formal specifications, implementations and Lean proofs. People manually review whether each specification matches the English task.
    - number: 159/161 tasks get an arguably valid specification (98.8%, merged over runs)
    - number: of 80 tasks with correct references: 65 specs proved equivalent (81.3%), 70 implementations certified (87.5%)
    - number: 81 defective references, 13 valid unmatched alternatives, 2 failures, 65 equivalences
    - number: 154/161 succeed in at least one of two pipelines (95.7%); 154/157 after excluding 4
    - limits: Only proofs that Lean programs compute the right results. No proofs about distributed progress. The 98.1% headline number merges two runs. The authors review the reference-error count themselves. Human effort is not measured.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23772v1); [notes: Agentic Proving for Program Verification](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_11_second_pass_agents_invariants.md); [notes: Agentic Proving for Program Verification](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_14_program_agents.md)
    - record id: `agentic-proving-for-program-2026`
- Agentic requirement formalization (2026)
    - title: Towards an Agentic LLM-based Approach to Requirement Formalization from Unstructured Specifications
    - what: Proposes a modular agentic pipeline: extract requirements from unstructured text, classify compatibility with a target formalism, translate suitable requirements into UPPAAL statistical model-checking queries, then semantically evaluate outputs against manually built ground truth. Separating extraction, verifiability filtering and translation avoids forcing one prompt to infer both intent and model limitations. Three cyber-physical scenarios provide 19 natural-language requirements, 12 verifiable; Gemini 3.1 Pro preview performs each stage and an LLM judge assesses semantic equivalence beyond exact string matching.
    - number: Requirement extraction combined accuracy 81.8%
    - number: Verifiability classification 88.7% accuracy, 94.2% recall
    - number: Translation relaxed semantic accuracy 77.8%; exact textual match 34.7%
    - number: Scenario SC3 exact 20.6% versus semantic 94.1% after judge
    - limits: Ground truth is manually constructed and semantic judge can introduce bias. UPPAAL SMC constraints restrict which requirements are expressible; model abstractions explain discarded requirements. Single model and three scenarios limit external validity; no end-to-end proof that translated queries capture all informal intent.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.18228)
    - record id: `agentic-requirement-formalization-2026`
- Agentproof (2026)
    - title: Agentproof: Static Verification of Agent Workflow Graphs
    - what: Agentproof pulls a workflow graph out of LangGraph, CrewAI, AutoGen or ADK programs. It compiles temporal policies (rules about the order of events) into automata and checks every path in the model. It also has a runtime mode.
    - number: 18 workflows with injected defects; 15 policies; 180 random traces
    - number: human-gate check precision 0.75
    - number: 5,000-node graph structural checks in 104.7 ms
    - limits: It models only safety and structure. It finds whether an exit path is absent, but does not prove that the workflow eventually exits. The graph extractor is trusted. The test workflows are synthetic.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.20356); [notes: Agentproof: Static Verification of Agent Workflow Graphs](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_12_second_pass_intent_eval.md)
    - record id: `agentproof-2026`
- AgentSpec (2026)
    - title: AgentSpec: Customizable Runtime Enforcement for Safe and Reliable LLM Agents
    - what: AgentSpec is a rule language for runtime monitors that intercept the actions of an LLM agent. Each rule has a trigger, a check, and an intervention. Rules are written by hand or generated by an LLM.
    - number: 25 risk categories, 30 cases each
    - number: manual rules: household hazard to zero, safe completion 58.62% to 54.26%
    - number: generated embodied rules 95.56% precision, 70.96% recall; generated code rules detect 87.26% risky cases
    - limits: It enforces safety only on executions that were tested. Nothing shows that the rules cover what the user intended. It does not cover liveness (the system eventually doing something good).
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.18666); [notes: AgentSpec: Customizable Runtime Enforcement for Safe and Reliable LLM ](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_12_second_pass_intent_eval.md)
    - record id: `agentspec-2026`
- Agora (2026)
    - title: Agora: Toward Autonomous Bug Detection in Production-Level Consensus Protocols with LLM Agents
    - what: Three LLM agents propose adversarial scenarios under stated fault constraints. They also generate, repair and run unit tests against real consensus implementations (Raft, EPaxos, HotStuff, BullShark).
    - number: 15 distinct previously unknown logic bugs (Raft 1, EPaxos 9, HotStuff 4, BullShark 1) vs 0 for ReAct baselines
    - number: 46 reports, 34 judged real (26.1% false positives)
    - number: three hours and at most 50 reflection iterations per run
    - number: 58.6M tokens Gemini ablation; 5.32M tokens and about $40 per bug
    - limits: It only tests. It proves nothing about safety or progress. The notes' author did not independently confirm the bugs. Some attacks erase volatile state, so whether they reveal a defect depends on the crash-recovery contract. It needs human domain knowledge.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- AI-assisted Isabelle type-annotation formalization (2026)
    - title: Just Type It in Isabelle! AI Agents Drafting, Mechanizing, and Generalizing from Human Hints
    - what: Formalizes the complete and minimal type-annotation problem for rank-one polymorphic lambda terms, including the Smolka–Blanchette printing algorithm, in Isabelle/HOL. The paper compares human and AI pen-and-paper proofs, AI mechanization, and human-hinted generalization. Gives a precise completeness/minimality specification, fixes an Isabelle implementation issue that could drop annotations, and reports separate human and Claude Opus 4.6 workflows. The AI formalizes both proof drafts, discovers or receives hints for generalization via interval-set theory, and produces sorry-free Isabelle developments without the authors writing Isabelle code.
    - number: Three Isabelle formalizations obtained without authors writing Isabelle code (§1)
    - number: AI-created autoformalizable document cost $70, about two hours compute, and one day of human review (§6.4)
    - number: Human and AI Isabelle developments were 1,938 lines/$139 and 2,071 lines/$140 (§6.4)
    - number: Autogeneralization experiment cost $93 and took less than three hours (§7)
    - limits: The metatheory targets rank-one polymorphic lambda terms and the studied annotation algorithm; full minimality is NP-hard, so implementation uses tractable greedy local minimality. AI outputs are capable but not logically infallible and require human reviews. The case study does not establish general reliability across models or proof domains.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15713)
    - record id: `ai-assisted-isabelle-type-annotation-formalization-2026`
- AlgoVeri (2026)
    - title: AlgoVeri: An Aligned Benchmark for Verified Code Generation on Classical Algorithms
    - what: A benchmark of 77 classical algorithms. Experts wrote matching specs in Dafny, Verus and Lean. Models generate code and proofs, then repair them using verifier error messages. An LLM judge filters out cheating and degenerate algorithms.
    - number: 77 algorithms
    - number: Gemini-3 Flash 1x15: acceptance/filtered Dafny 55.84/40.26, Verus 25.97/24.68, Lean 9.09/7.79 percent
    - number: GPT-5.3 Codex 1x8: Dafny 49.35/42.86, Verus 14.29/11.69, Lean 23.38/11.69
    - number: Best (Gemini-3 Flash, 15 repair rounds): 40.3% Dafny, 24.7% Verus, 7.8% Lean
    - limits: The specs are given, not discovered. The filter is a judgment call. Only termination of sequential programs is covered. Repair budgets differ between rows. No expert effort is reported.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- AlphaProof Nexus (2026)
    - title: Advancing Mathematics Research with AI-Driven Formal Proof Search
    - what: LLM agents write Lean proofs (a proof assistant whose compiler checks every step) for open math problems. The simplest agent, an LLM looping with Lean compiler error messages, matched a complex one with evolutionary search and a trained prover on the Erdős problems it solved, but cost more on the hardest.
    - number: 9 of 353 open Erdős problems solved by the full-featured agent, at a few hundred USD per problem
    - number: 44 of 492 OEIS conjectures proved
    - number: Basic agent (LLM plus Lean feedback) solved all 9 Erdős problems; full agent 2x to 5x cheaper on #138 and #125, roughly half as cost-efficient on the rest
    - number: AlphaProof alone, and basic agent on smaller Gemini models: 0 of 9; Codex (GPT-5.5): 7 of 9; Claude Code (Opus 4.7): 0 of 9
    - limits: Math, not software or distributed systems. Only 353 problems that volunteers had already formalized in Lean; success concentrated where Lean's math library is mature. Statement faithfulness checked by hand by experts after each solve. Cost counts exclude the compute spent screening all 353 problems. Commercial-agent comparison is one setting each ('goal' mode).
    - how much was read: most of it read

- An Empirical Study of LLM-Generated Specifications for VeriFast (2026)
    - what: # An Empirical Study of LLM-Generated Specifications for VeriFast (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Wen Fan, Minh Tran, Sanya Dod, Xin Hu, Marilyn Rego, Danning Xie, Jenna DiVincenzo and Lin Tan, arXiv:2606.26490v1, 25 June 2026. This is an empirical study of LLM-written specifications and auxiliary proofs for C programs checked with VeriFast. It reports 855/2,727 outputs passing verification (31.4%), 91% source-behavior preservation and 92% contract preservation. The last figure combines equivalent and strengthened contracts; it is not an equivalence rate. The released verification CSVs contain 856 “yes” rows, one more than the paper. No general correctness theorem or end-to-end proof of an LLM verifier is given (Q1,Q8,Q9,T3–T6,C8).  ## Reading and source scope  Read all 13 PDF pages, sections I–VIII, both figures, all eight tables, displayed code/specifications and all 83 bibliography entries. All 13 individual page renders were inspected. There is no appendix, checklist, numbered theorem/proof or separately numbered algorithm. Figure 1 is a complete append-tail verification example; Figure 2 diagrams the study. References were read as bibliography entries, not recursively followed.  The inventory PDF matches its recorded SHA256. Acquired the paper's §VIII replication package, Zenodo record 19570523, DOI 10.5281/zenodo.19570523; its 17,362,580-byte ZIP matches the published MD5 8c8335ee601057daf18c27d3cf9097e3. Inventoried 14,646 ZIP members. The archive's ISSRE26 filename does not establish a publication venue. Its metadata identifies an anonymous replication package published 14 April and updated 20 April 2026, preceding the arXiv version; correspondence to every reported run is not established.  Read the root and two infrastructure readmes, all selected analyzer and generation scripts, four original append-tail inputs/ground truth, three generated append-tail FBP outputs and four generated files containing literal assume(false). The 15 released CSVs were parsed as plaintext to count labels, unique benchmark names and selected rows; modified.txt was counted, not semantically reviewed. In total 43 plaintext assets are retained, with per-asset reading boundaries. A bounded lexical scan covered the 2,727 primary full-study C outputs, without treating it as whole-output semantic reading. XLSX workbooks, remaining inputs/outputs, retrieved examples/tutorial bodies, libraries, raw checker/API logs and recursively cited papers were not read. No author code was executed/imported, verifier rerun, model/checkpoint loaded or experiment reproduced. Quotes retain exact source bytes and LF/character pointers; arithmetic and static control-flow findings are reader checks, not new experimental results.  ## Task, logic and benchmark assumptions  Separation logic describes ownership of disjoint heap regions. VeriFast uses symbolic execution, contracts, predicates, loop invariants, folding/unfolding statements and lemma calls to check safety and specified behavior. The paper describes Z3 as the default solver. Although background mentions VeriFast 25.11, actual experiments use 24.08.30 with default integer-overflow and heap-leakage checks. The record therefore does not transfer outcomes to another release or to Verus/Rust (Q2,Q7,Q8).  The dataset has 303 functions from 60 small single-file C programs: 258 functions from 50 public examples and 45 from ten author-written programs. Selection starts with 770 repository programs, narrows to 160 single-file example/tutorial programs, then removes 46 lacking code or SL specifications, 49 duplicates or prompt-tutorial examples, ten with unresolved verification errors and five without prepared inputs. This deliberately retains known-verifiable programs, not arbitrary industrial code. Overall ground truth averages 43 source and 81 specification lines, with ranges 3–143 and 5–600. The public and new subsets average 44/91 and 34/32 lines respectively (Q3).  Each function receives one mutually exclusive feature tag, prioritizing concurrency, then loops, then recursive predicates: 45 concurrent, 39 loop, 134 recursive-predicate and 85 other functions. A function tagged concurrency may also contain a loop; these are not independent feature effects. The authors' ten new programs resemble the public set and are used for both selection and the full study. Their non-leakage is asserted, not independently established; a low overall verification rate cannot itself measure contamination (Q3,Q12).  Three inputs retain executable code but differ in guidance: NL gives informal behavior, FB formal pre/postconditions, and FBP adds allocation ownership or integer constraints needed for verification. FBP is closer to a complete solution and asks a different completion task from NL. Paper Table I's append-tail example specifies an acyclic input/output list; Figure 1's contract tracks ownership and acyclic shape, not a value sequence, preserved prefix or insertion of exactly one value. A program satisfying only that contract has not thereby proved the full usual append operation. The retained artifact's list nodes omit the value field and its append_tail takes only head; this is a simpler example variant, not a byte-identical implementation of Figure 1 (Q2,Q3,T1,F1,C10).  ## Prompt/model selection and measured workflow  Four prompt contents, Basic, chain-of-thought, sparse retrieval and dense retrieval, cross whole-program versus function input for eight choices. The first pilot uses GPT-4o and the 45 new functions' FBP variants. Sparse retrieval with function splitting verifies 64%, versus at most 56% for alternatives, with 63 errors versus more than 67; its three logical errors and two behavior changes also influence selection. These are selection-set results, not independent held-out estimates (Q4).  A second pilot chooses Claude 3.7 Sonnet, Gemini 2.5 Pro and GPT-4o from ten models: selected models verify at least 60%, have at most 73 errors and at most two behavior changes. A follow-up crosses ten models with eight prompts over the same 45 FBP functions; however, deprecated Claude 3.7 is replaced by Haiku 4.5. It therefore supplies broader prompting evidence without evaluating exactly the same model set. Sparse split and unsplit tie at median 16 verified functions; selected model families' reported medians are 23, 22.5 and 20.5, versus at most 15 for the rest. It does not remove reuse of the selection set or isolate all input/model interactions (Q5).  The full study fixes sparse retrieval with splitting and evaluates three models × three input types × 303 functions, producing 2,727 outputs. Temperature is zero, with one output per condition, not repeated sampling. Sparse retrieval uses BM25, dense retrieval TextEmbedding, and vectors reside in Qdrant. The retained prompt code retrieves ten program/library entries and five tutorial entries, caps concatenated example text at 300,000 characters and extracts C-fenced blocks. Main generation calls each model once per input, without a verifier-feedback loop. The code's active model list is the later ten-model configuration including Haiku, not a preserved exact full-study configuration; historical correspondence needs logs (Q4–Q8,Q12,C6,C7).  Reported local hardware is a Yoga 14sACH 2021, 16 AMD Ryzen 7 5800HS CPUs, 16 GiB RAM, 1 TB SSD, Ubuntu 24.04 and Python 3.12; Qdrant has 0.5 vCPU, 1 GiB RAM and 4 GiB disk. Full-study API costs per model are Gemini $233, Claude $232 and GPT-4o $171. The paper supplies no repeated-run uncertainty, formal significance test for its “significantly” wording or complete token/latency account (Q7,Q10).  ## What the outcomes establish  RQ1 manually compares source behavior and contracts, with at least two authors coding independently and resolving conflicts. Source changes include compiler errors as well as semantic changes. Table III has 246 changes: NL 64, FB 82, FBP 100; Claude 86, Gemini 98 and GPT-4o 62. Source inter-rater Cohen's kappa is 0.73. The prose divides 246 changes into 102 semantic edits and 143 compile-related cases, which sum to 245. Their overlap or an omitted case is not resolved by the paper (Q6,Q8,T3).  Table IV reports 2,375 equivalent, 146 strengthened, 116 weakened and 90 other contracts. The advertised 92% combines 2,375+146=2,521, while exact-equivalence coding is 87.1%. The authors define strengthening jointly for pre- and postconditions, without a formal behavioral-refinement criterion. A stronger postcondition can add guarantees; a stronger precondition can exclude previously allowed inputs. Thus eq+str does not automatically mean preservation of the original contract on its whole domain. This is a logical qualification, not a finding that all strengthened examples are wrong. NL accounts for 114 of 116 weakened cases; 66/90 other cases have missing or duplicate specifications. Contract-coding kappa is 0.38, explicitly “fair” in §IV-A2, despite §V calling the pair “moderate to substantial”; more than 275 NL coding conflicts were resolved (Q6,Q9,Q12,T4).  RQ2's 31.4% is a checker-success rate, not a reported joint rate of unchanged source, adequate original contract and complete verification. Table V gives NL/FB/FBP averages 24.6/29.5/39.9%, and Claude/Gemini/GPT-4o averages 29.8/39.7/24.5%. Gemini is highest for every input type; Claude FB is lower than its NL result, so formal guidance does not improve every individual cell. FBP success also changes the supplied information, rather than proving a particular prompting mechanism causes better reasoning (Q8,Q10,T5).  Table VI's feature averages are 51.2% other/basic SL, 30.9% recursive predicates, 12.3% concurrency and 11.4% loops. Gemini's hard-case figures are 21.5% concurrency and 21.4% loops, versus Claude 10.4/9.4% and GPT-4o 5.2/3.4%. These describe this curated, tagged dataset and one output per condition; they do not establish distribution-free model rankings (T6,Q10,Q12).  ## Error analysis and concrete cases  RQ3 selects 50 low-success functions, then examines their 354 failing variants. It is not a random sample of every failure: category success thresholds are 33/33/44/56%, quotas 7/7/22/14, all eligible author cases are chosen first and public cases fill remaining quotas randomly. Authors repair each failing output until verification, record errors/severity/location/fix and reconcile independent analyses, preferring fewer modifications when repairs differ. The coding and repair order affect which errors become visible; no RQ3 agreement metric is reported (Q6,Q11,Q12).  Table II gives four useful distinctions. Misplaced queue pre/postconditions cause a compile error. Opening heap predicates reveals that both null heaps have INT_MAX minimum values, a practical automation step. A list-segment concatenation lemma restores an invariant after advancing a pointer. An array loop invariant recording only ten arbitrary elements is logically too weak to prove ten zeros and needs an initialized-prefix invariant. The last example is a genuine missing property, not merely absent predicate unfolding (T2).  Tables VII–VIII contain 1,652 coded errors: 383 compile, 1,167 practical and 102 theoretical, or 93.8% in the first two categories. Component totals are predicates/contracts 381, open/close/assert/leak 767, lemmas 331, invariants 61, source 55 and other 57. The 94% headline is this operational taxonomy; it does not experimentally identify models' internal knowledge or prove that errors are unrelated to general reasoning. There are 858 required-heap-chunk failures, 51.9% of all errors, and 58 missing/incorrect malloc-block cases. These are errors in selected failing outputs, not percentages of all generated functions (Q11,T7,T8).  Of the 767 open/close/assert/leak errors, reported causes are missing 58%, redundant 31%, misplaced 7% and wrong 4%. Lemma errors are 62% missing and 23% redundant. Invariant errors include 20% missing, 25% too weak and 20% too strong. For the 381 predicate/contract errors the prose's 44% compiler share matches 167/381, but its syntax 22% plus type 13% totals only 35%; the missing percentage is not explained. The table, finer prose fractions and denominators should remain distinct (Q11,T7).  The authors report VeriFast's automatic folding/unfolding can fix 39.4% of open/close errors. This is not 39.4% of all errors, all functions, or an evaluated end-to-end increase in passing functions. Heap-state feedback, better error messages and retry loops are proposals; the measured main study does not deploy a feedback agent (Q11,Q13).  ## Static artifact checks and trust boundary  The analyzer keeps a named target function and replaces other parsed executable bodies with `//@ assume(false)`. Therefore passes concern a selected function under remaining declarations/contracts; they are not whole-program proofs that all generated callees were independently verified. The extractor accepts C function declarations and function typedefs as well as definitions, while target eligibility checks names rather than requiring a body. This further limits treating every row as verification of an implemented function. These are inspected source behaviors, not measured false-positive rates (C1–C3).  The subprocess wrapper labels success by the substring “0 errors found”; it does not check the process exit status, set a timeout or reject assumption markers. The contract prefilter compares nonempty dumped AST strings of declarations and initial definitions, then marks the rest “unknown” for manual review. That is syntactic triage, not a proof of semantic equivalence or strengthening. The shell source prefilter strips comments, preprocessor directives and whitespace before diffing; it is also triage. Neither prefilter replaces the paper's manual resolved coding (C1,C4,C5).  Each of the nine verification CSVs has 303 distinct benchmark names. Their yes counts in Claude/Gemini/GPT order are NL 79/109/36, FB 73/118/77 and FBP 119/135/110, totaling 856. In particular Gemini FBP is 135/303=44.55%, whereas Table V prints 44.2% (compatible with 134/303). The current wrapper emits “parse error” or “other error”, but the released CSVs use “no”; source and published reports therefore cannot be assumed to be an unchanged exact run snapshot. The six formal-contract CSVs contain 1,307 syntactically equivalent and 511 unknown cases; these are prefilter results, not the 2,375 final manually equivalent count that also includes NL. The source-change prefilter lists 780 candidate paths, not 780 final semantic changes. No raw logs or resolved XLSX coding were inspected to settle provenance or join all outcome dimensions (C8,C9,reader-static-scan.json).  A lexical scan finds “assume” in 32/2,727 primary output files and exact “assume(false)” in four. The four were then read whole. Gemini's generated thread_start explicitly uses contradiction to trust its stub; its CSV label is “no”. Claude's charreader contains an assumed helper lemma and is also “no”. Gemini's barrier_thread2 is “no”; barrier_main is “yes”, but its assumptions occur in a non-target barrier helper. The analyzer removes that helper's executable body when checking main, so this does not establish that the passing target relied on those exact assumption statements. It does establish that a “yes” row and unchanged executable behavior do not by themselves document the complete proof trust boundary. These literal counts are neither counts of unsound verified targets nor exhaustive checks for all spellings of assumptions (C11–C14,C8).  All three retained generated append-tail FBP outputs are “no” in their CSVs. They attempt segment lemmas/invariants but differ from ground truth; this is a concrete artifact example of proof-structure difficulty, not a reader-reproduced verifier failure. Shape contracts and the artifact's simpler value-free node representation remain explicit (C10,C15,C8).  ## Contribution to the requested topics  The authors position this study as an extension of Rego et al.'s preliminary separation-logic work: “We extend and refine their preliminary work to explore more LLMs, prompting approaches, and benchmarks.” They additionally claim function-level analysis, error severity coding and actionable insights as extensions. Their AutoVerus comparison explicitly distinguishes its multiple-agent refinement from this study's lack of LLM feedback loops. These are the authors' related-work comparisons (§VI,p11,Q14); the cited studies were not independently read as part of this record.  This directly informs evaluation of LLM-written specifications and proofs: report checker success alongside contract meaning, allowed source changes, proof assumptions, function-versus-program scope, dataset selection, repeated sampling and evidence provenance. Its error examples explain why heap ownership, unfolding and segment lemmas challenge automation even when the intended property is understood. The manually coded dataset and released outputs can support further work, subject to the recorded discrepancies and unread coding workbooks.  There is no distributed protocol theorem, Verus/Rust development or TLA+ model in this study. Concurrency is a C benchmark category, not a proof of a message-passing distributed service. Applying its lessons to Verus agents or distributed-system proof pipelines is a reader proposal: preserve the original code/contracts, audit trusted assumptions, distinguish auxiliary-proof completion from specification synthesis, and count the joint validated outcome rather than checker acceptance alone. The paper does not measure those proposed pipelines.
    - number: 303 C functions from 60 programs
    - number: overall verifier success 31.4%
    - number: Cohen kappa 0.73 and 0.38

- APE-Bench repository-scale proof engineering (2026)
    - title: APE-Bench: Evaluating Automated Proof Engineering for Formal Math Libraries
    - what: Defines Automated Proof Engineering (APE) for repository-scale Lean/Mathlib maintenance and releases APE-Bench plus APE-Harness. Contracts pin repository/toolchain, objectives, access boundaries and verification protocols. APE-Bench mines real Mathlib commits, synthesizes natural-language instructions and validates patches with dual checks: Lean compilation and LLM-judge semantic requirement/scope validation. APE-Harness provides isolated workspaces, version-scoped retrieval, execution services and interchangeable APE-Agent, Claude Code and Codex scaffolds. The benchmark contains 100 tasks from 67 Mathlib commits (2026-01-06–12), 10–284 changed lines, 17 mathematical domains, and is evaluated against miniCTX (334 tasks) and miniF2F (244). Under $3 and 100 turns per task, Gemini 3 Flash reaches 47% APE-Bench success, Gemini 3 Pro 24%, GPT-5.2 27%; miniCTX reaches 87.1%, 80.5%, 71.9%. Static gold-solution analysis finds 95/100 tasks require declarations from other files (median nine declarations across eight files). Difficulty declines with diff size and cross-file sources. Three judges achieve 90.6% overall semantic accuracy (92.2% correctness, 93.8% requirement alignment, 89.1% scope). APE-Agent outperforms Claude/Codex by 15–23 points; content-addressable storage cuts 480 GB to 71 GB and retrieval annotation cost $12.9K to $197. Limitations are Lean/Mathlib-only, single-file modifications and LLM-judge scope errors. Appendix A traces a sqrt continuity/filter task solved in 32 turns and $0.79, while Appendix B gives judges accepting unauthorized declaration deletions and rejecting benign refactoring; these cases explain why compilation and LLM consensus still need calibrated scope checks.
    - number: 100 tasks, 67 commits, 17 domains; 10–284 diff lines (median 30)
    - number: APE-Bench success: Flash 47%, GPT-5.2 27%, Pro 24% under $3
    - number: miniCTX: Flash 87.1%, Pro 80.5%, GPT-5.2 71.9%
    - number: 95/100 gold solutions use cross-file declarations
    - limits: Evaluation pins Lean/Mathlib versions and blocks target files while exposing read-only context; agents have no internet and only APE-Harness services. Semantic correctness depends on majority-voted LLM judges, despite expert validation. Tasks are single-file modifications mined from commits, so multi-file engineering and other proof ecosystems are not covered. Fixed $3/100-turn budgets confound reasoning capability with cost efficiency; the paper itself finds model ranking is budget-sensitive.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Automating Formal Verification with Agent-Guided Tree Search (2026)
    - what: # Automating Formal Verification with Agent-Guided Tree Search (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Leo Yao’s 78-page MIT MEng thesis measures Lean code/proof completion on 423 selected benchmark specifications. Its strongest result is a persistent single-agent baseline: “GPT-5.4 hits 95.0% on the 423-spec subset at K = 50 LLM calls” (§5, p63). The final context tree search reaches 88.2%, with some benefits at intermediate token budgets. Neither search design provides a formal correctness, completeness or optimality theorem. This is empirical verification automation, with Lean acceptance as the reported outcome.  All 78 pages, 94 references, 18 tables and eight figures were read. All page renders/contact sheets and 13 larger dense-page views were inspected. There is no appendix, checklist, numbered algorithm, numbered equation or formal theorem. Exact quotations with PDF and printed pages are in quotes.json; all table pages, including complete captions/cells, are retained in tables.json. The original pool and official arXiv PDFs are byte-identical. The abstract states, “Code available upon request by contacting the author at leoy@mit.edu.” (p3; Q1). No public thesis implementation or separate supplement was linked on the acquired arXiv page. The author was not contacted. No author code was run/imported, and no serialized data/model/checkpoint was loaded.  ## Tasks and what verifier success means  The population is deliberately selected: “We evaluate on three Lean subsets: bignum, verified_cogen, and verina, totaling 423 problems.” (§3.1.1, p25; Q2). BigNum has 62 arithmetic tasks originally authored in Dafny, then LLM-translated to Lean. VerifiedCogen has 172 Verus tasks for memory safety/functional correctness, also LLM-translated. Verina contributes 189 original Lean data-structure, algorithm and mathematical tasks. Six other Lean subsets are excluded because of autoformalization, informal/Python or GitHub-derived provenance. The thesis does not independently audit translation fidelity, task contracts or contamination.  The Vericoder returns textual replacements for implementation/proof holes. “Each new set of replacements is always inserted into the unmodified original spec rather than into the previous iteration’s output.” (§3.1.2, p26; Q3). Lean compiler success ends an attempt. The described rejection step disallows known generated proof bypass patterns, including sorry, admit, axiom, unsafe, Unchecked.cast and @[extern]. This is an explicit description of the Vericoder harness, not an independently checked claim that every later orchestrator preserves the contract or rejects every possible bypass. The implementation, accepted proofs and full run logs were unavailable.  Thus the measurements concern reported completion of these Lean targets under this harness. They do not independently establish correctness of the original Rust/Verus programs, distributed protocols or production software. The trusted boundary includes Lean/libraries, supplied contracts, the translation into Lean and the harness. The state orchestrator can change the base and its hole structure; original-contract preservation is an additional audit obligation (proof-audit.json).  ## Baselines, parsing and accounting  The targeted reproduction uses ten author-labelled models, five iterations uniformly, no retrieval/tools and a 16,384-output-token cap (§3.1.2–4, pp26–33). The earlier benchmark allowed ten BigNum attempts. Original reasoning settings were unspecified, and provider/quantization choices differ, so sibling-generation comparisons are qualified. Table3.1 reports GPT-5.4 at 24.6%, $23.57 theoretical list-rate cost and 5.2M tokens. The ten-model union is 32.2%.  Model output format confounds this baseline. “The harness first-match extractor consumes the first generated code block as final, discarding any subsequent refinements.” (§3.1.4, p32; Q7). Table3.4 records multiple blocks in 53.9% of Sonnet4.6 responses. Table3.3’s visible-deliberation proxy prorates character counts against reported output tokens; it is not measurement of inaccessible hidden reasoning. The author concludes that “cross-model comparisons are not strictly comparable due to differing model behavior and handling” (p32). Figure3.1 is a truncated illustrative excluded-model response, not an accepted proof.  The new agent bundles medium reasoning, a growing conversation, schema-validated submit_code, filtered compiler feedback and search_mathlib through Lean-LSP-MCP/LeanFinder/Loogle (§3.2.1–2, pp32–34; Q8–9). K counts LLM calls; one call may issue several searches. It is neither a submission count nor a tool-call count. At K10 GPT-5.4 reaches 60.3%; the same-six-model union rises from 31.7% Vericoder to 62.2%=263/423 agent (Table3.5). That is the matching union comparison; 32.2% comes from ten models. Initial agent runs have 1.8% output-truncation crashes, 74% of them Sonnet. The bundle does not identify which component causally supplies the gain.  Costs assume “optimal caching for all input blocks and free cache reads” (§3.2.3, p35; Q11). Unique tokens count each input/output block once; output includes reasoning. These are not actual provider bills or elapsed time. The author notes real cache-read/write charges and provider-routing effects (Q12), and that Vericoder prompt reordering could reduce unique input by around 50%. No wallclock or peakmemory measurement is reported.  At K50 the persistent GPT-5.4 agent reaches 95.0%, 52k average unique tokens per original spec (Table3.7, p42). The BigNum/VerifiedCogen/Verina rates are 87.1/96.5/96.3%. Solved tasks stop contributing later tokens. The BigNum cumulative counts are 9,12,15,20,31,42,50,52,53,54 at K5,10,…,50 (Table3.8). The 22 additional solves from K20 → 30 motivate the author’s possible anchoring explanation. This curve alone does not identify the cause. “Effectively saturated” should remain scoped to these 423 selected tasks, model and budget.  Table3.6 has an unresolved arithmetic issue. Its caption says Ratio is total search divided by total submit (p39; Q13). Printed GPT values 17.53/3.48 yield about 5.04, not the printed 5.29; Sonnet 12.24/3.28 yields 3.73, not 3.91. Rounding of the displayed inputs cannot account for those differences. The totals sum survival-conditioned per-turn means, rather than mean calls per original specification. Another aggregation/denominator may explain the ratios, but no raw logs resolve it. Retain the printed values and label the discrepancy as a reader diagnostic, not an established code defect.  ## What the search designs actually test  The state design asks an LLM parent to select high-level proof directions and subagents to expand them. “We aim to replace both the numerical evaluation signal and selection rule” (§4.1.1, p44; Q17). This is not an implemented MCTS value function. explore_variations dispatches up to five-turn subagents with advice from a five-class palette, whose exact classes are not fully enumerated. Subagents receive parent history/current partial proof, not sibling search histories. Failed runs return debriefs. update_base/undo_base change partial source and may add auxiliary holes (§4.1.2, p45; Q18–20). The 50-call budget includes both parent and children.  At K50, the initial state design reaches 67.8%/75k for GPT-5.4 and 49.6%/85k for mini (Table4.1). Fresh variations repeatedly search for lemmas; they average roughly 0.77–1.14 submissions in five turns (§4.1.4). resume_variations preserves selected contexts, but base updates invalidate old ones (Q22–24). The state+resume result is 66.4%/73k and 47.0%/87k (Table4.4), so adding resume does not improve the headline rates. Small-batch dynamic budgets, skeptical reviewers and Elo judges reportedly stay within “run-to-run variance” (§4.1.5, p51; Q25), but batch sizes, repeat counts and a numeric variance estimate are absent.  The context design preserves complete transcripts/endpoints and gives the initial subagent up to ten turns. The parent can dispatch a fresh branch, resume a prior exact context or abandon (§4.2.2, p55; Q27). Initial results are 81.8%/57k and 49.9%/88k (Table4.7). Initial round-2 dispatch/resume rates are 17.9% of 95 cases versus 38.7% of 62 cases (Table4.8). The author explicitly recognizes selection: “the parent is more likely to resume subagents closer to a proof” (§4.2.4, p58; Q28). This comparison is not a randomized effect of context reuse.  The final design lets dispatch branch from an existing non-root endpoint and also prompts the parent to prefer resuming (§4.2.4, p58; Q29). Both inheritance and advice change together. It reaches 88.2%/55k and 54.8%/102k at K50 (Table4.9), improvements of 6.4/4.9 percentage points over initial context. The agent remains ahead at 95.0%/52k and 57.0%/105k. For GPT-5.4, final context is 6.8 percentage points worse overall at K50, and 14.5 points worse on BigNum. It also uses more average unique tokens overall than the persistent agent. The broad claim that tree search is simply better or cheaper is unsupported.  There are limited budget regions where it helps. At BigNum K20, final context achieves 35.5%/72k, versus agent 32.3%/94k (Tables4.9/3.7). Figure4.4 shows a lead across part of the token range; the author places a crossover near 120k unique tokens/spec (§4.2.5, p59). That is an approximate curve interpretation, not a tabulated threshold. AtK50 the agent is 87.1% on BigNum against final context 72.6%. No released task-level intersection establishes exactly which distinct strategies or task categories explain the regional gain. The claim that parent interruptions inhibit difficult proofs is plausible author interpretation, not an isolated intervention.  Final round-2 dispatch/resume rates are 18.0% of 100 cases versus 48.6% of 70 cases (Table4.10). Dispatch search/submit ratio falls 8.1→3.8 across designs and average termination turns 7.8→5.7. These behavioral aggregates support the context-reuse motivation, with the same parent-selection and cross-design confounds.  ## Use for distributed verification and Verus work  This paper is useful for designing and evaluating proof agents: preserve useful verifier/retrieval history, compare branching against an adequately budgeted persistent agent, count parent and child work, and retain task contracts across partial-proof updates. Those are reader recommendations derived from the experiments. The direct evidence is Lean completion. VerifiedCogen’s Verus ancestry does not make the measurements a Verus run or a proof of original Rust semantics. There is no direct TLA+, consensus, temporal liveness, crash-recovery or distributed-protocol experiment.  For this repository, a useful follow-up would compare agent and tree methods on the same held-out protocol proof obligations, preserve the original source/specification, report accepted proofs and transitive trusted assumptions, and separate unique tokens from actual cost/time. That proposal is not a result of this thesis. Chapter 5 explicitly warns, “Contamination of the benchmark is also a possibility” (p64; Q32), and requests harder recent human-written tasks. Independent repeated runs, uncertainty, exact verifier/library/harness revisions and per-task outcomes would be needed for stronger comparative claims.  Whole78pages,94references,18tables,8figures; scopes selected423tasks, distinct model-union baselines, parser confounding, idealized cost, bundled agent changes, five orchestrator variants, late BigNum solves, selective round2cohorts and regional-vs-final tree-search outcomes. Preserves Table3.6ratio arithmetic ambiguity as reader diagnostic.
    - number: GPT-5.4 reaches 95.0% on 423 specs with K = 50 LLM calls
    - limits: No author implementation or accepted proofs/logs public link; no independent verifier execution. Lean translations of Verus/Dafny tasks do not establish original-program semantics. No repeated-run statistics, exact verifier/library/harness versions or actual cost/time. Context inheritance and prompts change together; dispatch/resume selected by parent. Contamination possible.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Beyond Theorem Proving: Formulation, Framework and Benchmark for Formal Problem-Solving (2026)
    - what: Introduces FPS, a Lean 4 metaprogramming DSL that synthesizes an answer term together with its correctness proof, and D-FPS, which synthesizes an answer proposition by forward implication and then verifies the reverse implication. Provides mathematical soundness/expressiveness arguments, 1,086 formal problems across three benchmarks, restricted-tactic answer comparison RPE, and evaluations of existing specialist provers and generalist LLMs. Crucially, D-FPS experiments run only forward reasoning plus RPE, not the framework’s complete backward soundness stage. FPS elaborates to a dependent function returning an answer/proof pair, with Answer and Proof goals; extract_answer/extract_proof recover terms and certificates. D-FPS changes the unknown to Prop and separates Ψ→A from A→Ψ: forward completeness captures all original solutions, backward soundness excludes spurious ones. Appendix E derives these conditional guarantees from completed proof terms and a correct canonical ground truth, rather than proving a new general-purpose solving algorithm. RPE accepts equality of generated/canonical answer objects only when a fixed tactic set proves it: rw_search, rfl, norm_num, aesop, ring_nf. Expert-in-loop benchmark construction checks contradictory hypotheses and known-answer provability, then performs dual expert review. Benchmarks and evaluations expose unsimplified or tautological answer terms that satisfy a specification while missing the desired answer representation.
    - limits: FPS soundness is relative to the formal specification, admitted axioms, Lean kernel and extraction/metaprogram implementation. The paper does not supply a machine-checked correctness proof of that implementation or a detailed adversarial proof-bypass audit. This reader did not execute it. Assumption 3.4 requires a solution whenever the hypotheses hold. D-FPS completeness/soundness additionally assumes the canonical ground truth is equivalent to the formal problem. The expressiveness argument for unique answers requires uniqueness; for solution sets it requires a globally complete characterization. None establishes fidelity to informal intent automatically. Only forward reasoning is run for D-FPS in Appendix I.1. Thus its experimental Typecheck% is forward-proof acceptance, which can be satisfied by True, rather than completion of both implications. RPE separately compares the resulting answer against the canonical one. Backward soundness follows after the backward proof is completed. Appendix E.4 also claims an exception for unique answers represented as a=â; this needs â to be independent of the original queried variable a, which the printed D-FPS dependency rules do not explicitly enforce. RPE is an operational criterion of bounded tactic provability, not complete semantic equality or a theorem that a representation is human-preferred. Mathematically equivalent but less simplified answers can be deliberately rejected. Success establishes formal equality under the chosen environment; failure does not establish mathematical inequality. No general theorem gives a universal human-alignment guarantee, transitivity, or freedom from canonical-answer shortcuts. The 300-example xVerify validation includes xFinder answer extraction and DeepSeek-V3 four-shot translation before symbolic checking. Thus the symbolic tactic phase is deterministic, but this evaluation is not an LLM-free semantic-fidelity test. Zero observed false positives is finite empirical evidence, not a universal bound. Benchmarks focus on canonical/closed-form answers: 387 FormalMath500, 375 MiniF2F-Solving, 324 PutnamBench-Solving. Geometry and Counting & Probability are excluded from FormalMath500; generic find-one witnesses and open conjectures remain future work. Existing public benchmark/model training exposure is not audited here. Experiments evaluate existing provers/prompts without task-specific training. Whole-proof, search, DSP+, FPS prompting and D-FPS prompting use different sample/search/token budgets, so cross-method rates are not equal-compute efficiency comparisons. Table 1 reports mean±std for selected methods but the supplied settings do not state the number of independent runs. Preference evidence uses two learned preference models on commonly solved problems with comments removed, not a direct blinded human study of full reasoning. Formal proof steps certify logical claims, not faithfulness of informal comments or the model’s hidden reasoning process. No distributed-system implementation, Verus/Rust proof, TLA+ model, concurrency/liveness task, or agent-generated systems specification is evaluated. Transfer to those topics is reader inference.
    - how much was read: most of it read

    - record id: `beyond-theorem-proving-formulation-2026`
- BFS-Prover-V2: Scaling up Multi-Turn Off-Policy RL and Multi-Agent Tree Search for LLM Step-Provers (2026)
    - what: A Lean4 step prover combines success-trajectory supervised expert iteration with tactic-surprisal filtering and periodic retraining from a base checkpoint. At inference a separate Gemini2.5Pro planner proposes intermediate lemmas; parallel best-first provers race on one active subgoal and reuse solved proofs. Authors report95.08% miniF2F-test with32B+planner and41.4% ProofNet-test with32B pure search, both accumulated across search settings. Describes inner-loop one-epoch updates and outer-loop corpus re-synthesis,more aggressive filtering and three-epoch base-model resets. Filters both highly predictable and highly surprising successful tactics rather than only selecting theorem-level difficulty. The planner produces have statements, augments context after verified subgoals and replans at bottlenecks. All provers focus on the same current lemma; first success populates a shared cache and cancels redundant searches, after which the group advances. Long examples compare concise tactics/library reuse with whole-proof scripts and exhibit dynamic decomposition of a finite trigonometric sum into a nonzero sinusoid.
    - limits: Formal acceptance concerns supplied Lean theorem statements under Lean4.10.0,Mathlib/imported axioms and verification infrastructure. Planner suggestions and autoformalizations are untrusted proposals; checking syntax with sorry does not establish semantic fidelity or prove the theorem. The paper does not supply an independent final-artifact trust/admission audit. Training uses roughly3million autoformalized statements from NuminaMath-CoT,NuminaMath1.5 and Goedel-PsetV1; GPT4o/Claude3.7Sonnet help formalize statements. Base models are Qwen2.5Math7B and Qwen2.5-32B with initialization from BFS-ProverV1. Distillation-free refers to the self-generated prover training loop, not absence of external statement formalizers or the Gemini planner. Only successful proof trajectories train the policy after filtering. Failed searches,compiler errors and negative tactics are not systematically incorporated. Off-policy RL here is an expert-iteration/search-and-supervised-learning framework; no value-network or policy-gradient objective is developed in this paper. Filtering retains central tactic-surprisal quantiles:approximately15% removed from each tail during ordinary iterations,20% each during resets. Curating a successful tactic out of training does not invalidate its source proof. Interpretations of low/high surprisal as trivial/noisy are empirical hypotheses, not a certified classification. Accumulated results are unions over expansion widths{2,3,4,8} and depth rewards{0,1,2},with8192 passes evaluated separately per configuration. A planner pass counts cumulative cooperating prover instances sharing cache; pure-search passes count independent searches. Each instance has600seconds including Lean interactions. These are different experiments from8192 independent whole-proof samples. The named planner is Gemini2.5Pro,additional model capacity beyond the reported7B/32B prover. Planner calls,cost,latency,total inference GPU hours and concurrency are not fully accounted for. Comparisons with much larger whole-proof models are not compute/model-system matched. Authors label ProofNet an out-of-distribution benchmark relative to Olympiad-style training, but do not give a corpus-level leakage/deduplication audit. Public benchmark adaptation and unknown model pretraining overlap limit generalization claims. Training reset decisions use new solved counts and downstream miniF2F accuracy;Figure4 explicitly plots miniF2F-test throughout training. Thus the reported test is adaptively monitored rather than clearly untouched by training-method selection. Training cost250–300 A100GPUhours per7B epoch,1500–1800 per32B epoch;reset uses3epochs. Cost prevents independent full-pipeline random-seed repetitions and comprehensive end-to-end ablations. Plateau recovery is primarily an observed trajectory,not a controlled causal experiment. The planner and prover are separately optimized. Parallelism races independent searches on a single active lemma; it does not concurrently expand one search tree or prove all dependent lemmas at once. There is no general search-complexity,completeness or convergence theorem.

- BrokenMath: A Benchmark for Sycophancy in Theorem Proving with LLMs (2026)
    - what: Introduces BrokenMath, a 504-item benchmark of plausible false mathematics statements for testing whether LLMs challenge a user’s incorrect theorem request. It combines 321 proof-style and 183 final-answer items from 2025 competitions, LLM-generated perturbations, expert review, and a four-way LLM-judge label (Ideal,Corrected,Detected,Sycophant). Across eleven models,GPT-5 has the lowest reported sycophancy at29.0%,but still attempts false proofs often. Proof-style tasks and unsolved originals increase sycophancy;prompting,best-of-n,self-verification,confidence heuristics and fine-tuning reduce but do not eliminate it. Perturbations are context-sensitive false answers,nonexistent counterexamples,inverted properties or semantic inversions rather than simple contradictory constraints. Original and corrupted theorem plus generated response are jointly given to a judge. Non-sycophantic labels distinguish detecting falsehood from recovering the original theorem. Utility uses parsed answers or OPC-R1-8B proof judging. GPT-5-mini majority judge gets95% granular/98% binary agreement on240 human labels;an alternate Grok judge gives nearly identical scores. Difficulty is measured by solving original problems with OPC-R1-8B;proof problems are subsampled per model to match final-answer accuracy. Self-sycophancy replaces a model’s generated theorem with a corrupted benchmark item in a three-turn dialogue. Agent tests use four-candidate tournament or up to four self-verification rounds. Qwen3-4B fine-tuning uses a13,000-sample filtered dataset from roughly15,000 source problems,including roughly8,000 sampled from a35,000-problem competition pool,90% sycophancy-focused,5% each valid final-answer/proof.
    - limits: The benchmark’s falsehood and original correctness rely on human-curated competition problems,LLM perturbations and expert annotation. A 100-item secondary review found97% agreement,but two near-trivial and one potentially true perturbation were identified;all models attempted the latter,so absolute scores and residual label noise remain possible. The main judge is an LLM. It is validated on240 responses but can confuse Corrected and Detected;five sycophant/non-sycophant errors remain. OPC-R1-8B judges proof utility and is explicitly “not infallible”;neither judge supplies formal proof certificates. The operational sycophancy label concerns attempting a false proof or missing a flaw,not direct measurement of internal belief. §4.4 finds97.7% Qwen3-4B responses follow a prompt to disprove the false statement;this measures disproof intent and instruction following,not formally checked correctness of the disproof. The authors infer sycophancy from the prompt contrast. Coverage is competition mathematics up to undergraduate level,not research mathematics,program verification or distributed systems. Agentic/self-sycophancy experiments use only selected models because of cost. Utility and sycophancy are measured on different outputs and judged with different procedures;reported Pearson correlation −0.65 is observational. Proof/final-answer comparison subsamples per model to approximate equal original accuracy,so denominators differ. The difficulty-control prose reports an average selected proof count as95%,an unclear/inconsistent unit;the record does not infer a corrected sample count. Prompt engineering,confidence and fine-tuning results use selected models and settings. Black-box confidence AUC reaches0.75 at best;white-box Qwen3-4B metrics AUC0.61–0.66. Fine-tuning two epochs on four H200s changes Qwen3-4B sycophancy55.6→51.0% and utility33.4→37.9%,not elimination. No external artifact or API output was rerun.

- Can Code Specify a System Precisely Enough to Formally Verify It? (2026)
    - what: Audits a deployed restaurant point-of-sale payment workflow (register, payment terminal, Finix backend). The authors built TLA+ models with Claude agents under their supervision. They also compared LLMs generating specifications from the TypeScript source in three forms: library-based JavaScript, a bare JavaScript next-function, and TLA+.
    - number: pre-fix models have 43-103 states, check in under a second, counterexamples 6-9 steps
    - number: 7 original gaps found, 3 reproduced on the unmodified code with its emulator
    - number: final failure-enabled model has 376 distinct states; 24-hunk patch; passes all 23 scenario tests
    - number: 105 generations (7 models x 3 contracts x 5), each replayed on 101 observed transition windows
    - limits: Every checked property is a safety invariant (a fact true in every reachable state) on finite, simplified models, not on all executions of the TypeScript. Timeout liveness is not checked. The model and the emulator shared a wrong reading of how Finix responds to a duplicate key. There is no mechanical proof that code matches model. Crashes and power loss remain accepted risks.

- Can LLMs Reason Like Automated Theorem Provers for Rust Verification? VCoT-Bench: Evaluating via Verification Chain of Thought (2026)
    - what: Introduces VCoT-Lift, which transforms low-level Z3 proof traces into explicit Verus-level proof steps, and VCoT-Bench, 1,988 completion tasks with semantic proof holes. It evaluates whether LLMs reconstruct missing invariant, assertion and lemma reasoning, rather than merely causing a verifier to pass. The four-stage pipeline uses a global proof transformer, rule-specific checker agents for high-level Z3 rules, a pruner for trivial/redundant steps, and a repair agent that compiles/checks transformed Verus proofs. A 36-rule Z3 hierarchy groups high/medium/low importance. Checker mappings must explicitly associate Z3 steps with Verus steps; a transformer-checker loop keeps global context while applying partial checks. VCoT-Bench derives 1,159 ratio tasks, 439 proof-type tasks and 390 location tasks from 150 verified Verus programs. Overall accuracy weights semantic and syntactic correctness (1, 0.5, 0.25, 0 for levels 3 through 0). GPT-5 mini is selected as semantic judge after 100-task, eight-judge validation.
    - number: 1,988 tasks: 1,159 ratio, 439 type, 390 location
    - number: 150 source programs; lifted corpus averages 6.5× proof lines, up to 32×
    - number: best greedy overall: Claude Sonnet 4.5 68.71% invariants, 39.55% assertions, 51.54% lemmas
    - number: at 100% block removal Claude Sonnet 4.5 17.22%, Qwen 3 think 0.66%
    - limits: The benchmark relies on Z3 proof traces, Verus syntax and a specialized GPT-5-mini semantic judge, so semantic accuracy is not itself a verifier theorem. The checker assesses only selected high-level rules; low-level steps are abstracted/pruned as trivial or redundant. Models are zero-shot and outputs are judged on completion tasks derived from existing verified programs, not independently generated distributed protocols. Context removal can overrepresent small one-block programs at 100%. The study reports current-model fragility and does not show that VCoT-Lift guarantees complete semantic equivalence for arbitrary proofs. Overall Acc gives partial credit (0.5 for semantically correct but syntactically invalid; 0.25 for syntactically valid but semantically incorrect); it is not the fraction of fully correct verified completions.
    - how much was read: most of it read
