- Can LLMs Write Correct TLA+ Specifications? Evaluating Natural-Language-to-TLA+ Generation (2026)
    - what: # Can LLMs Write Correct TLA+ Specifications? Evaluating Natural-Language-to-TLA+ Generation (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Bisharat, Ortiz, Spencer, Bhadauria, Wang, Thiruvathukal, Läufer and Abuhamad evaluate generation and completion of TLA+ specifications. The useful contribution is a public task, four prompt designs, checker outcomes, reference-text comparisons and a concrete error taxonomy. The experiment reports 2,600 open-weight runs and 130 proprietary-model runs. Its headline “semantic correctness” means an operational TLC outcome for a finite configuration. It does not establish correspondence to the natural-language requirements or equivalence to a reference behavior. Infrastructure failures, inconsistent counts and released evaluator differences materially limit the proposed ranking of strategies.  The saved arXiv version is 2606.05792v1, dated 4 June 2026. The publisher first page identifies ICSOFT 2026, pages 39–50, DOI 10.5220/0015070400004088 (B1). This record reads the entire 12-page arXiv paper, including all 34 references and 11 tables. All 12 pages were inspected as rendered images. There are no figures, numbered theorems, mathematical proofs, appendix, checklist or separate algorithm listing. The publisher PDF was acquired and its first-page bibliography inspected; no byte identity or complete final-version comparison is claimed.  The FormaLLM source is pinned to b159f5df093e7ed71f4793bba99459b97a2bb23d, dated 2 April 2026. Twenty-four selected plaintext files were read: documentation, split and prompt code, SANY/TLC evaluators and collectors, completion metrics, four pipeline files and three graph helpers. Identical files were checked by bytes and read once. The complete 1,864-entry tree was inventoried, not wholly read. Original/generated specifications, recorded checker outputs, full corpus/splits, run.sh, unselected analysis scripts and cited papers were not read. No author code was executed or imported; no models, checkpoints or serialized datasets were loaded. Source correspondence to the published historical runs remains unknown. quotes.json preserves 43 exact excerpts with source, line, character and section pointers; numeric_tables.json preserves all 11 literal table blocks and reader arithmetic.  ## Task, inputs and experiment  The corpus contains 205 specifications from 98 TLA+ Examples projects, including consensus protocols, concurrency examples and puzzles. Table 1 lists 92 with both comments and configuration, 14 with only configuration, 95 with only comments and four with neither; totals are 106 with configurations and 187 with comments. Thus “205 paired specifications” needs qualification. The paper reports a project-stratified 143/31/31 train/validation/test split, with five test exclusions leaving 26 evaluated specifications (Q1,T1). Training supplies examples; validation tunes prompts/hyperparameters. These are public repository comments rather than new, independently elicited requirements. No contamination or memorization analysis is supplied.  Twenty-five open-weight models use four strategies; five proprietary models use only few-shot prompting. This gives 25×26×4=2,600 plus 5×26=130 runs. Table 2 preserves model aliases, reported token counts and contexts. Its many `latest` aliases, missing Qwen3:4b context cell and unexplained extreme totals such as 1,141.7K for r1:1.5b limit exact model/budget reconstruction. These token summaries are not measured end-to-end time, price or energy. The “eight families” combine architecture families with overlapping categories such as code specialization and proprietary APIs; they are the authors' grouping, not eight independent experimental factors.  Few-shot (FS) supplies three training comment/specification examples and target comments. Progressive (Prog.) supplies three examples plus instructional messages about module structure, variables, operators and temporal properties. Fill-in-middle (FIM) supplies the first and last 30% of the reference and asks for the middle 40%. Half-completion (HC) supplies the first 50%, comments and configuration and asks for the remainder (Q2–Q4). Completion therefore receives substantial correct reference code and tests a different task from full generation. In selected source, full-generation prompts also include the original configuration, while the written generated configuration becomes the preferred checker input (C10–C11).  The paper says SANY passes on “Parsing completed” with a 30-second timeout, then sends passing specifications with configurations to TLC (Q5). Textual measures apply to the generated reference segment: BLEU, ROUGE-L, edit distance, exact match and line accuracy. Source C13 defines line accuracy by matching line subsequences divided by reference line count, character similarity by SequenceMatcher, character edit distance, whitespace-token BLEU and default ROUGE tokenization. These are textual measures; different but equivalent specifications can score poorly, and reference overlap does not establish correct behavior.  ## What the tables report  Table 3 reports SANY FS173/650, HC80/650, Prog162/650 and FIM92/650. TLC is FS0, HC0, Prog56 and FIM0, with the Prog rate 8.6%. These are printed experimental outcomes, not independently rerun results. Table 5 distributes those outcomes across models, and Table 10 across specifications. The strongest listed individual open-model TLC result is DeepSeek r1:8b with14/26=53.8% under Prog; Qwen235b has12, Qwen30b10, GPT-OSS20b6 and Phi-mini4. These individual rates differ from the aggregate 8.6% headline.  Table 6 is essential to the headline's scope: under FS, GPT-5 reports SANY26/26 and TLC7/26; Sonnet4.5 reports24/26 and3/26, Haiku4.5 reports21/26 and3/26, Opus4.1 reports23/26 and1/26, GPT-4o reports20/26 and1/26. Thus “only progressive” applies to the open-weight multi-strategy experiment; it is false as an unqualified statement about all30 models. Likewise 26.6% is an aggregate open-strategy syntax rate, not the maximum individual result. The sentence calling GPT-5's26.9% TLC “comparable to the open-weight FS ceiling” conflicts with the reported open FS0% (Q7,T3,T6).  Tables 7 and9 preserve all per-model completion similarities. Table 3 reports mean BLEU HC.062/FIM.077, ROUGE-L .189/.213, line accuracy10.90%/12.27%, and exact matches0/650 versus1/650. Table 7 instead prints FIM average12.37%; reported dispersions also differ across summary and detailed tables. The one FIM exact match together with zero TLC passes is another reason to inspect infrastructure/configuration before interpreting zero as behavioral failure.  Table 8 reports bad-module-body first errors FS228,HC264,Prog243,FIM290; other parse errors72/227/70/191; unexpected-token errors82/29/65/17. These are not all categories. Its first-error location counts sum to477/570/488/558, matching 650 minus the Table 3 SANY totals. A first detected error does not count all defects. Early/middle/late thirds are the paper's definition; the selected collector instead labels absolute line numbers below10 and below50 (C14), so the published calculation needs a different, identified analysis path.  Table 10 gives examples rather than a simple “distributed is harder” result. YoYoNoPruning has100% SANY for HC/FIM; HDiskSynod has FS32%, Prog20% and FIM48%, contradicting the prose example that it scores0%. BinarySearch has12%FS/24%Prog syntax and8%Prog TLC. The highest listed per-spec Prog TLC rate is16%, shared by EnvironmentController, MultiPaxos, VoucherCancel and YoYoNoPruning. MCDieHarder has zero TLC across strategies. Multiple spelling/wrapping forms are retained in literal rows; no semantic equivalence across similarly named modules is assumed (Q10,T10).  ## Infrastructure confounding and inconsistent totals  Table 3's footnote explicitly says zero TLC rates “reflect failures (i.e., State Directory Conflicts and Module Not Found errors).” Table 4 labels97.2%/99.2%/99.2% of FS/HC/FIM failures as infrastructure and9.8% for Prog. A pipeline unable to load a module has not tested its behavior. Calling invalid configuration a “genuine semantic” failure also does not establish a mismatch between the generated transition system and intended requirements. These failures can be important practical generation defects while remaining distinct from behavioral incorrectness (Q6,T3–T4).  Table 4 is internally incomplete or inconsistent. Its displayed module/configuration rows sum FS177,HC164,FIM161 against totals650 each; an unlisted state-directory category might explain some gaps but is not numerically supplied. For Prog it gives122 TLC failures,455 parse failures and56 passes, totaling633 rather than650. Table 3's162 SANY passes imply488 parse failures. Moreover122+56=178 exceeds162. The record retains these printed claims and cannot reconstruct a valid common denominator from them.  Reader arithmetic over Table 5 gives column totals FS173/0,HC78/0,Prog162/56,FIM92/0. Table 10 implies HC80, matching Table 3 but conflicting with Table 5. Table 5's averages6.92,3.17,6.38,2.08,3.71 differ from row-derived6.92,3.12,6.48,2.24,3.68 for the corresponding nonzero columns. Also80/650=12.31%, whereas Table 3 prints12.2%; Qwen30b's23/26 is88.46%, whereas its best-model cell prints92.3%. No source output logs were inspected to settle which transcription or analysis is wrong.  ## Claims about models, causes and error repair  The observed results support some nonmonotonic size comparisons. They do not support “r1:8b outperforms70b across all strategies”: HC SANY is1 versus3 and FIM3 versus3. The claim that LLaMA1b's12 Prog SANY passes are highest among all LLaMAs conflicts with both3.3 variants'18. “Just three model families” conflicts with the paragraph listing DeepSeek,Qwen,GPT-OSS,Phi and Table 5's Gemma pass. Model training, instruction alignment, context limits, prompt interaction and output processing are not separated by a controlled factorial study (Q6,Q8–Q9,T5).  The authors attribute poor code-model performance to negative transfer, operator substitutions to rendered mathematics and reasoning leakage to training. These are proposed explanations. They did not manipulate training data, measure causal mediation, run controlled fine-tuning ablations or establish statistical significance. “Not noise” is especially strong given their explicit limitation: “We run each model-specification pair once per strategy” (Q14). More heterogeneous tasks do not substitute for repeated measurements of a given model/task condition.  Table 11 and §4.4 give five categories: Unicode operator substitutions, syntax from other languages, reasoning/formatting leakage, length miscalibration and module structure. Semicolon first-error counts are18/11/25/10, while file-presence scans count49/23/82/22; these have different denominators and meanings. Backtick counts are28/10/21/13; think-block counts15/30/not-applicable/28. The paper says QwQ contributes26/26 HC and FIM think blocks. It reports mean output/target length HC3.46× and FIM2.24×, with9.84× as an extreme. These are observed author counts, not a controlled explanation of accuracy (Q11–Q13,T11).  The claimed243+ missing Prog terminators numerically matches Table 8's243 bad-module-body first errors, but that broad parser category is not equivalent to an independently counted missing terminator. The selected generator also appends a terminator. The historical counting path is unresolved. A semicolon-presence scan is not itself a syntax-error test: semicolons can occur within comments, strings or PlusCal text. Similarly a blanket claim about all Unicode needs a tool-version/context boundary. Suggested grammar constraints, retrieval, normalization, fence removal and checker-guided repair are future interventions here. Their correctness and benefit were not measured; indiscriminate character deletion could change intended content (Q13,Q15,C16).  ## Released source qualifications  The primary parse step checks return code0 plus a success substring (C1–C2). Its separate metrics collector computes `parse_success` from that substring and error keywords but never uses it to return status: it returns PASSED whenever its line-number regexes find no errors (C3–C4). By static inspection, a present empty/unrecognized-error log can therefore receive PASSED in that collector. This is a source-level possibility; it is not a measured false-positive rate in the paper.  The selected TLC loops iterate every parsed dictionary entry and ignore `parse_status`, contrary to the paper's stated filtering. They prefer any generated configuration file, even an empty one, before fallback to the corpus; full generation writes one. Hence the checker may assess model-selected properties/configuration rather than an independently fixed behavioral oracle. The success collector accepts “Model checking completed. No error has been found.” before checking exit status. The alternate progressive evaluator instead accepts “The specification is correct”; the two strings are different. TLC subprocess calls have no Python timeout, despite TIMEOUT classification later; clearing `generated/states` without setting a working directory/metadir does not establish isolation of TLC's actual state directory. These observations require run-path/log validation before assigning them to historical results (C5–C7,C11).  The split helper performs unseeded flat `random.shuffle`, not project stratification, and floor slicing205 gives143/30/32 rather than143/31/31. Selected prompts read val.json rather than test.json. Those labels may have been repurposed by uninspected run tooling; this record cannot certify the published test set from the selected code (C8,C15).  The `fifty-fifty` and `fill-in-middle` prompt files are byte-identical and implement30/40/30 completion; their metric files are also identical. This does not reproduce two distinct completion designs. The progressive prompt accumulates instructional messages and makes one `llm.invoke` per target; it does not execute separate checked module/variable/operator generation stages. Its cleaner removes fences and explanatory text, adjusts headers and appends `====`, as do other selected generators. Thus Table 11's FIM cleanup footnote does not establish cleanup unique to FIM. The source/documentation mix should not be silently treated as the exact experimental pipeline (C9,C11–C12,C16).  ## Contribution to this literature effort  The paper is directly relevant to evaluating LLM-written distributed-system specifications. It supplies task definitions, public examples, prompt contrasts and failure cases. Its strongest lesson is methodological: separate parser success, tool execution, checked finite properties and faithfulness to intended behavior. The evidence does not justify treating an8.6% pipeline pass fraction as a measured semantic-equivalence rate, or infrastructure-confounded zero passes as proof that a strategy cannot generate valid behavior.  A reader recommendation is to use fixed independent configurations/properties, inspect explored states and vacuity, compare behaviors or traces against requirements, isolate checker paths, publish per-run logs and exact model versions, repeat runs, and report infrastructure failures separately. The recommendation extends beyond the paper's measured interventions. This study verifies no Verus/Rust implementation and proves no distributed protocol. It evaluates draft TLA+ artifacts rather than an autonomous multi-agent proof process.  Prose consultation is complete; independent review is pending. REVIEW.md records consultation scope and disposition. This is a whole-paper reading with selected static source inspection, not an experimental reproduction.  Fresh independent review found no material conclusion issue; two table-packaging defects were corrected (REVIEW.md).  All11tables/34refs,43literalquotes,16diagnostics;operational-vs-behavioral correctness,infra confounding,count arithmetic,proprietaryresults,source evaluator/split/prompt discrepancies.

- CIll (2026)
    - title: CIll: CTI-Guided Invariant Generation via LLMs for Model Checking
    - what: Integrates an LLM with rIC3 to generate helper assertions from counterexample traces and iteratively strengthen RTL proofs. CIll feeds source SystemVerilog plus CTI traces to an LLM, checks correctness and k-inductiveness, and injects accepted helpers before re-synthesizing BTOR. It targets high-level RTL of nerv, picorv32 and serv rather than low-level HWMCC netlists; invariant migration reuses assertions across related instructions.

- CircuitProver (2026)
    - title: CircuitProver: Agentic Lean 4 Theorem Proving with Reusable Circuit Proof Library for Hardware Verification
    - what: Introduces CircuitProver, a Lean 4 framework that translates parameterized Chisel hardware and natural-language specifications into Lean, uses an agent to iteratively construct machine-checked proofs, and distills proof trajectories into reusable guidance and theorem libraries. The deterministic Chisel-to-Lean translator preserves parameters, interfaces, registers and width-aware bitvector transitions; small-width co-simulation checks the translation. Agents formulate Lean specifications, build hardware-aware scaffolds, prove one-step/invariant lemmas, and repair scripts using Lean feedback. Two libraries separate reusable proof plans from module-independent checked theorems. The carry-skip-adder case extracts a block recurrence, full-width radix closure and targeted representation repair. The 63-task suite spans arithmetic/control/memory/miscellaneous designs plus XiangShan/Rocket arithmetic modules, with ablations isolating guidance and theorem-library effects and model-scaling comparisons.
    - number: 63 benchmark tasks: 34 arithmetic, 9 control, 9 memory, 11 miscellaneous

    - number: 50.0% fewer proof rounds
    - number: 23.2% lower verification time
    - limits: The frontend supports only a subset of Chisel and generated Lean models are validated by exhaustive small-bit-width co-simulation, not all parameter widths. Success requires a final Lean file with no sorry/admit/axiom/unsafe. Cost averages are over solved tasks, so comparisons have different coverage. Results use Claude agents and a benchmark authored by the researchers; industrial RTL, richer properties and broader designs remain future work. Natural-language specification formalization still requires semantic agent reasoning.

- CoCo-Prover (2026)
    - title: Cost-Efficient Theorem Proving via Agent Orchestration in Program Verification
    - what: Introduces CoCo-Prover, a cost-aware Lean 4 program prover that treats proving as metalevel decision-making over a two-level proof graph: intra-declaration AND/OR proof hypergraphs and an inter-lemma dependency DAG. A symbolic scheduler exposes dependency-ready goals, while an evidence-grounded router purchases actions from deterministic automation, Proof Writer, Lemma Planner, and Recovery Reviewer agents. The formal model defines state, frontier scheduling, action outcomes/costs, a Bellman objective 1[solve]-lambda C, and regret decomposition. The implementation clusters goals, bypasses model calls for definition-nested goals, tracks provisional lemmas and rollback, rejects cyclic/unelaborated helpers, and uses Lean as proof authority. The evaluation fixes theorem statements and implementations, compares against Ax-Prover-Base, Humanize, and coding agents on CLEVER, VERINA, AlgoVeri, NTP4VC, and Vero with GPT-5.6-Terra, Gemini-3.5-Flash, and DeepSeek-V4-Flash. CoCo-Prover reaches 100% on CLEVER/VERINA in several settings, up to 60.5% on Vero versus at most 7% coding-agent, and lowers cost up to 30.9% against Humanize on commonly solved tasks. Ablations show removing routing raises cost 39.9% and removing lemma planning 41.6%; case studies show shared lemmas solve 56 interdependent Vero obligations. Independent review checks no holes, unchanged statements, no new axioms, automated review, and manual audits.
    - number: 5 Lean benchmarks
    - number: 3 backbone LLMs
    - number: up to 100% function-level solve rate
    - number: Vero 60.5% versus coding-agent 7.0% under Gemini
    - limits: Theory assumes finite reachable abstractions, finite admissible computations/text vocabularies, Markov specialist outputs, bounded positive paid costs, deterministic verifier transitions, proper policies, and an explicit stop. The deployed router is not proved low-regret; semantic decisions rely on LLM judgment. Evaluation is Lean 4 only, proof-only with fixed implementations/specifications, and repository-scale evidence uses two benchmarks. Local computation, Lean checking, retrieval, and wall-clock costs are not charged; CoCo-Prover can be slower in wall-clock time. Shared-helper savings assume equal success probability and can be lost to false or abandoned lemmas.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- COINS specification evaluation (2026)
    - title: How Powerful Are LLMs in Generating Formal Program Specifications?
    - what: Introduces COINS (COq-based INstantiated Specification evaluation), which judges a generated input/output predicate by Rocq proofs on positive and mutated negative test cases. It separates specification quality from implementation conformance and makes successful proofs sound evidence while treating failed proofs as ambiguous. Sections 2–3 define implementation-agnostic pre/post specifications, the four-stage SYNTAX, PASSfirst, PASSall and REJECTall pipeline, and the sort-third example. Appendix A gives AST and LLM mutation operators, input perturbation, one-second execution limits and 1,640 generated negatives. Appendix B models an LLM prover probabilistically and proves that failed verification cannot distinguish a false specification from a hard true one. The main experiments compare six API models, study Fixpoint versus Inductive styles, equivalence to human specifications, input-type difficulty, Rocq compiler errors, and proof failures such as phantom goals, library predicates and normalization resource exhaustion. HumanEval/12 exposes a compiling but contradictory longest-string specification on duplicate maxima.

    - number: >100,000 proof attempts

The 164 human references were manually reviewed but are not absolute ground truth. Positive evidence is sound only because Rocq checks proofs; rejection of negatives is incomplete because an LLM may fail to find an available proof. Candidates can still miss behaviors outside the test suite. Scope is 164 HumanEval tasks, Python descriptions/implementations and Rocq; scaling requires substantial annotation and compute (over 100,000 proof attempts).

- Compile to Compress: Boosting Formal Theorem Provers by Compiler Outputs (2026)
    - what: Introduces a learning-to-refine framework for Lean theorem proving that treats compiler diagnostics as a lossy many-to-one compression of failed programs into recurring error modes. It trains provers on direct solutions plus compiler-conditioned repair traces, uses expert iteration to collect on-policy failures, and performs random or value-guided tree search that alternates fresh proof generation with local refinement. On MiniF2F-test, ProofNet, MathOlympiadBench and PutnamBench, Kimina-Expert and Goedel-Expert generally improve over direct baselines; Goedel-Expert value search reaches 110 solved PutnamBench problems at budget 256 and Kimina-Expert reaches 25. Defines programs C, compiler messages M, verifier Φ:C→M, equivalence classes Ψ(c) of programs sharing feedback, and a refinement supervision operator that maps correct programs to synthesis targets and failures to repaired programs. The chain-of-distributions view models each next program as conditioned on current program, compiler message and problem. Markovian refinement discards long interaction histories while retaining the failed code and diagnostics. Cold-start data pairs base-prover failures with base-prover correct solutions and Claude-3.7-Sonnet-generated explanatory traces; direct examples are subsampled to balance refinement data. Expert iteration repeatedly refines on-policy failures and trains a value head from pairwise preferences along successful and failed trajectories. Compiler messages are inserted at reported source lines using <error> delimiters; insertion beats appending in a one-direct-plus-one-refinement MiniF2F comparison (279 versus272 solved after one epoch,and284 versus246 after two epochs).
    - limits: The compiler projection is explicitly lossy: the paper says messages ‘do not capture full semantic context’; shared error signatures are an inductive bias, not a proof that all programs in a class share a repair. The Markov treatment assumes each refinement step depends only on the current failed program, its compiler feedback and the problem, with history ‘implicitly encoded in Lean semantics’. This is a modeling choice and is not established as a theorem. The chain-of-distributions claim that refinement can reach solutions outside direct-generation support is supported by edit-distance energy tests and MDS visualizations. Edit distance is syntactic, and MDS is a low-dimensional visualization; these do not establish semantic support escape. The authors call the synthesized pipeline ‘hallucination-free’ because corrected solutions are validated by Lean and come from base-prover failures; Claude supplies explanatory traces, not executable repairs. This does not make the natural-language reasoning truthful or independently verified. Main evaluation uses Lean v4.15.0, a pinned Mathlib snapshot, Kimina-Prover-Distill-8B and Goedel-Prover-V2-32B, and four mathematics benchmarks. It does not evaluate Verus, Rust, TLA+, concurrency or distributed systems. Reported Pass/solved counts use different sampling budgets and methods. PutnamBench is described as 660 problems, while the leaderboard includes an Aleph result of 668; this apparent version/scope mismatch is preserved rather than reconciled. Goedel-V2 already supports non-Markovian refinement, so the main experiments use only its direct generation ability as a base; direct-vs-refinement comparisons therefore are not a uniform comparison of all native capabilities. Error-type correction probabilities aggregate occurrences and treat multiple errors independently; they do not measure transition-node success. Training frequency alone does not imply repairability. The Claude ablation and Table 16 use fixed budgets and selected datasets. Expert iteration, not cold-start SFT or Claude traces alone, accounts for most reported gains, but this is an ablation interpretation rather than a causal decomposition over all possible training procedures. No external artifact or proof checker was rerun here; quoted correctness of the worked repair is the paper's report that the generated Lean program compiled.

- Decompose, Structure, and Repair: A Neuro-Symbolic Framework for Autoformalization via Operator Trees (2026)
    - what: Presents DSR, a statement-autoformalization pipeline: Gemini 3.0-Pro decomposes informal mathematics into conditions and conclusion, a Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct formalizer produces Lean components and operator trees, and Qwen3-Max repairs compiler-localized subtrees before a mandatory whole-statement semantic review. Introduces PRIME, 156 manually formalized textbook statements with informal proofs. Reports highest LeanScorer consistency scores among main Table 2 methods (84.00/79.51/67.95% on ProverBench/ProofNet/PRIME), while syntax scores and supplementary FATE results are more mixed. This evaluates statements ending in admitted proofs, not completed verified theorem proofs. Operator trees are labeled ordered scope trees derived with ASSESS/Lean Language Server, adapted to component granularity with parentheses retained. Training constructs 283,958 NL-component/Lean-component/tree triples from 120,627 NuminaMath-LEAN+ATLAS-Synthetic statements, using Qwen3-Max backtranslation. It filters extreme tree complexity, retains 281,209 examples in simple/moderate/complex tiers, and applies four one-epoch LoRA phases with replay. Assembly prioritizes tree reconstruction and falls back to linear components for malformed trees. Repair maps compiler row/column to a tree node, expands bottom-up from parent subtrees through component and full statement, with one pass per repair attempt. Local prompts omit informal text; component prompts see the informal component; the final whole-statement prompt restores full informal context and checks semantics even when compilation succeeds.

Outputs intentionally end in by sorry; passing SC establishes a well-formed admitted statement, not its truth, consistency of premises, semantic correspondence or a proved theorem. Consistency Check (CC) is LeanScorer using DeepSeek-V3.2 at threshold 0.6, not a kernel certificate of NL/Lean equivalence. The framework uses larger external models: Gemini 3.0-Pro for decomposition, Qwen3-Max for backtranslation and repair, and DeepSeek-V3.2 for evaluation. Calling the system 7B describes only its trained formalizer. No all-components fixed-parameter comparison follows from the smaller formalizer. Decomposition may infer missing domains or assumptions incorrectly; the source explicitly acknowledges error propagation. A 90-item manual sample reports 95.56% decomposition accuracy for Gemini versus 87.78% Qwen3-Max, not universal correctness. The final semantic check is another LLM repair call and supplies no formal semantic guarantee. PRIME covers 156 textbook theorems, not distributed-system specifications. Expert formal statements and informal proofs are supplied, but no full formal proofs or independent statement-quality audit is reported here. Textbook/domain selection and judge limitations constrain generalization. Appendix B.1 says PRIME Lean references were private/manual and shares only 4 conceptual overlaps with FATE-M. Calls differ across decomposition, component generation and local/global repair; exact component batching/counting and strict per-problem truncation are insufficiently specified. §4.2 says four total calls, but §4.4.2 says four refinements following initial generation. - Djinnlang (2026)
    - title: Djinnlang: Higher-Level Programming by Unambiguous Specification with an LLM in the Compiler
    - what: Proposes Djinnlang, a specification-only language whose translator emits Dafny relations and proof/implementation obligations, then uses an LLM to fill implementations and proofs checked by Dafny. Its central unambiguity constraint requires the specification relation to be deterministic, eliminating semantic freedom for the generated implementation; procedures add ordered effects through generated step machines. The paper formalizes unambiguity as pre(x) ∧ q(x,o1) ∧ q(x,o2) ⇒ o1=o2 plus an existence obligation, and distinguishes deterministic funcs, nondeterministic rels, and effectful procs. The translator pins regenerated declarations, scans solutions for Dafny escape hatches, and re-translates the source before verification. Examples show sorting via a permutation relation, string parsing as a preimage, Sudoku and optimal LZ77 specifications, and a step-function compilation of procedures. Bootstrapping djinnc itself required 2,106 obligations and found four genuine specification bugs before synthesis succeeded. Two LLM implementers discharged all benchmark obligations, but generated performance differed by hundreds-fold.
    - number: three benchmark programs; 256 obligations
    - number: djinnc: 4,088 Djinnlang lines, 487 declarations, 2,106 obligations
    - number: Codex 33 minutes/$47 versus Claude 21 hours/$1,026
    - number: Sudoku optimized 0.13s Claude versus 47.84s Codex

Compilation may time out without distinguishing unsatisfiable specs from hard proofs or weak models; concurrency, interleaving, timing side channels, and performance bounds are outside the current language. The specification is still the main source of intent errors. Benchmarks use Dafny and LLM-generated specs that authors checked manually; results do not establish general LLM reliability.

- Editable Proof Sketch for Automated Theorem Proving (2026)
    - what: Introduces EditableSketch, a dependency-explicit editable proof representation, and SketchRefine, a Lean 4 ATP agent alternating verified subgoal proofs with local repair/decomposition while retaining unaffected proofs. Evaluates MiniF2F-test (244 statements) and FormalMath-Lite (425). Reported 99.6% and 76.0% solved rates count either proofs or certified refutations, not only proofs of the original theorem. Table 2 FormalMath-Lite proof-only rate is 69.2%. Four node types: Deduction produces a term, Construction produces witnesses satisfying a predicate, Assumption opens a local scope, Conclusion discharges it. Distinguishes syntactic dependencies from proof premises; compiler adds indirect syntactic dependencies. Scopes must nest without crossing. DSL supports NEW/UPDATE/DELETE and ADJUST of logical premises. Compilation produces per-node subtheorems and a Lean fragment composing them. Lean checks structure/types with temporary sorry; all subproofs must subsequently verify and final script is checked. Direct prover first samples eight proofs; unsolved subgoals get eight attempts. LLM plausibility judgment directs error repair versus decomposition; modified subgoals invalidate cached proofs. LeanExplore supplies up to three candidates for each of five queries, then selects up to five lemmas and inherits parent selections. Refutation strategy restarts with original theorem assumed and False as goal; can switch back when that attempt appears invalid.

Correctness of completed certificates depends on Lean and the formalized assumptions; there is no separate mechanized correctness theorem for the EditableSketch compiler in the supplied paper. The solved metric combines theorem proofs with certified refutations. FormalMath-Lite 76.0% with refutation versus 69.2% without; MiniF2F Table 1 gives DeepSeek 97.5% versus 96.7% and Gemini 99.6% versus 98.8%. These are different outcomes and cannot all be described as proofs of the original statement. MiniF2F uses the Goedel-Prover-V2 repository version; two statements are false under those formalizations. Hilbert uses slightly different corrected statements, so headline comparison is not identical-input proof generation. The 99.6% Gemini result includes upgrading from Gemini 2.5 Pro (99.2% in prose) to Gemini 3.0 Pro Preview on imo_2001_p6. Output budget is 10M general-purpose-model tokens per MiniF2F problem and 2M DeepSeek-V3.2 tokens per FormalMath-Lite problem. Table 3 averages condition on problems not solved in pre-proving, rather than all problems. Baseline pass@ and models/budgets differ; many baseline figures are imported from other papers. Figure 4 reports a matched general-purpose-output-token Hilbert reproduction, but that is not a full-system compute comparison. No repeated-run confidence intervals or significance tests are supplied. LLM correctness/plausibility and dependency annotations are heuristic search guidance, not sound semantic oracles; acceptance requires formal verification. Authors identify compiler-based extraction of syntactic dependencies as future work. - ExVerus (2026)
    - title: EXVERUS: Verus Proof Repair via Counterexample Reasoning
    - what: An LLM proof-repair pipeline for Verus synthesizes source-level Z3Py queries, generates concrete candidate counterexamples, diagnoses wrong versus weak annotations, proposes mutations and chooses repairs with verifier feedback. Final proofs are checked by Verus. Counterexample validation is implemented for loop-invariant failures only; other examples are heuristic reasoning aids. Avoids decoding lowered solver models by asking the LLM to encode the failing source obligation directly, with finite vector concretization, namespaced element variables and model blocking. The validation module extracts one loop body, translates invariants into start/end assertions and injects candidate assignments; expected assertion-error patterns distinguish establishment from preservation failures. Error triage chooses replace/relax versus strengthen, five candidate mutations are ranked by blocked validated examples for invariant errors or verified subgoals otherwise. Appendices supply all prompts, construction/filtering policies, toolchain sensitivity and failure cases. A separate qualitative Copilot/Sonnet temporal proof rerun illustrates lifting state invariants to execution-trace entailment in an Anvil Kubernetes controller.

Printed prompts forbid changing executable code and requires/ensures. No independent artifact replay was performed. LLM-generated queries can simplify or mistranslate obligations and concretize arrays. Finding no candidate proves neither absence of violating states nor correctness. Validation for non-invariant errors is explicitly future work; the loop module classifies verifier error symptoms rather than supplying a general independently checked concrete execution witness. An inductiveness counterexample may satisfy current invariants but be unreachable from program initialization. LLM reachability triage is heuristic, not a reachability proof. Blocking finitely many states is a search score, not proof that the repaired annotation is inductive; final verification is essential. Results use Verus0.2025.07.12.0b6f3cb/Z3Py4.15.1.0, temperature1.0, at most10 repair iterations and5 mutation responses. Its one-repair experiment measures near-correct invariant repair, not arbitrary proof synthesis. - FLV formal logic verification training (2026)
    - title: Pushing the Boundaries of Natural Reasoning: Interleaved Bonus from Formal-Logic Verification in Language Models
    - what: Introduces FLV, which interleaves formal symbolic verification with natural-language reasoning and trains Qwen2.5-7B/14B in two stages: execution-validated formal-logic supervised fine-tuning, then GRPO policy optimization with hierarchical rewards for format, correctness and verifier feedback. Formal modules return satisfiability, counterexamples or execution outputs during generation; a flexible mode allows direct numerical calculation with formal checks used as validation. The paper measures a logical-consistency gap before training: 39.3% of steps in chains with correct answers are formally disproved, rising to 52.4% for incorrect answers. Data synthesis uses DeepSeek-R1 reasoning, GPT-4o answer judging and Claude Sonnet formal translation; exact-match and semantic-equivalence checks retain executable traces. Training uses about 17k samples, 16 NVIDIA H800 GPUs, 120 RL steps and at most four verification rounds. Across KOR-Bench, BBH, MATH-500, AIME24, GPQA-Diamond and TheoremQA, FLV-RL averages 51.9 (7B) and 58.6 (14B), versus Natural-SFT 36.5 and FLV-RL-14B reaches 30.2% on AIME24 and 63.5% on TheoremQA. Ablations show FLV-SFT 49.8 versus Natural-SFT 36.5; symbolic/logic tools are 62.5% of FLV-RL calls. Verification roughly doubles RL training time and formalization errors limit open-ended generalization. A detailed MATH-500 appendix case is a caution: the smallest cube that is a sum of three consecutive integers is 27 (=8+9+10), but a flawed generated proof and Z3 script claim 81; the model rationalizes a DISPROVED result. This motivates the flexible mode that separates arithmetic calculation from logical validation.
    - number: 39.3% formally disproved steps in correct-answer chains; 52.4% in incorrect-answer chains
    - number: FLV-RL averages 51.9 (7B) and 58.6 (14B)
    - number: FLV-SFT 49.8 versus Natural-SFT 36.5 average
    - number: AIME24 30.2% and TheoremQA 63.5% for FLV-RL-14B
    - limits: Formal translation must preserve the natural-language reasoning semantics; ambiguous or commonsense-heavy descriptions can generate incorrect feedback. Benchmarks are six curated reasoning datasets and use OpenCompass (greedy decoding except AIME avg@16), so gains do not prove deployment reliability. Real-time verification adds about 2x RL cost and is capped at four rounds. The enforced format initially suppressed direct arithmetic, motivating flexible post-hoc validation. The appendix exposes a correctness threat in generated verifier scripts: a solver call can be mis-specified and its failure rationalized, so executing a formal tool is not itself a guarantee unless the encoding is validated.

- FormalJudge: A Neuro-Symbolic Paradigm for Agentic Oversight (2026)
    - what: Post-hoc agent-trajectory oversight separates intent decomposition,atomic-fact extraction,formal predicate generation and Dafny/Z3 checking. Structured logs are parsed directly where possible;LLMs extract semantic facts and synthesize the constraints. Authors report improved agreement with an LLM-ensemble reference,including deception classification and feedback-based behavioral refinement. Formal guarantees are conditional on faithful facts and specifications,not proofs about an agent policy or natural-language intent. Provides explicit atomic taxonomies and composition formulas for file-grounded deception,tool safety and constraint compliance;localized context extraction separates tool errors,disclosure,document access and fabrication. Adds a trusted-trace evidence-manipulation boundary test,manual integrity atoms,latency breakdown,qualitative-intent proxy examples,multi-turn stress tests and action/task-level evaluation. Iterative feedback sends generated Dafny constraints and failed-predicate explanations back to an agent. Detailed appendices show which observed facts are composed and reveal semantic/specification mismatches that solver success alone cannot catch.
    - limits: §3.4 explicitly requires faithful extracted facts and a Dafny specification capturing intended constraints. Errors can enter decomposition, semantic extraction and synthesis. The solver does not verify the LLM, policy, truth of observations or intended meaning. No theorem proves end-to-end soundness of this oversight architecture. Observed trajectories and trusted tool-observation logs must retain provenance. Agent-controlled files and self-reports may be fabricated. Appendix A.3 assumes attackers cannot overwrite the trusted trace; without external provenance a trajectory-only checker cannot recover rewritten original events. Reference labels in §4.1 are majority judgments from GPT-5.2, Gemini-3.0-Pro and Claude-4.5-Opus across three rounds. Metrics measure agreement with that ensemble, rather than independently established ground truth. Opus is also the main judge and specification compiler; correlated model errors remain possible. Seven main agents: GPT-4o, Gemini-3.0-Pro, Claude-4.5-Sonnet, Qwen-2.5 7B/14B/32B/72B. Table 1 uses an Opus judge. Extended robustness uses eight judges including GPT-5.2, Grok-3, Gemini-2.5-Pro, Sonnet and four Qwen sizes. The main five-judge description and extended eight-judge analysis describe different settings. Full benchmark sizes are 2,000 Agent-SafetyBench cases, 400 VitaBench tasks and 200 Deceivers tasks. Vita evaluation actually uses a 100-task split; robustness/cross-verifier analysis uses 30 tasks and 164 tool calls. Deceivers weak-to-strong analysis uses 200 deceptive plus 200 nondeceptive trajectories per agent size. Full benchmark size is not every experiment’s denominator. Agent-SafetyBench reference protocol in Appendix D combines fulfillable/risk annotations with actual actions and uses the ensemble for ambiguity, unlike the uniformly ensemble-based wording in §4.1. Deceivers design labels provide a consistency check; broken tools do not automatically make every resulting trajectory dishonest. Some atomic questions remain substantial semantic judgments: whether a file contains requested information, an answer ignores content, or fabrication follows disclosure. Boolean answers do not make these judgments objective. Qualitative intent uses observable proxies rather than an exact semantics of politeness or manipulation. Agent-SafetyBench refinement uses Opus for decomposition/specification synthesis, including small-agent experiments. For the 7B-judge versus 72B-agent claim, the exact allocation of extraction/synthesis roles matters; judge size alone does not specify the capacity of the complete pipeline. Verification audits completed traces. Refinement regenerates responses or attempts and cannot undo actions already executed. X-Teaming and OpenClaw additions measure unsafe-trace detection, not online prevention or reduction in the native attack success rate. Dafny/Z3 checking can time out or fail without proving the negation. The paper models V as binary but does not fully specify timeout, unknown or syntax-error handling. The 6.4% backend error share classifies an unspecified sample of errors; it does not establish logical unsoundness in Z3. No independent artifact reproduction, compiler/version audit, complete human-label audit or systematic correctness proof of generated checkers is supplied. Attribution to the solver alone is limited when decomposition, contexts, prompts and generated code differ.

- Freer de Finetti human-steered Lean agents 2026 (2026)
    - title: Three Roads to de Finetti’s Theorem in Lean 4
    - what: Freer reports a Lean 4 formalization of the de Finetti–Ryll-Nardzewski equivalence for infinite random sequences: contractability iff exchangeability and conditional identical independent distribution. Three routes converge on one finite conditional-factorization interface; only reverse martingales cover general standard Borel targets, while the L2 and Koopman routes assume real square-integrable variables. Human-selected definitions, architecture and accepted edits guided Claude proof generation and GPT review/repair. The reported 42,745-line research artifact builds on a pinned Lean/mathlib toolchain, but needs substantial library refactoring. A reusable skill records proof patterns, concrete instance-management failures, LSP feedback and library search. Records exact theorem hypotheses; shared-interface inputs and kernel outputs; route-specific generality and indicator/embedding/L1/L2 transport obligations; human versus model responsibilities and model versions; original 2.5-page informal source and early common-ending design; reported build and axiom command; full Table 1 data and reader arithmetic discrepancy; code duplication/refactoring categories; skill size and repeated instance-pollution timeouts; absence of controlled proof-agent evaluation.
    - limits: Figure 1 assumes StandardBorelSpace Ω, measurable/standard-Borel nonempty target α, probability measure µ and each X i measurable, with X indexed by natural numbers. It concludes Contractable µ X ↔ Exchangeable µ X ∧ ConditionallyIID µ X. The theorem concerns infinite sequences. The Ω standard-Borel assumption is explicitly attributed to Lean conditional-expectation infrastructure; this reading does not claim it is a minimal mathematical necessity (§2, Figure 1). Exchangeability is invariance under arbitrary index permutations; equivalence with finite-support finite-marginal invariance is also reported formalized. Contractability compares equally long increasing subsequences. Conditional i.i.d. is relative to a directing random probability kernel, not unconditional independence. The paper also reports the converse conditional-i.i.d.-to-contractable direction. Exact formal definitions of all predicates and complete proof bodies are not printed here (§2). The common ending takes a sub-sigma algebra G and conditional factorization for each finite initial block, constructs a G-measurable probability kernel and packages ConditionallyIID. It centralizes measurability/product-measure uniqueness. Three routes sharing this interface reportedly exposed definition/support-lemma drift; reader inference: this checks agreement between routes, but is not an independent oracle for intended semantics, and common definitions/ending can share an error (§3.1, §5). Route A alone handles general standard Borel target spaces. Contractability supplies reverse-filtration symmetries; reverse martingale convergence supplies factorization. Real-valued conditional expectations must be converted to event indicators and transported through measurable embeddings back to arbitrary target-valued finite blocks. Supporting work includes Levy downward convergence, time reversal of filtrations and demonstrating the reversed process is a forward martingale (§1, §3.2). Route B has stronger real-valued square-integrability premises. Correlation bounds from contractability and Cesaro/L2 convergence give factorization. Its 12,476 local lines do not mean mathlib lacked CDF/Stieltjes theory. Existing StieltjesFunction, MeasurableStieltjes, conditional-CDF and CDFToKernel APIs overlap. Local code connects particular L1 empirical indicator limits to rational-cut monotonicity, normalization, right continuity, conditioning measurability and finite-block factorization. Authors recommend refactoring against existing APIs before considering upstream contributions (§3.3). Route C is also real square-integrable. On path space a shift induces the Koopman L2 operator; mean ergodic convergence identifies the Cesaro limit as projection onto the invariant subspace. Formal work connects that subspace to the invariant sigma algebra, converts L2 convergence into L1 conditional-expectation/set-integral identities for finite indicator products, and packages the common interface. These three proofs are not equally general (§3.4, Figure 2). Human directed architecture, mathematical interfaces, main definitions/theorem statements and acceptance of edits. Primary proof generators were Sonnet 4/4.5 and Opus 4.5 in Claude Code, with Lean CLI/LSP MCP feedback and the skill; GPT Codex reviewed/repaired, GPT Pro occasionally helped mathematics. Opus 4.5/4.6 collated about 4,000 commit messages for writing history; GPT Codex 5.3, GPT 5.5 and Claude 4.7 reviewed paper text. Paper-writing models must not be conflated with proof-generation models. Skill was also written under human direction using Claude (§GenAI declaration). Project began from approximately 2.5 pages of Kallenberg proofs, without a detailed blueprint. LLMs suggested auxiliary lemmas/interfaces/proof structure and filled proof holes, followed by review/refactoring/golfing. The common ending was formalized early. Authors qualitatively call route efforts comparable, but report no per-route human/model time breakdown. Three months is calendar duration, not autonomous proof runtime or quantified human labor (§1, abstract). Author reports Lean 4.27.0-rc1, pinned mathlib, lake build, and #print axioms deFinetti returning only propext, Quot.sound and Classical.choice. That checked name differs from the exact equivalence name in Figure 1; this paper alone does not establish an independently audited dependency list for every route variant. Reader did not execute a build or axiom command. Kernel acceptance with standard axioms does not itself certify intended theorem selection, minimal assumptions or reusable code quality (§1 artifact). Table 1 reports 42,745 lines, 112 files and 783 declarations. Reader independently added printed component rows: 43,512 lines, 112 files, 783 declarations. Line discrepancy is 767. All values visually verified; printed total preserved rather than silently corrected. No checkout establishes which line count reflects the archived artifact, whether categories overlap or why the discrepancy occurs. Rows group project role, not mathematical novelty (Table 1). Library is explicitly a checked research formalization, not mathlib-ready code. Four categories: existing-mathlib replacement candidates, wrappers/adapters, reusable bridges needing refactoring, exploratory route-specific code. First triage replaces/deletes overlap, keeps needed adapters and extracts reusable lemmas. Naming, imports and slow generated proofs need checks. Reported code volume is not evidence of necessary proof complexity or agent efficiency (§3 assessment). Skill contains 36 domain references, 6 slash-command recipes, 4 role prompts plus hooks/scripts. Concrete repeated failure is measure-theory instance pollution causing timeouts; guidance covers sub-sigma-algebra instances, conditional expectations, domain patterns, error-to-fix mapping, LSP goals/diagnostics and mathlib search to avoid reimplementation. Claude-compatible packaging was also exercised through Codex. Reported other-project reuse, including Numina setup installing the skill, is qualitative and not independently checked here (§4, §1 related work). Formalization reference identifies v1.2; software archive 294caf4a04e4d205a7d1de2dd52dc1cbbc0691e9 and skill archive a332440301a2ed286a4051f57ece1e3eed09cc9e are author-provided reproducibility pointers. No artifact commands were run. Reading the described skill does not install or adopt its instructions (§front matter, Reference 7). No controlled baseline, success/pass rate, failed-attempt dataset, token/cost accounting, human effort measurement, skill ablation or proof-throughput benchmark is supplied. Main evidence is a reported completed checked artifact plus qualitative development history. No distributed protocol, safety/liveness proof, Rust/Verus or TLA+ implementation appears. Direct relevance is evaluating human-steered LLM proof construction, semantic supervision, library reuse and maintenance; generalization beyond this mathematical case remains unmeasured.
    - how much was read: most of it read

    - record id: `freer-de-finetti-human-steered-lean-agents-2026-2026`
- From LLM-Generated Conjectures to Lean Formalizations: Automated Polynomial Inequality Proving via Sum-of-Squares Certificates (2026)
    - what: NSPI learns approximate polynomial sum-of-squares structures with Qwen3-8B, refines their coefficients numerically, recovers exact rational certificates, and generates Lean identity/nonnegativity proofs from templates. PolyIneqBench has 522 goals with 3–10 variables: 102 literature/competition examples and 420 synthetic ones. Reported final success is 44/102=43.14% on literature examples and 109/420=25.95% on synthetic examples, totaling 153/522≈29.3%. It has the best reported higher-variable success, while Maple/Z3 and LIPS outperform it on relevant low-dimensional/literature subsets. Exact final checking can protect accepted proofs from faulty heuristic discovery; several supplied theoretical/benchmark listings contain errors that require qualification. Training synthesizes polynomial–certificate pairs through eigenvalue shifting, factorized PSD matrices, LMI shifting and diagonally dominant/scaled diagonally dominant matrices. Over one million pairs support supervised cold start, followed by GRPO on unsolved samples in 3–5,6–8,9–10 variable curricula. Rewards combine coefficient error, format and missing/spurious-monomial penalties. At inference, 32 conjectures are ranked by residual; Gauss–Newton refinement is followed by full-rank projection/rationalization or rank-deficient LDLᵀ/Diophantine reconstruction. A final exact identity and positive weighted squares, rather than an LLM judgment or numerical tolerance, support the claimed Lean result. Appendices supply all stages, source examples, training costs, budget curves, complete Lean scripts, failure analysis and proposed extensions.
    - limits: A polynomial SOS certificate is sufficient for global nonnegativity, not necessary. Nonnegative polynomials outside polynomial SOS, inappropriate monomial support, rank constraints or failed rational recovery can remain unproved. Real-coefficient SOS existence does not itself establish a rational certificate in the chosen basis. The task is global nonnegativity over real variables, not general constrained inequalities, optimization algorithms, floating-point implementations or distributed protocols. Converting a constrained/informal problem to this target requires a separate faithful reduction, which Lean acceptance of the resulting polynomial alone does not certify. The final claimed trust boundary is Lean checking polynomial identity and nonnegativity using linear_combination and positivity. Heuristic generation, floating-point refinement and rational recovery need not be trusted if their concrete outputs are fully checked against the intended target. The reader did not run Lean or audit imports, bypass prevention, source conversion or repository implementation. Numerical YALMIP/SDP infeasibility or failure to produce an SOS is not a certified proof of non-SOS without an exact infeasibility witness. The source switches between numerical-certificate failure and categorical non-SOS claims; preserve the weaker measured evidence. Appendix D.2 explicitly states training is disjoint from evaluation. Both synthetic sets share the proposed construction families; no detailed deduplication, near-duplicate search, pretraining audit or independent distribution test is provided. Manual sampling of 50 examples per variable setting is not exhaustive semantic validation. One-hour cap and candidate budget 32 are reported. Mean runtimes are conditional on each method’s solved instances, which differ; reported speedups are not paired-instance or total-throughput comparisons. Baseline default prompts/configurations and API systems differ; no statistical uncertainty or repeated-seed variance is reported. Success at up to ten variables is empirical within the supplied datasets and certificate class. It does not prove asymptotic scalability, complete SOS discovery, human readability, neural novelty, convergence of numerical recovery or superiority on all polynomial inequalities. Future applications to global optimization and Lyapunov stability are proposals. No Verus, verified Rust, TLA+ or distributed-systems experiment appears. Transfer to generating invariant certificates would require an appropriate semantics, target reduction and checker.

- GLARE: Scalable Neuro-Symbolic Reward Shaping for LLM Agents via Group-Level Automata (2026)
    - what: GLARE shapes LLM-agent reinforcement-learning rewards by extracting observation triples, tracking their most recent values, asking a group-level LLM critic for milestones and bad behaviors, translating these into temporal logic and executing shared automata across the rollout group. ALFWorld and WebShop experiments compare task success and token usage with RL and direct LLM judges. Appendix E analyzes group-centered reward errors, but one claimed ranking lemma is false under its stated assumptions. Records all reward/normalization equations, complete experimental table values and uncertainty terms, exact token-chart labels, six behavioral templates and eight full prompts, all training settings, auditing denominators and concrete hallucination/translation failures. Checks the finite-trace/automaton boundary, supplies a bounded-noise counterexample to Lemma E.2, and preserves discrepancies in global-repeat templates, strict-sequence wording, normalization pseudocode and audit examples.
    - limits: §3/§4: Environment interaction is modeled as a POMDP with text observations and sparse task success. The actor uses history, whereas observation extraction operates independently per observation with the goal; action strings are mapped by rules. Deterministic action-to-symbol mapping is conditional on the emitted action, not proof that the temperature-sampled actor is deterministic or that its action succeeds. §4.1/Eq5: For each subject/category pair, a partial function stores exactly one latest value and retains values not contradicted by later extracted observations. This assumes extraction fidelity and adequate frame/update rules. There is no Bayesian uncertainty or explicit unknown-state semantics; stale or hallucinated values can persist. Multiple simultaneous values for one pair have no specified resolution, and a coarse state category can lose independent attributes if represented as separate values. §4.1/Figure3: Identical observation strings within a group share extraction. This removes repeated computation, not semantically equivalent spelling variants. Location and state triples are joined with action propositions to scan each full trace. Figure 3 updates door closed→open, retains door kitchen and adds sliced apple; no correctness proof connects the partial state to the hidden environment. §4.2/Figure11: The critic sees a whole sampled group and goal, identifies state-driven milestones and temporal dependencies, and assigns bad behaviors to six predefined categories. All-failed groups can still supply actually achieved necessary partial milestones. Main counterfactual wording must be read with Figure11’s prohibition on unobserved invented milestones; the all-failure mechanism is not access to a ground-truth planner. §4.3/AppendixA.3/Figures13–14: Positive nested-eventuality clauses are conjoined; negative constraints are separate monitors. Templates and manual examples remain substantive specification engineering. The translator is instructed to replace missing APs with False despite an explicitly incomplete AP list. This is a closed-world approximation: missing observations can make a desired milestone impossible or prohibit a valid action. §3.3/§4.3: The paper mixes finite-state acceptance over finite episodes with Büchi acceptance over infinite traces. General infinite-trace LTL is not always expressible as a deterministic Büchi automaton, and a finite-prefix accepting state alone does not decide arbitrary infinite behavior. Nested eventual milestones and safety templates admit useful specialized monitors, but the paper does not define finite-trace endpoint semantics, end-of-episode handling for X, or a verified translation contract. Spot compilation success establishes accepted syntax/automaton construction, not intended task meaning. Figure13: F(A & F(B)) permits A and B at the same time under ordinary LTL semantics because F includes the present. It does not enforce the prompt’s strict temporal sequence. A trace where A and B both hold only at one instant is a counterexample to strict precedence. Parallel formula F((A & F(B)) | (B & F(A))) permits either order, including simultaneity. No implementation inspection established whether a custom strict-F interpretation is used. AppendixA.3 versus Figure14: Printed global_repeat template G(act_A -> G(!act_A)) forbids even a first A because the inner G includes the current instant. Figure14 instead inserts X, G(act_A -> X(G(!act_A))), which permits one occurrence and forbids later ones under ordinary infinite-trace semantics. Preserve both; do not silently treat the appendix template as a correct repeat-only restriction. §4.3/§6.1: Shared formulas and deterministic transitions ensure repeatable rewards given the same symbolic trace, chosen automata and reward procedure. They do not ensure that errors relative to true rewards are constant across trajectories. An incorrect shared constraint can penalize valid behavior selectively. Neural extraction/critic/translation are stochastic at temperature 1 and formulas are regenerated per group; determinism is conditional on these outputs. §4.4/Eq6: Positive rewards depend on a set P(q) of progressive successor states, described as moving toward acceptance without an explicit graph-distance or progress-ranking algorithm. Trend rewards cover strict intervals between milestone updates, hence can use later progress when retrospectively rescanning the trace. Initial timestamp, handling of empty milestones, repeated accepting states and whether an already rejecting safety state incurs repeated penalties are not fully specified. §4.4/Eq6/Eq7: A violation of any negative monitor triggers one penalty indicator, not a sum over violations. R_total=r_env+β*r_LTL+r_fmt. Appendix sets λpos=2, λtrend=.5 and λneg=−.5, success10/failure0 and format−1; the separate shaping multiplier β is not numerically identified there and shares notation with KL β elsewhere. Dense positive rewards can reward time between milestones; no potential-based shaping theorem, policy invariance, optimal-policy guarantee, hard action shield or universal safety proof is supplied. §4.5/Eq8 versus AppendixC/Algorithm1 lines13–18: Main text explicitly pools all valid environment-step rewards in a group, without timestamp alignment. Pseudocode instead computes mean/std across trajectories separately at each timestamp t up to Tmax, with no explicit short-trajectory masking. These are different normalizations; the paper alone cannot resolve which generated reported results. Main pooled normalization weights long trajectories by their number of steps. §3.2/§4.5/AppendixE: Main Eq8 normalizes immediate scalar step rewards, then invokes a trajectory-ratio GRPO objective. Exact token/action weighting, reward-to-return propagation and masking implementation are not fully specified. Appendix E analyzes scalar trajectory reward rankings under a single common denominator; it is not a derivation that either differing step-level normalization yields an unbiased policy gradient or stable convergence. AppendixE/LemmaE.1: Under r_i=r*_i+b+η_i and a common positive normalization denominator, group centering cancels a common additive b exactly. If all η_i are equal, true rankings are preserved. This algebra is valid; it does not remove trajectory-dependent systematic reward errors or establish that a shared automaton has equal η_i. AppendixE/LemmaE.2 and CorollaryE.3: The proof correctly derives reversal iff η_j−η_i exceeds the positive true reward gap Δ*. Its next claim that any nonzero intra-group error variance gives positive reversal probability does not follow. Counterexample: true gap1, independent errors each in {−.1,+.1}; errors are not almost surely equal and have nonzero variance, but their difference is at most .2, so reversal probability is zero. A deterministic difference in the favorable direction also disproves the stated generality. A support/tail condition P(η_j−η_i>Δ*)>0 is required; independent nondegenerate Gaussian errors satisfy it. Corollary’s otherwise clause is likewise too strong. §6.1/AppendixE remark: Fixed evaluation logic alone is insufficient for reliable ordering relative to true goals. For example, a shared but wrong prohibition on a task-required move can reverse rankings deterministically. The paper supplies no measured latent true rewards, bound on trajectory-dependent grounding/specification errors, or proof that GLARE has lower error-difference variance than judges. Reported learning stability is empirical within tested runs. §5/AppendixA.2: ALFWorld total benchmark size is stated as3,827 across six household task families; the paper does not specify its exact train/validation/test instance counts or task split. Models are Qwen2.5-1.5B/7B-Instruct in verl-agent. Baseline values are borrowed from GiGPO except GRPO rerun on author hardware; not every comparison is a fresh identically controlled run. Table1: Three random seeds, success rates per task and overall, with ± values whose statistic is not named. Overall success is not necessarily an unweighted average of six columns. No formal significance test is reported. For1.5B,84.5−72.4=12.1 and84.5−76.4=8.1 percentage points; the paper’s relative-gain percent wording is inaccurate. For7B,86.7−75.7=11 and86.7−82.4=4.3 points. GLARE is not best in every task column:1.5B direct judge Pick2=77.8 versus73.6;7B PPO Clean92.5 versus89.9. §5.2/§5.3: Direct group and trace judges are said to share judging policy, prompts, reward scale and trend rewards; context scope differs. Group judge is early-stopped for poor performance; trace judge supplies Table1’s best direct-judge comparison. 7B trace result is selected at peak step100 before later collapse. No complete group-judge success table or full learning curves quantify every stability claim. Step-level judge is excluded for reported20min/step versus100–200seconds for other settings, with the latter time scope not explicitly defined. §5.4/Figure4: Only the first50 training steps enter the token comparison, because trajectories change with learning. Chart labels are averages per training step, not per environment action. Completion tokens GLARE28,042.6/trace184,986.6=15.16%; prompt tokens215,479/294,921.8=73.06%; unweighted combined token count243,521.6/479,908.4=50.74%. These are token ratios, not15% total FLOPs, wall-clock time, monetary cost or end-to-end training compute. Group judge completion54,604.2 is also above GLARE. §6.3/AppendixB: Gemini-3-Flash audits800 textual observations and200 training-log trajectory groups; no human-ground-truth validation, judge agreement or confidence intervals. AP error4.4%, omission2.6%, redundancy1.1%; AppendixB.1 narrows omission to non-seen predicates. Exact triplet/formula counts and temporal sampling protocol are not supplied. The critic’s milestone adequacy/dependency judgments are not independently quantified by these two module audits. §6.3/AppendixB.2: Regex formatting repair and compiler-error retry precede discarding remaining noncompilable formulas. Semantic mismatch12% includes invalid/ineffective plus harmful errors, and harmful4% uses all formulas as denominator, not4% of mismatches. Rates do not measure raw syntax failure, discarded-formula frequency, or all end-to-end reward errors. Harmful errors can punish valid actions, beyond merely reducing reward density. Figures7–8: Concrete AP failures include missing seen(microwave), hallucinating drawer2 located at self, and butterknife/butterknive duplicate names. Redundancy-case prose also mentions a cabinet not present in the displayed example. Harmful negative LTL forbids moving a heated egg into the goal fridge. These illustrate observation/translation faults; no formal proof or exhaustive failure taxonomy follows from a few examples. Figure10: The extractor prompt says only visible facts, noun-normalized IDs and loc/state/goal_match/seen categories; its displayed input contains room, closed drawer and unsliced tomato, but output invents a held cup2 and omits instructed seen triples. The definition says underscores while most examples use spaces. Figure15 calls egg-state-heat and apple-state-hot redundant despite different subjects, contrary to its different-apple example. Figure16’s seen-false exception is not identical to Figure10’s seen-true demonstration. These printed audit/example inconsistencies limit the claim of a clean semantic reference; actual runtime prompt identity is unverified. §6.4/Figure6: Negative-only is reported worse than the sparse baseline, positive-only close to full GLARE with larger deficits on Cool/Pick2. Radar plot has no point labels, replicate counts or uncertainty; exact coordinates are not invented. Positive-only visually exceeds full on some axes, so the synergy conclusion is an aggregate interpretation. No separate ablation isolates state tracker, deduplication, critic or trend shaping, and harmful penalties preclude a hard-safety conclusion. §6.5/Table2a: WebShop preserves critic/translator and changes extractor few-shot examples plus free-form product attributes. Scores/success are GRPO75.8/56.8, direct judge78.8/61.2, GLARE80.2/63.4. This is manually adapted cross-environment evaluation, not zero-shot transfer of an unchanged system. WebShop model scale, split sizes, training/evaluation populations, repeat count and uncertainty are not specified in this comparison. §6.6/Table2b: Authors explicitly say GiGPO beats GLARE on original ALFWorld and WebShop. Perturbed ALFWorld injects random distractor descriptions while preserving task-relevant names/relations: GiGPO86.7original→71.4perturbed, GLARE83.3perturbed. Table2 does not give the corresponding original GLARE row or model scale. Do not equate unrelated86.7 entries without evidence. Perturbation construction/sampling/repeats/uncertainty are not documented; robustness scope is this textual distractor setting. AppendixD/Figure9: Across training, positive milestone triggers increase, unique observation counts fall and generated negative-LTL counts generally fall with spikes. Plots provide no raw series/uncertainty or causal control; falling negative counts alone does not prove penalties are never arbitrary or that behavior improves on every safety criterion. AppendixA.2: Prompt2048/response512tokens;50environmentsteps/episode; group8×16rollouts=128parallel environments; actorLR1e−6, PPOcritic1e−5; rollouttemperature1/validation.4; minibatch256/KL.01/150iterations. Auxiliary4B FP8model temperature1 is already pretrained; training-free refers to no auxiliary fine-tuning, not no policy RL.1.5B policy/reward share2H100,7Bpolicy4A800+reward1A800; these different hardware regimes are not a scale-normalized cost study. §7: This is an LLM-generated temporal reward/specification pipeline, not a proof of agent task correctness. No Verus/verified Rust, TLA+, distributed-protocol safety/liveness proof or mechanized proof kernel is evaluated. It adds concrete evidence about specification grounding, monitor execution, evaluation noise and the gap between deterministic checking and correct goals. Official README: repository is provided for reviewer verification of authenticity, with reorganization promised upon acceptance. Lists trpo/ reward/automata/extraction/evaluation/prompt modules, verl-agent/Spot/torch/numpy/vllm dependencies and training-script/configuration entry points. README names do not establish that the printed normalization or prompt discrepancies are resolved; no code was inspected or executed. §6.2/Figure5: Direct judges can identify bad behavior in reasoning but emit incorrect reward-step indices, described as off-by-one errors or hallucinated indices. Figure5 reasoning names steps27–31–32 while displayed penalty output includes20and26. This is an illustrative reasoning-to-output binding failure, with no measured frequency.     - title: Large Language Models (LLM) and Temporal Logic of Actions (TLA): How Effective are LLMs for Verification Systems
    - what: This poster reports a systematic natural-language-to-TLA+ evaluation over 205 community specifications, 30 models and four prompting strategies. The dataset is drawn from the TLA+ Examples repository, so its task mix is not a random sample of industrial requirements.

- HarnessLLM (2026)
    - title: HarnessLLM: Rust Verification Harness Generation with Large Language Models
    - what: An LLM workflow turns a Rust project's existing unit tests into harnesses (small driver programs with symbolic inputs) for the Kani bounded model checker. It finds the call patterns in tests, builds symbolic arguments for the project's own types by following constructors, and repairs compile errors without letting the LLM invent types.
    - number: 9 Rust crates, 494 tests, 6651 lines of test code: 294 calling scenarios extracted, 94.66% preserved the original behavior
    - number: 294 of 294 harnesses compile in Kani within 10 attempts; 97.3% within 3 attempts; about 1.4 generations per harness
    - number: Average 145 s and $0.03 per harness (GPT-4.1)
    - number: Kani Autoharness baseline produced harnesses for 41% of the same scenarios
    - limits: Needs a codebase with good tests; sparse tests lower scenario coverage. 'Success' means the harness compiles in Kani, not that it checks anything beyond memory safety and panics. Bounded checking; the LLM picks the bounds (slice lengths, unwind). Only functions visible in source (macro-generated ones are missed).

- HERMES Lean reasoning agent (2026)
    - title: HERMES: Towards Efficient and Verifiable Mathematical Reasoning in LLMs
    - what: Introduces HERMES, a tool-augmented agent that interleaves informal LLM reasoning with Lean4 formalization, proof/counter-proof attempts, verifier feedback and a memory block of validated steps. Each selected critical step is translated to Lean, checked for compilation and back-translated for semantic agreement; a prover attempts both the goal and its negation, and the result directs continuation or revision. Top-k embedding retrieval preserves context across chains. Theoretical token complexity is between outcome and process reward methods; ablations show prover and memory modules matter, and appendices document failure distributions, prompts and five-run variance.
    - number: average accuracy improvement 23% over four benchmarks; up to 40% AIME improvement with 80% fewer FLOPs
    - number: Hermes@1 DeepSeek-V3.2: 98.4 MATH, 70.0 AIME, 83.3 CollegeMath, 33.6 HARDMath2
    - number: Hermes uses 4–6× fewer reasoning tokens than reward-based methods
    - number: manual audit: CoT coverage 87% MATH/76% AIME; translation accuracy 86%/80%; 5.5 and 10.4 calls per problem
    - limits: Only a subset of critical steps is verified, formalization can fail, and an unproved step yields verification failure rather than a correctness guarantee for the whole answer. Performance depends on Lean/Mathlib coverage, autoformalizer/prover quality and sampling budgets; geometry and sparse-library topics fare worse. Manual audit samples only 100 steps.
