- Improving LLM Code Reasoning via Semantic Equivalence Self-Play with Formal Verification (2026)
    - what: # Improving LLM Code Reasoning via Semantic Equivalence Self-Play with Formal Verification (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Poon Tsz Nok and Antonio Valerio Miceli Barone use generated Haskell program pairs to train a language model to reason about code. Alice generates a transformed program and either an equivalence proof (SEQ) or a distinguishing input (SINQ). Bob judges equivalence. The paper evaluates rejection-based supervised fine-tuning and downstream benchmarks. It supplies no new soundness theorem for Liquid Haskell or the complete training pipeline. Its reported verifier labels require an additional qualification: the inspected current wrapper does not independently enforce that the model submitted the intended equality obligation.  This record covers arXiv:2604.17010v2, dated 3 May 2026, all 20 pages, 34 references, 19 figures, eight tables, two numbered listings and Appendices A–H. The official and inventory PDFs are byte-identical. ACL Anthology lists “Findings of the Association for Computational Linguistics: ACL 2026,” July 2026, pages32273–32292, DOI10.18653/v1/2026.findings-acl.1615 (saved publication metadata). That publication metadata is separate from the arXiv version actually read. The paper does not identify an experimental repository revision. Static findings below concern commit `490fe084d705cb501e0fe34e592f17db2eb44a7b`, not an authenticated historical run.  ## What the method establishes  The reference corpus starts with OpenCodeInstruct programs translated into Haskell by DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-70B. Compilation and execution on a generated input filter the candidates, yielding about 28,000 functions (§3.2, Figure2). Passing that filter establishes that a particular candidate compiled and executed on one input under the filter. It does not prove that the translation preserves the original task, that the reference function meets its intended specification, or that it behaves correctly on every input. AppendixA's recursive versus fold-based reverse example illustrates alternative Haskell implementations; its typing discussion does not establish that every Haskell program is pure, total or safe.  SEQ asks Alice to generate Q and a Liquid Haskell proof of equality with P for all inputs of the declared type. The intended mechanism uses reflected definitions and proof by logical evaluation (PLE). SINQ asks for an input on which their observations differ. AppendixB's SEQ example compares `double x = x + x` with `double' x = 2 * x`; its SINQ example distinguishes `"zero"` from `"non-positive"` at zero. These are concrete proof and witness examples, not distributed-system specifications. Conditional equality in the checker semantics remains distinct from a specification of the original natural-language task.  Bob samples answers, normally ten, and difficulty is `10 * (1 - N_success/N)` (§3.4). Alice generates with target difficulty10 and separately learns to predict observed difficulty. The paper retains hard generator cases and a smaller easy sample, difficulty-prediction examples, and successful proof-generation traces. Bob is fixed throughout seven Alice rounds and fine-tuned once afterward. Each Alice training round restarts from the original base model on accumulated examples; generation uses the previous adapter. This is supervised fine-tuning with rejection sampling. AppendixC discusses reinforcement learning as a possible extension; it is not the evaluated implementation.  The base model is DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, with LoRA adapters. AppendixE gives temperature0.6, top-p0.95, top-k20, presence penalty1.5 and a32,768-token context. The actual reported hard threshold is3, rather than the example/default5. A main run takes “about 3 days on 4 NVIDIA L40S GPUs” (AppendixE.4). That is an author-reported duration, not a reproduced measurement.  ## Static checks of the verification and selection boundaries  Seven selected repository files were read completely: root and SEQ_v2 READMEs, `run_liquidhaskell.py`, `SEQ.py`, the seven-round launcher, the dataset compiler filter and the EquiBench evaluator. The full nontruncated repository tree was used only as metadata. Stored datasets, predictions, checkpoints and scientific result JSON were not loaded. No author code was imported or executed; no GHC, Liquid Haskell, solver or training run was performed.  The main equivalence wrapper accepts a generated code block, post-thinking response or entire response as the proof body (`generate_proof_body`). Its fixed refinement lemma template is commented out. It inserts the model's text into a module containing reflected P and Q, rejects an empty body, invokes GHC with the LiquidHaskell plugin and returns `"proved"` on exit0 (`verify_equivalence`). No inspected check independently requires a refinement annotation asserting the intended equality, fixes the proposition outside model control, or rejects model-supplied additional assumptions/options. A successful process exit alone therefore does not show that this wrapper submitted and checked the target equality. This is a static obligation-integrity issue, not a demonstrated successful bypass or evidence that any reported training example was mislabeled. The small `run_liquidhaskell.py` example overwrites its initial annotated double example with an addNumbers proof lacking an explicit equality refinement annotation; it is source text, not a saved successful run. The paper's second few-shot proof example has the same omission.  The inequivalence wrapper rejects a Q compilation error and accepts different successful stdout. It also accepts one successful and one failed execution, or differing failure statuses (`check_divergence`). Runtime and timeout statuses can thus contribute to labels. An observed timeout is not proof of semantic nontermination. Reference compilation is checked separately, but that does not remove every execution failure. The record does not infer the frequency or historical impact of these cases.  Selection differs from the paper in the pinned driver. Both generator branches append candidates only when difficulty is at least the threshold; the later easy-generator sample therefore has no candidates. The difficulty-prediction sample takes at most `0.5 * n_hard` easy items, at most one third of the resulting subset, rather than half the subset. SEQ generation training retains the original target10 prompt; SINQ generation training rebuilds its prompt with observed Bob difficulty. Both difficulty-prediction branches use observed difficulty as their output label. The source uses `>=`, while the paper's hard-case definition uses `>`. The launcher sets threshold1, versus the paper's reported3 and README example5. Its base-model restart and single final Bob training do support the described schedule. These differences do not identify the configuration actually used for the paper's experiments.  `parse_bob_output` normally returns a Boolean, but a missing match falls through as None and an exception returns `(None,None)`. `run_bob` uses truthiness: ordinary missing output is treated as an inequivalence answer; the exception tuple is treated as an equivalence answer. That can affect difficulty and training selection. No stored-output audit establishes how often either case happened.  The selected dataset validator checks function-binding heuristics and compilation; its input-argument filter is commented out. It does not itself implement the paper's one-input execution stage, which may occur elsewhere. The EquiBench evaluator uses a Haskell-specific system prompt on the loaded benchmark pairs, extracts the first regex match rather than necessarily the final answer, and skips parse failures/exceptions before computing accuracy, precision, recall and F1. It saves parsed/unparsed counts, but the paper tables do not give those counts. These are current evaluation-path limitations, not a reanalysis of stored experimental outputs.  ## Evaluation and numerical evidence  The main E0 regime attempts SEQ/SINQ50/50 over500 reference programs per round. E1 is SINQ-only with500, E2 is96/4 with500, and E3 is SINQ-only with40 (Table1). Tables6/8 and Figures6/12/15/18/19 report total accepted pairs:  - E0:34SEQ and903SINQ, total937 - E1:1,839SINQ - E2:62SEQ and78SINQ, total140 - E3:156SINQ  AppendixF estimates E0 yields as34/1750≈1.94% and903/1750=51.6%, using expected branch-attempt counts. It defines expected accepted volume `K=P*(p*r_SEQ+(1-p)*r_SINQ)`, predicts E2≈19.632 pairs per round and chooses E3≈38.04 references, rounded to40. This controls an estimated pair count. It does not equalize actual accepted counts, proof tokens, difficulty, class balance or compute. E2 and E3 actually have140 and156 pairs. The text's “isolates the causal effect” and “fixed compute resources” claims exceed that control. The paper itself acknowledges residual difficulty/class differences and small samples, and asks for stronger statistical evidence (§7). No independent training-seed replication or uncertainty for those causal differences is established by averaging16 evaluation samples/trials.  Intrinsic Bob accuracy increases87.57→91.34% on training-split generated challenges and88.24→88.79% on unseen-split challenges (Table2). The latter is0.55 percentage points. The seven overall difficulty means are0.50,0.50,0.65,0.87,0.66,0.93,1.18 (Figure3); they increase overall but not monotonically. Figure7's SEQ means end1.89, after2.17,3.00 and1.00, so AppendixH's “2.0−3.0 in later ones” is only a loose description of some rounds. Difficulty is performance relative to this Bob and these accepted cases, not an independent measure of semantic complexity.  Table3 reports Haskell HumanEval Bob Pass@1 rising17.7→26.4%, with compilation errors130→110; final Alice is26.3%/104errors. On MBPP Bob rises26.7→36.9%, errors250→203; Alice is34.3%/218errors. Figure9 labels final Alice MBPP errors212 instead of Table3's218. This discrepancy remains unresolved. Plot averages over16 trials do not establish independent repetitions of the complete training process.  EquiBench's largest E0 accuracy increase is OJ_V56.5→69.8%, or13.3points (Figures4–5). OJ_A rises50.2→57.4, OJ_VA55.8→55.9, STOKE48.0→51.8; DCE falls55.1→51.9 and TVM51.7→50.1. F1 improves on OJ_A/OJ_VA/OJ_V/STOKE and declines on DCE/TVM. PySecDB accuracy rises67.6→68.8 and F151.3→54.0 (Table4). CodeXGlue accuracy rises48.8→50.5, while recall falls51.1→41.7 and F147.9→43.2 (Table5). The authors describe near-random defect detection; no statistical chance-baseline analysis is supplied.  Table7 does not support universal dominance by the mixed or volume-controlled SEQ arms. E0 loses to E1 on MBPP Bob36.9 versus38.8 and Alice34.3 versus39.3, and HumanEval Alice26.3 versus28.1. E2 loses to E3 on OJ_V accuracy63.2 versus65.8/F161.5 versus64.7, TVM accuracy48.8 versus50.8/F133.5 versus33.9, and CodeXGlue F144.1 versus48.5; PySecDB accuracy ties68.7. E2 wins several other comparisons, including the four Haskell generation scores. These small, mixed results support investigating complementary supervision, not a general proof that SEQ uniquely improves reasoning.  The validated-count plots contain percentage-denominator errors. Figures12,15,18 divide labels by250, even though E1 uses500SINQ attempts, E2 expects480SEQ/20SINQ attempts, and E3 uses40SINQ attempts. E1 consequently prints yields above100%. For example248/500=49.6%, not99.2%; E3's26/40=65%, not10.4%. Figure15's actual branch counts are random, so480/20 are expected denominators rather than authenticated attempt totals. The reported raw accepted counts and Table8 totals need not be wrong merely because the percentage labels are wrong. Figure19 reuses the raw counts. All figures, tables, count trajectories and these qualifications are retained separately.  ## Contribution and limits  The useful contribution is a concrete way to generate semantic reasoning tasks and combine proof-oriented examples with distinguishing-input examples. The study exposes low positive-equivalence yield, verification-wrapper obligations, label handling and the need to compare training volumes and costs carefully. Its strongest direct relevance is to evaluating LLM agents that write proofs: successful verifier invocation must be tied to a fixed intended theorem, and benchmark gains require checks beyond accepted proof labels.  The paper explicitly notes proof-generation failures, Liquid Haskell/reflection limitations, partiality/nontermination and the gap to low-level, stateful and memory-manipulating programs (§8). There is no Verus, Rust, TLA+ or distributed-protocol experiment. Applying these ideas to distributed verification is a reader inference. The record claims a whole paper and selected static-source read, not a fresh proof, experimental reproduction, exhaustive repository audit, historical configuration recovery or verification of every generated item.
    - limits: checker-semantics/inputtypes/termination/reflection/currentwrapperdoesnotfixexpectedtheorem/expectedpairmatchingnotcomputecontrol/smallmixedablations/currentpinnotauthenticatedhistoricalrun
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Integrating Symbolic Execution with LLMs for Automated Generation of Program Specifications (2026)
    - what: # Integrating Symbolic Execution with LLMs for Automated Generation of Program Specifications (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Yang, Ma, Wang, Xu, Cao, Zhan, Li and Gu present SESpec, a pipeline that combines QCP symbolic execution, LLM proposals and Frama-C checking to generate C/ACSL loop invariants and function contracts. Its main evidence is a benchmark evaluation, not a new soundness or completeness theorem. The authors seek specifications “as strong and informative as possible” (§1, p.2, Q2); the experiments measure syntax, invariant validity and sufficiency for supplied or masked goals. Those measurements do not establish that every accepted contract completely characterizes a program.  This read covers the 27-page arXiv:2506.09550v4 dated 20 January 2026, initially submitted 11 June 2025. The PDF says “Manuscript submitted to ACM” and prints a placeholder DOI (Q1, Q47; saved arXiv history). The paper does not establish an accepted venue. Its linked repository now resolves to anon-hiktyq/TSE2026-SESpec; that name is not publication evidence.  ## Method and assumptions (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The input is C code with optional verification goals and potentially designer-supplied preconditions. The default precondition initializes and constrains reachable memory locations; the flattened memory model represents primitive locations and tracks validity, separation and aliasing. Other requirements are left to users (§§2.1, 3.1, Q6, Q8). Therefore a successful generated contract remains conditional on those input assumptions. It does not recover designer intent or establish that every caller satisfies the assumptions.  For loop-free segments, symbolic execution constructs a disjunction of path conditions and symbolic stores, which the paper describes as the strongest postcondition under the chosen precondition (§3.1, p.8, Q8). Calls are processed bottom-up and their generated summaries are reused. At a loop, a single-iteration analysis identifies unchanged and non-inductive variables; the remaining variables receive inductive templates. Five template forms describe unchanged values, skipped loops, non-inductive updates, inductive updates and verification goals (§§3.2–4.3, Q9–Q13). LLMs fill ACSL placeholders using this context, with error-specific prompts for repair, weakening, adjustment, strengthening or regeneration (§4.4, Q14–Q15).  The symbolic guidance has an explicit exception: nested loops are “processed purely via LLM” without the outer-loop preconditions and templates (§4.2, p.11, Q12). Recursive functions, library functions and system calls are outside the current supported scope (§6.2, Q22). Supporting singly linked data structures is distinct from supporting recursive function calls.  Definition 1 checks initiation, preservation and `Inv ∧ ¬B ⇒ Q`. The last is called “Termination,” but states what follows **if the loop exits** (§4, p.10, Q10). It does not prove that the loop eventually exits. The inspected generation prompt says “Do not generate loop variant” (Q41). Algorithm 4 can reach an iteration limit and remove invalid invariants, leaving a valid subset; validity alone does not ensure that the retained subset proves the goal (Q14–Q15). The paper supplies no guarantee that arbitrary generation attempts succeed or converge.  QCP supplies symbolic states and a predicate/lemma library; Frama-C checks ACSL obligations using SMT. A translator converts ACSL to QCPL and back, while the LLM sees natural language and ACSL skeletons (§5.1, Q16–Q17). The authors acknowledge that QCP “may introduce unproven lemmas that require additional Coq proofs” (Q16). They do not prove the combined translator/pipeline sound or show that every generated obligation has an independently checked Coq proof. Accepted results depend on the specified C/memory semantics, preconditions, predicate definitions, translation and verifier configuration.  ## Concrete cases (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The motivating CheckCalFun example sums up to ten array entries inside a struct and writes the checksum through a pointer. Its caller sets length to 3 and asserts equality with the first three array elements (§2, Fig.1, pp.4–5, Q4). The figure and saved benchmark input include validity assumptions, array values between 0 and 100, and length at most 10; these arithmetic and size assumptions are not all synthesized default memory conditions (Q46; figure-evidence.json). Negative length is permitted by the printed upper bound and skips the loop. SESpec’s templates distinguish that case from positive-length prefix summation and retain unchanged fields. The authors report that GPT-4o, GPT-5 and AutoSpec produced incorrect candidate annotations, including unsupported sum syntax and invariants that assume the loop is entered (Q4–Q5). This is an illustrative example, not a controlled GPT-5 benchmark.  The evaluation also includes linear and nonlinear arithmetic loops, nested/multiple loops, structs and pointers, array operations, and singly linked lists. The three added datasets are numer-S, with 20 linear and 20 nonlinear loops; pIp, with 50 selected and adapted Sun Search aerospace-control cases; and list-S, with 24 singly linked-list cases derived from LIG-MM (§6.1, Q19, Q21). The pIp programs were explicitly “adapt[ed] and reformulate[d]” for Frama-C and QCP (Q19). The largest printed dataset average is 57 lines for pIp. This is evidence about selected small C functions, not verification of the complete aerospace system.  ## Evaluation and measured results (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The stated verifier is Frama-C 29.0 Copper with WP, Z3, the Typed memory model and a three-second timeout per query. The models are GPT-3.5-turbo, GPT-4o-mini, GPT-4o and Claude-3.7-sonnet, all at temperature 0.7 (§6.1, p.17, Q20). The main table reports Pass@1, Pass@3 and Pass@5. The paper does not give exact dated model identifiers, hardware, end-to-end runtime, measured token/cost totals, repeated-run variability or confidence intervals.  The three rates have different meanings: syntax requires well-formed annotations; validity additionally requires semantic checks such as invariant initiation and preservation; accuracy additionally requires the verification goal (§6.3, Q23). Goal accuracy is stronger than validity but remains specific to that goal. The paper’s argument that a syntax/validity gap rules out trivial accepted invariants is insufficient: a gap shows some candidates fail, not that every accepted candidate is informative or nonvacuous. No general equivalence, maximality or specification-completeness score is reported.  Table 1 reports 354/395 programs solved, about 89.62%, with dataset counts 133 SyGuS, 46 OOPSLA, 21 SV-COMP, 30 NLA, 40 numer-S, 51 Frama-C, 50 pIp and 24 list-S (§6.2, Q21–Q22). The dataset solved counts match the strongest-model Pass@5 results in Table 2: 133, 40, 16, 25, 34, 39, 44 and 23. Thus “nearly 90%” is an aggregate at that reported outcome, not a single-attempt success rate for all models. The printed category breakdown reports 197/204 linear, 41/53 nonlinear, 15/24 nested/multiple, 45/53 memory-and-loop, 56/58 iteration-free and 0/3 external/recursive-call cases. There is a counting discrepancy: the memory-and-loop cells sum to 55, not 53; the SV-COMP category cells sum to 23, not its summary 21. The dataset and category totals each print 395, while the cell matrix sums to 397. No value was silently corrected.  Table 2 contains 32 model/dataset rows and 288 percentage cells, all retained in numeric-evidence.json (Q25). Representative accuracy results are:  - GPT-4o: SyGuS 93.98% at Pass@1 and 100% at Pass@5; pIp 64% to 78%; list-S 54.17% to 79.17% - Claude-3.7-sonnet at Pass@5: SyGuS 100%, OOPSLA 86.96%, SV-COMP 76.19%, NLA 83.33%, numer-S 85%, Frama-C 76.47%, pIp 88%, list-S 95.83% - GPT-3.5-turbo at Pass@5: OOPSLA 52.17%, NLA 56.67%, pIp 76%, list-S 16.67%, showing substantial model dependence  Some printed values need reconciliation. OOPSLA GPT-4o-mini accuracy decreases from 65.41% at Pass@1 to 43.48% at Pass@3 and 47.83% at Pass@5; syntax also decreases slightly. These do not behave as cumulative success counts would. The sampling/population explanation is unspecified. The Frama-C GPT-3.5 Table 2 accuracy is 54.17%, versus Table 3’s 51.0%; its Table 2 percentages align arithmetically with 48 cases rather than the stated 51, but that does not establish the actual denominator. The prose says GPT-4o and Claude match on numer-S, while the rows give 82.50% and 85.00% (Q24–Q25). These are reporting limitations, not independently reproduced failures.  The ablation uses a stated random subset of 100 cases, 70 loops and 30 functions, caps refinement at three iterations, and says token usage is consistent (§6.4, Fig.5, Q26–Q27). It displays three configurations despite saying four: LLM alone and LLM+symbolic execution at Pass@3, and LLM+symbolic execution+refinement at Pass@1. Loop accuracy rises 28.75 → 60.00 → 87.21%; function accuracy rises 16.67 → 46.67 → 86.67%. The rows support a benefit from symbolic context and refinement on this subset. They do not show the “perfect scores across all metrics” claimed in the prose: the final loop syntax/validity are 95.71%, accuracy 87.21%. Several loop percentages are not integer fractions of 70, with no averaging or denominator explanation. No measured budget totals substantiate exact token equivalence, and the model used for this ablation is not identified in the paper.  Table 3 compares 13 methods/model configurations over eight datasets, mixing cited and author-reproduced values marked by gray shading (Q28; figure-evidence.json). Stronger-model SESpec has clear gains on pIp and list-S. Broad dominance does not hold across configurations: SESpec-3.5 loses to AutoSpec-3.5 on OOPSLA, SV-COMP, NLA, numer-S and Frama-C; SESpec-4o ties ACInv-4o on OOPSLA and SV-COMP. Specialized HOLA scores 93.5% on OOPSLA versus SESpec-Claude’s 87.0%, and CLAUSE2INV scores 93.3% on NLA versus 83.3%. The authors’ causal explanations about model reasoning and baseline limitations are interpretations, not controlled causal measurements (Q29–Q31).  The masked-goal experiment uses SESpec GPT-4o at Pass@1 and AutoSpec’s own configuration (§6.5, Fig.6, Q32–Q33). SESpec accuracy falls from 93.98% to 80.45% on SyGuS, and from 70.83% to 64.71% on Frama-C. AutoSpec falls from 85.71% to 57.89%, and 60.78% to 29.41%. These are decreases of 13.53/6.12 and 27.82/31.37 percentage points respectively. The Frama-C unmasked 70.83% differs from Table 2’s GPT-4o Pass@1 66.67%. This experiment supports useful generation when the goal is hidden, assessed against that goal afterward; it does not prove complete goal-independent semantics or compare identical model and sampling budgets.  ## Threats, artifacts and relevance (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The paper’s contamination assurance is too strong. Section 7 says it avoids “few-shot prompting” and concludes that outputs are grounded in program semantics “rather than in prior exposure to benchmark-related data” (Q34). Section 5.2 explicitly supplies two public array/list examples to calibrate inductive predicates (Q18), and Figure 4’s prompt includes example guidance. Avoiding fine-tuning does not establish the pretrained models never encountered these benchmarks. No contamination audit is reported. This is an unresolved assurance, not evidence that leakage actually occurred. SpecGen is excluded because it targets Java/JML and a different custom dataset (§7, Q35).  The author repository was inspected at commit da17c126b159a06a5d1fecde5311737811a73145, retrieved 19 October 2026. The original URL redirects to TSE2026-SESpec. The recursive root tree is truncated, so a separate complete src tree was obtained; only metadata, README and 13 selected source/config/prompt/input files were read. Those files contain 1799 logical lines. Stored RESULTS, output programs, serialized vector databases and experiment spreadsheets were not loaded. No supplementary appendix was recovered in the inspected root/src listings; this record claims a whole read of the paper PDF, not an unseen appendix.  Two concrete source differences limit reproducibility. The paper specifies three seconds per WP query, but both inspected verifier wrappers actively set ten seconds, with three-second commands commented out (Q38–Q39). The default-precondition code’s dynamic-array branch assumes the next parameter is length and adds `length > 0 && length < 100` (Q40). Those bounds are stronger than memory safety alone for arbitrary safe arrays. Neither observation identifies what the historical experiments used. The default function config enables `use_db` and disables `think`; the loop config disables both. These snapshot switches do not prove retrieval was used in reported runs (Q42–Q43). The exact experimental revision/configuration is not pinned by the paper.  The contribution to the requested topic is direct evidence for LLM-assisted specification writing: provide symbolic state and typed annotation templates, separate validity from goal strength, and use verifier feedback to refine candidates. It supplies numerical and pointer/data-structure benchmarks and a masked-goal evaluation. There is no distributed protocol, Verus, verified Rust or TLA+ experiment here. Transfer to agents writing distributed-system specifications or proofs is a reader inference: retain the assumptions, the exact verifier obligation, the translation boundary, and independent strength checks; do not equate successful annotation generation with a complete system proof.  Read every line of the 1202-newline layout extraction, all 27 PDF pages, all 38 references, three tables and six figures. Viewed nine contact sheets and fullsize pages 5, 15, 17, 19, 20 and 21. The local and official v4 PDFs are byte-identical. The bundle preserves literal excerpts with page/line/character anchors, all table/plot values, raw hashes and reader-derived discrepancies. Only reader extraction, rendering, hashing, arithmetic and validation scripts were run. No author code was executed or imported, and no compiler, verifier, solver or prover was run. All experimental outcomes remain author-reported, statically inspected results.
    - limits: C/ACSL, QCP symbolic execution, Frama-C/WP/Z3 and Typed memory semantics, generated or designer-supplied preconditions. Exact strongest postconditions apply to loop-free segments; LLM loop abstractions are checked for validity and optional goal sufficiency. No complete functional-summary, termination, translator-soundness or end-to-end Coq theorem. Nested loops lack the outer-loop template guidance; recursive/library/system calls unsupported. Reporting inconsistencies and current artifact differences are retained.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- INVWEAVER (2026)
    - title: INVWEAVER: Deductive Feedback for Invariant Synthesis in Interacting-Loop Programs
    - what: Presents INVWEAVER, a neuro-symbolic loop-invariant synthesizer for nested and sequential loops. A loop-level call graph exposes dependencies; obligation-guided LLM prompts and WP-derived targets drive iterative repair with delayed filtering. Defines state-transition and verification-dependency edges in the LCG, top-down/boundary-inward scheduling, failure diagnosis scopes, and a cycle-length delayed-filtering rule. Bubble-sort case derives missing inner invariants from outer WP obligations. Evaluates 67 prior plus 85 CLRS-Alg programs, comparing seven systems and ablations.

- Lean4Agent FormalAgentLib and LeanEvolve (2026)
    - title: Lean4Agent: Formal Modeling and Verification for Agent Workflow and Trajectory
    - what: Presents Lean4Agent, a Lean4 dependent-type framework with FormalAgentLib for structural, semantic and execution-trajectory verification of LLM-agent workflows, plus LeanEvolve for verification-guided workflow refinement. Workflows are typed heterogeneous graphs of read/write variables, natural-language instructions and execution types; trajectories record pre/post states and reasoning/tool traces. Layer 1 checks node reachability, edge validity and read/write consistency. Layer 2 translates explicit and implicit variables into decidable predicates and graph-level information-flow/context predicates, proving precondition entailment under an LLMExec local-correctness assumption. Layer 3 checks concrete trajectories using Lean propositions, Python validators and LLM-as-judge, localizing failed steps. LeanEvolve feeds diagnostics and optional test feedback to an LLM for targeted workflow edits, with a pure-LLM exploratory mode. Experiments generate 40 workflows, compare verification-passing/failing workflows across five LLMs on 50 hard SWE-Bench-Verified tasks and 100 ELAIP-Bench questions, and study graph-predicate and pure-LLM ablations.
    - number: Layer-2 passing workflows improve SWE task rate by 14.80 percentage points (95% CI 10.00–19.60)
    - number: ELAIP passing workflows improve by 9.07 points (95% CI 5.66–13.07)
    - number: LeanEvolve adds 7.47 points on SWE, raising 56.93% to 64.40%
    - number: formal-guided evolution solves 7.00 points more initially failed ELAIP cases than pure-LLM evolve
    - limits: Layer-2 soundness depends on LLMExec: each step satisfying predicates is assumed to produce postconditions; this abstracts away failures of long-horizon black-box LLM behavior. Custom predicates may have only name existence and natural-language explanations, and trajectory checks can rely on an LLM judge. Evaluation uses small random subsets (50 SWE and 100 ELAIP), 40 generated workflows and contemporary model versions, so generalization and causal effects are limited. The paper is a preprint and reports no mechanized verification of the LLM internals or distributed-system protocols.

- LIFT loop-bound termination verification (2026)
    - title: LLM-Guided Loop Bound Generation for Program Termination Verification
    - what: Introduces LIFT, a guess-and-check termination framework in which LLMs generate conjunctive or lexicographic loop bounds and a formal validator proves them or refutes them. A candidate bound b(V) is converted to a safety query by adding a counter initialized with b and asserting cnt>0 at each loop head. Constant bounds use bounded unrolling; symbolic bounds use ICE-DT or LLM invariant learners plus Boogie/SMT. If invariant proving fails, bounded violation analysis searches for a reachable counterexample state and feeds it back, while timeout and syntax diagnostics steer later prompts. Progressive generation starts with simple conjunctive bounds and escalates to lexicographic tuples; constant-offset correction and k-induction are included.
    - number: 171 benchmarks
    - number: 152 solved with Gemini
    - number: 139 solved with DeepSeek
    - number: 19 unsolved
    - limits: A valid over-approximate loop bound implies termination, but bounded violation analysis can miss violations after depth m and then treats the witness as potentially spurious. The framework is presented for one loop and says it generalizes to multiple loops. It relies on LLM inference, ICE-DT invariant quality, Boogie/SMT arithmetic, fixed iteration/time budgets, and syntactic restrictions excluding division/nonlinear operators. Nineteen of 171 benchmarks remain unsolved: ten lacked precise bounds and nine exceeded validator capability.

Uses 355 retained programs from the 506-file CASP dataset, evaluates EVA preprocessing and hundreds of WP runs per source/solver, and reports proof success, solver timeouts and Qed time. It finds rule-based and RTE annotations more reliable; DeepSeek is the strongest LLM but remains variable and can produce weak contracts.

- LORIS loop invariant synthesis (2026)
    - title: Guiding LLM-based Loop Invariant Synthesis via Feedback on Local Reasoning Errors
    - what: LORIS iteratively synthesizes C loop invariants: an LLM proposes an invariant, writes a natural-language proof of failed verification conditions, another LLM translates local steps into first-order implications, and Z3 checks those implications to produce targeted feedback. The framework checks local reasoning steps rather than demanding a fully formal proof script, maps an invalid implication back to a specific natural-language error, and feeds that diagnosis into subsequent invariant synthesis. It integrates Frama-C for inductiveness, Z3 for implication validity, and evaluates multiple model families against LaM4Inv and Clause2Inv, including a BMC combination study and manual auto-formalization checks.
    - number: GPT-4.1 LORIS solves 445/460 main programs with 93.1% success, versus 84.6% and 84.9% baseline success rates
    - number: best reported non-linear result is 47/50 programs; GPT-4.1 LORIS solves 45/50 at 83.2% success
    - number: five runs produce 2,300 attempts on the main suite; per-run cap is 150,000 tokens and 600 seconds
    - number: successful GPT-4.1 run costs about $0.038 on average; sampled auto-formalization evaluates 108 proof steps
    - limits: Results use single-loop, single-function C programs filtered from established suites; floating-point cases unsupported by the chosen Frama-C version were removed. Each method receives five independent runs, a 600-second limit, and 150,000 tokens per run; success means the verifier accepts the generated invariant. Feedback quality depends on LLM formalization of reasoning steps and solver-supported first-order implications, so it does not establish correctness of arbitrary natural-language reasoning outside the checked obligations.

- MALICE: Memory-aware Loop Invariants Generation on Symbolic Execution Traces (2026)
    - what: A proposal-and-check framework generates separation-logic loop invariants for C memory-manipulating programs from bounded symbolic execution traces. Three prediction stages choose heap predicates, abstract spatial memory regions and infer pure constraints; a two-step explanation/correction refinement loop uses QCP feedback. Authors report82.69% ICL and86.15% SFT pass@8 on260 LIG-MM+ programs. Separates read/write-permission and region planning from heap abstraction and scalar constraints. Two alternative spatial strategies either fold one deeper unrolled assertion or align multiple states to infer a common predicate shape; the reported solved set is their union. QCP supplies detailed pre-failure states, error locations and symbolic histories. A postorder loop-dependency traversal resolves sequential loops in order and inner loops before outer ones. Successful refinement trajectories yield9698 explanation/correction examples for an8B LoRA refiner with program-grouped fivefold cross-validation. A long array_max example reveals the exact memory-safety specification and both useful trace information and limits of automated diagnosis.
    - limits: Correctness relies on QCP semantics, symbolic execution, entailment checking and supplied predicate/specification definitions. The paper invokes standard partial-correctness Hoare rules; no new soundness/completeness theorem, termination proof or successful-generation guarantee is supplied. Unrolling depth4 provides examples for proposing invariants, not a proof of induction over unbounded executions. Pure properties retained after all observed branch-specific states must still pass full QCP initialization, preservation and exit obligations. The method assumes annotated C tasks, pre/postconditions and a supplied library of separation-logic predicates such as data_at,listrep,lseg,array shapes. It selects/folds these predicates; it does not generally synthesize arbitrary recursive predicate definitions or discover intended requirements. LIG-MM+ has133 doubly-linked-list,96 singly-linked-list,8 array and23 other(tree/hash-table) programs,260 total,reported average35 lines. At least one loop and data structure per task; authors mention several two-loop cases but give no count or broad nested-loop stress test. Main pass@8 result takes the union of solved sets from two spatial strategies, with three prediction interactions and up to10 refinement iterations per run. The relationship between strategy union and the stated call budget is not fully specified; token/cost/latency equivalence across methods is not established. SFT changes the refiner to Llama3.1-8B but retains DeepSeek-V3.2 for prediction. Full-8B uses Qwen3-8B prediction plus distilled Llama refiner; inference uses only8B models, while training-data generation still used the large teacher. Fivefold splitting groups all trajectories from each program, preventing direct trajectory sharing across train/test folds. The paper does not establish project/family-level disjointness, deduplication of near-identical programs or absence of base-model contamination. AutoSpec is a numerical tool and obtains0 here; LIG-SE/SLING lack array support. The comparison measures tool applicability plus invariant generation on this benchmark; it does not isolate framework effects under identical models/predicate coverage. Same-backbone Vanilla and Full-8B/budget comparisons add narrower controls. No repeated-run confidence intervals, significance tests, total API costs, full per-category results or independent artifact replay are supplied. Broad deployment to larger C systems, concurrency, distributed protocols, Verus/Rust and temporal liveness is untested.

- MathlibLemma: Folklore Lemma Generation and Benchmark for Formal Mathematics (2026)
    - what: Introduces MATHLIBLEMMA, a four-stage LLM pipeline for mining reusable folklore lemmas missing from Mathlib. Discovery proposes statements from Mathlib seed files; an LLM judge filters mathematical plausibility; a Lean-connected formalizer repairs syntax and type errors; a prover generates proofs with Lean checking and a proof-bypass screen. The resulting benchmark contains 4,028 non-trivial type-checked statements, and the released repository contains 1,506 Lean-checked generated proofs. Across seven model/settings configurations, union Success@2 is37.39% while the best individual GPT-Reasoning model reaches19.39%; a small pilot curated six outputs for Mathlib, with three merged. Formalizes each target as a pair(S,P) in seed context C⊂L with semantic novelty, mathematical plausibility and syntactic validity requirements. Discovery is prompted to generate at least 100 candidate lemmas per seed with sorry; Judge ignores Lean syntax and returns correct/wrong; Formalizer uses Kimina Lean server diagnostics for up to10 syntax-repair trials; Prover allows an initial attempt plus two repair rounds and rejects sorry/admit/sorryAx and model-added axiom/constant/opaque declarations. Benchmark seeds 109 Mathlib source files, manually stratified into Foundational, Applied and Abstract domains; aesop-immediate instances are excluded. Main evaluation is closed-book, sequential Success@2, with full context C supplied but no retrieval. Appendix D audits 138 model-unsolved statements (107 human-proved,78%); held-out audit proves79/94 (84.0%). Appendix E documents three merged lemmas: Gronwall-bound monotonicity, restriction of a constant kernel, and almost-everywhere congruence of central moments.
    - limits: LLM Judge filtering is imperfect: in a 53-rejected-candidate audit,54% were false and46% valid false negatives. Judge acceptance is not a proof of truth; kernel checking establishes well-formedness, and proof checking establishes the supplied theorem, not preservation of the intended informal idea. The benchmark is generated by GPT-5.1 discovery/judging/repair and is explicitly a versioned pipeline distribution, not a model-independent census. Discovery is seed-file local and one-shot, with no repository-wide retrieval, dependency traversal or proof-attempt-driven gap discovery. The 78% residual audit samples model-unsolved instances and lets human experts use full Mathlib, AI assistance and external search. It estimates provability of that stratified sample, not the full 4,028 benchmark. The 84.0% held-out audit uses a different five-seed design and is not pooled. Closed-book Success@2 measures internalized library knowledge plus reasoning under a serial two-repair budget. It is not directly comparable to parallel Pass@k; the 8B Pass@16 appendix is explicitly a separate stress test. The proof-bypass screen excludes common incomplete or assumption-injection artifacts but does not by itself establish that a lemma is novel, well named, minimally stated or suitable for Mathlib. Authors state novelty filters are mainly syntactic and semantically equivalent restatements may remain. All Lean checks use Lean v4.25.0-rc2 and a fixed Mathlib revision through a stateless server; results may change as APIs and typeclass elaboration evolve. The reader did not rerun this environment. Union performance is an oracle union over model outputs, not one executable ensemble with a stated orchestration cost. Model comparisons use different architectures and inference settings; domain interpretations are author claims. Three of six curated submissions merged, one was rejected as redundant and two were not pursued. This is a small selection signal, not a 1,506-proof upstream acceptance rate. Costs exclude curation, local open-model serving and maintainer review. Human audits can prove statements with open-book tools, while models receive no retrieval; the human/model gap therefore mixes proof ability with access to library search and assistance.

    - title: MINI F2F-DAFNY: LLM-Guided Mathematical Theorem Proving via Auto-Active Verification
    - what: Translates the miniF2F mathematical benchmark into Dafny and measures how SMT automation plus LLM-generated proof hints perform in an auto-active verifier. The benchmark has 244 test and 244 validation lemmas with compact definitions.dfy and library.dfy support. Dafny translates Hoare-style specifications through Boogie weakest preconditions to Z3; ghost assertions, calc chains and lemmas guide opaque solver search. The authors validate that generated solutions preserve requires/ensures, reject assume or axiom cheating, and audit the trusted support layer: 128 of 160 originally axiomatized lemmas are proved, 14 selected lemmas are independently solved, and 142 of 201 empty-proof successes remain without definitions.dfy. LLM attempts receive up to four trajectories and three error-correction rounds.
    - number: 488 total problems

    - limits: The translation is human-led and the mathematical support is an intentionally small axiomatized library rather than a foundational Mathlib formalization; opaque transcendental and number-theoretic properties remain trusted. Results use Dafny 4.11/Z3, 30-second attempts, eight off-the-shelf models, and no fine-tuning. Verification is brittle to assertion order/calc organization; models often confuse Dafny syntax with Lean and fail when required facts are absent. Pass rates measure generated Dafny code accepted by the validator, not independent proof objects.

- Neuro-formal verification (2026)
    - title: Neuro-Formal Verification: Agentic Language-Agnostic Formal Program Reasoning
    - what: Proposes NFV: an AI agent translates mainstream-language source, property and environment assumptions into staged formal artifacts, then uses Dafny or CBMC to produce machine-checked proofs/refutations. Goal-blind stages (formalize model, property and environment separately before proof search) reduce proving a mismatched artifact, while admitting formalization itself is not soundly guaranteed. The pipeline distinguishes verification and refutation arms, performs artifact audits and abstains when formalization/type checks fail. A balanced nl2post-safe corpus has 206 program/spec pairs (103 correct, 103 mutants), with library tiers L0–L3. Dafny handles proof/refutation; CBMC translates libraries to C for bounded counterexamples. Error analysis attributes wrong verdicts to missing or stronger preconditions and library formalization.

    - number: CBMC refuted 65/103 buggy, 7 false: 63% recall, 90% precision
    - limits: NFV optimizes empirical accuracy rather than end-to-end soundness because informal environment assumptions cannot be fully guaranteed. Results assume docstrings capture intended properties; unstated preconditions caused all six false refutations. Dafny modeling of libraries and Python semantics is incomplete; CBMC is bounded and checks C translation rather than original Python.

- OptProver: Bridging Olympiad and Optimization through Continual Training in Formal Theorem Proving (2026)
    - what: OptProver adapts BFS-Prover-V2-7B from Olympiad mathematics to Lean optimization via successful-search expert iteration and preference training. OptBench contains 400 Optlib-derived goals. Reported full-method success rises from base 13.25/32.00% to 37.75/55.25% under step prover Pass@1/32. The evidence is finite proof-search evaluation and ablations, not a theorem guaranteeing domain transfer or absence of forgetting. Separates tactic validity from budget-conditioned usefulness. Successful proof-path actions win over sibling actions that error or consume the search budget without closing a proof; locally valid but unresolved actions are not labeled mathematically invalid. Perplexity-weighted DPO replaces sequence log scores with clipped inverse-reference-perplexity weighted token averages; PW-UAPO combines this with utility-aware pairs. Expert iteration mixes Mathlib self-play, LLM-formalized Convex Analysis/Real Analysis and nearly 7k DeepTheorem analysis/linear-algebra problems. Detailed cases show optimization definitions unfolded before arithmetic, reuse of existing library lemmas, an ADMM inequality proof and extended-real edge cases.
    - limits: Lean acceptance establishes the formal goal relative to imported definitions, axioms and implementation. It does not establish faithful autoformalization of textbook/DeepTheorem prose or optimization software correctness. No statement-faithfulness audit, axiom audit or explicit sorry/admission bypass filter is documented in the supplied paper; the reader did not run the artifact. Utility labels depend on the current policy, sibling sampling, search allocation and stopping budget. Unresolved branches may be provable and may become useful under another policy. No theorem establishes the pairwise preference as optimal or a state-value oracle. OptBench has 121 basic, 135 convex and 144 algorithm-analysis goals; algorithmic goals are extracted partial proof obligations, not independent end-to-end algorithm or implementation verification. The benchmark is optimization mathematics in a fixed Lean 4.10.0/LeanDojo 2.1.3 environment. Whole-proof baselines use 32/256 generated proofs; step provers use accumulative 1/32 search budgets. These labels are not equal tactic counts, wall time or compute. No absolute node/time/token budget, hardware cost comparison, per-problem timeout or statistical intervals are supplied. Training includes large-scale Mathlib and Optlib-related mathematical material. No explicit theorem/dependency deduplication, benchmark exclusion rule, base-pretraining contamination audit or held-out construction protocol is reported; do not infer a leakage-free novel-theorem result. MiniF2F parity and ProofNet gains are finite evidence on two benchmarks. They do not establish universal retention, robustness or domain-transfer ability. No repeated-seed uncertainty or causal mechanism study is provided. Perplexity is a model-likelihood statistic, not semantic correctness. Poorer naive-SFT performance supports a failure case for the tested training recipe; it does not establish that textbook data or perplexity filtering generally harms proving. PW-DPO uses normalized weighted token log scores; these need not define a normalized sequence probability distribution. Its numerical-stability and anti-forgetting explanations are author interpretations, without a convergence, gradient-variance or stability proof. No distributed protocols, Verus, verified Rust, TLA+ or temporal/concurrent semantics are evaluated. Search-budget labels and domain-adaptation methods may inform proof-agent design; transfer to software verification remains a reader hypothesis.

- PPDL: LLM-Based Flows as Probabilistic Programs (2026)
    - what: PPDL extends declarative prompt language PDL with factor scores and a probabilistic execution model for multi-call LLM/tool flows. A weighted sampler accumulates messages and log scores; importance sampling(IS) and sequential Monte Carlo(SMC) approximate a weighted output distribution;majority voting is the uniform-weight baseline that ignores factor scores. Experiments cover math,code,fact checking and a Rocq proof/repair agent. Provides written operational semantics and empirical results,not a machine-checked implementation theorem or a guarantee that judge scores reflect semantic correctness. Kernel blocks contain definitions,body,parser,context-contribution flag and result variable. LLM calls sample conditioned on accumulated pdl context; factor adds to pdl score,with −∞ expressing a hard rejection. Functions preserve local environments while propagating context and score. Ideal semantics takes infinitely many traces and normalizes exp(score); IS uses finite complete traces,majority voting uniform weights. SMC resamples program/environment states at factor boundaries and resets scores after resampling. Threads parallelize particles; futures and lazy functional structures parallelize independent calls within a trace. Full appendix reduction rules cover data/code/model calls,sequences,conditionals,loops,functions,expressions and parsing. Broad evaluation uses the same flows with different engines; factors combine model judges,syntax checks and flake8. MiniF2F-Rocq agent generates whole proofs,checks them,feeds errors back and resamples failed branches; a final verify_proof call compares against the named formal statement. Figure24 applies a constant−1 factor per Error event,while prose describes scoring by number of errors; this is an implementation-description ambiguity rather than evidence of a richer error-count score. Agent success stops all particles. Fixed-budget results favor wider searches but reduce sequential repairs from up to74 at IS@1 to9 at SMC@40.
    - number: {'supplied_PDF_pages': 26, 'models_broad': 6, 'benchmarks_broad': 5, 'dataset_cap': 500, 'particles_broad': 5, 'runs': 3, 'MiniF2F_Rocq_tasks': 244, 'Rocq_token_budget': 1000000, 'IS1_solved_mean': 72.7, 'IS40_solved_mean': 87.7, 'SMC40_solved_mean': 95.0}
    - limits: Factors may be neither sound nor complete. An output distribution expresses weighted constraints and sampling,not a calibrated probability of correctness. Authors identify factor calibration as future work. Ideal infinite-trace semantics is intractable. Finite IS/SMC approximations depend on particle number and scoring; no convergence or implementation-correctness theorem is proved in the supplied paper. Normalization presupposes usable total weight; zero-weight/divergent execution cases are not developed as a metatheory. AppendixC assumes expression and external-code evaluation do not have side effects. Arbitrary Python/Shell/Jinja tools and external LLM responses are otherwise modeled abstractly. General evaluation uses six historical model releases,temperature0.8,5 particles,three runs and dataset cap500. LiveCodeBenchv6 uses2024-08-01 through2025-05-01. Claimed minimal MiniF2F-Rocq contamination risk is an author assessment,not demonstrated absence of training overlap. Rocq study uses only gpt-oss-120b,temp1 and1M prompt+completion tokens per exercise. Authors call the prover a perfect verifier in the pass@k comparison; this reading does not audit Checker,admissions/axioms,statement matching or trusted runtime. Formal-language acceptance is distinct from validating the informal theorem translation. No Verus/Rust/TLA+ or distributed-system verification case. No state-of-the-art performance comparison is intended. GitHub icml-26 branch and rocq-ml-toolbox are supplied pointers,not inspected/rechecked artifacts. All authors except Guillaume Baudart are employed by IBM,which leads Granite development; Granite models are included. The supplied paper has no declaration that an LLM wrote its proofs or implementation.

    - title: ProofWala: A Framework for Multilingual Proof Data Synthesis and Theorem-Proving
    - what: Provides itp-interface and ProofWala, a shared infrastructure for extracting and executing Lean/Rocq tactics, training proof-step models, and searching cloned proof environments in parallel. Controlled 220M-parameter model experiments find significant multilingual gains on Mathlib and CategoryTheory adaptation, with smaller nonsignificant pass@5 gains on CompCert, MathComp and GeoCoq. Sections 2–3 formulate proving as transitions between finite sets of obligations, implement Lean instrumentation inside the elaborator, export declaration/dependency graphs to SQLite, and retain reusable environment pools keyed by current proof state. Beam-search pseudocode generates, concurrently executes, and prunes tactic candidates; annotated trees contain only assistant-accepted transitions. Sections 4–5 document theorem-level corpus splits, identical prompt serialization without assistant/repository labels, fixed-step CodeT5 training, pass@1–5 and paired bootstrap tests, analogous Lean/Rocq category-theory examples, search-tree size/degree and tactic-length/runtime tables, MiniF2F transfer and CPU scaling. Section 3.1 explains why preliminary Isabelle/PISA integration is too expensive to scale.

Lean support is reported as 4.15.0–4.24.0 with a compatibility layer; detailed Rocq backend support is 8.10–8.18. CompCert/Mathlib reuse prior splits; other corpora use theorem splits, so structurally analogous theorems across languages can remain in training (explicitly analyzed in Table 4). No stronger contamination audit is demonstrated. Search uses 600-second timeouts, 1200 seconds for GeoCoq. Isabelle support is preliminary and requires tens of gigabytes per PISA instance. Gains outside Lean and adapted CategoryTheory are not statistically significant; multilingual MathComp and GeoCoq pass@1 are lower than monolingual. Proof acceptance inherits assistant correctness; this paper does not prove a distributed program or generate Verus/TLA+ specifications.

- PSV-Verus (2026)
    - title: Propose, Solve, Verify: Self-Play Through Formal Verification
    - what: Introduces PSV, an iterative proposer/solver loop for verified Rust/Verus code. At each iteration the solver samples k_trn candidates per specification, Verus supplies binary correctness outcomes, rejection fine-tuning trains on at most one verified solution per problem, and a proposer generates B new specifications conditioned on solver-estimated difficulty classes. Specifications are parsed, deduplicated and body-ignored checked before solving. The paper explains why RFT is preferred to advantage-weighted RL when Verus is sound but incomplete, and evaluates transfer learning and test-time training on Dafny2Verus, MBPP-Verified and HumanEval-Verified. Ablations isolate solution verification, difficulty-aware proposal and prompt diversity; scaling studies vary question budget and iterations.
    - number: Main Table 1 (mean over 5 seeds): test-time Dafny2Verus pass@1 65.63% vs AlphaVerus 24.06% and RFT 34.46%; MBPP 36.78% vs 6.48% and 3.83%; HumanEval 19.07% vs 7.24% and 5.56%. Transfer pass@1 is MBPP 25.25% and HumanEval 16.18%. PSV averages 48.12% across benchmarks/metrics vs 25.43% RFT and 25.55% AlphaVerus. Scaling from 4k to 32k questions raises Dafny pass@1 59.5→74.3%; MBPP 1k→32k raises 22.3→44.3%; HumanEval 13.9→26.3%. Fixed-budget iteration increases Dafny pass@1 21.0→42.3% at 1k. Removing verification drops pass@1 by 51.5% Dafny, 55.2% MBPP and 52.1% HumanEval relative; -Difficulty averages −5.6%, -Diversity −10.6%. Only 47.7% of generated specs are valid on average without spec verification, causing 2.1× inference compute.
    - limits: The key guarantee is conditional on Verus soundness: accepted code satisfies the formal specification. Verus is incomplete, so rejected correct solutions can be mislabeled and advantage-weighted RL is avoided. Formal specifications may themselves be invalid or underspecified; spec filtering is syntactic/body-ignored and generated questions need not match human intent. Benchmarks are translated/verified datasets, use a 3B Qwen model, five random seeds and roughly 24 hours on one 8×L40 machine; human-written solutions are not trained. Difficulty prompting only partially controls difficulty (target means 0.55 easy, 0.45 medium, 0.36 hard with substantial overlap). Generalization beyond Verus or sound verifiers remains future work.

    - what: Quokka directly validates LLM-generated loop invariants and retains only invariants that help a verifier prove assertions, avoiding unsound dynamic-analysis labels or heavy symbolic post-processing. The benchmark filters 866 SV-COMP loop problems and trains on 3,589 synthetic instances. UAutomizer is the baseline; end-to-end time includes inference. Quokka analyzes timeout failures: many outputs duplicate assertions, are trivial bounds, or are correct but too weak. LoRA fine-tuning and Best-of-N improve performance, while comparisons show gains over LEMUR, Clause2Inv, LaM4Inv and Loopy.

- Rocq out-of-order multiprocessor refinement (2026)
    - title: Formal Verification of an Out-of-Order Multiprocessor against an In-Order Weak-Memory ISA
    - what: Mechanizes an unbounded refinement proof that a multicore out-of-order RISC-V implementation with weak-memory behavior refines an in-order ISA, using a clean core specification to eliminate excess executions and then serializing memory/inter-core behavior. The proof decomposes n·Coreᵢ+ShMem⊆ISA into core refinement Coreᵢ⊑Coreₛ and system inclusion n·Coreₛ+ShMem⊆ISA. Coreₛ stores instructions in a single program-order list, allowing invalid excess instructions to be dropped; lightweight option-lifting encodes pure method preconditions without changing the refinement metatheory. Rocq mechanizes module refinements, arbitrary traces, and arbitrary design parameters.
    - number: end-to-end refinement is fully mechanized in 64k Rocq LOC
    - number: Theorem 1 holds for all instantiations and arbitrary execution lengths
    - number: system inclusion proof by LLM agents took nearly three weeks; core refinement nearly one week
    - number: comparison table marks the present work as multicore, out-of-order, and unbounded, unlike cited prior cases
    - limits: The design is a single-issue out-of-order RV32I core with LR/SC, an axiomatized coherent shared memory, and promising/RISC-V memory semantics. The precondition encoding is deliberately limited to pure predicates, and the proof abstracts implementation details through specified modules. LLM-written proofs remain trusted only after Rocq kernel checking; performance or silicon behavior is outside the formal theorem.

    - title: S CHWARZ: Solver-Aware Agentic Program Verification
    - what: Presents S CHWARZ, a trusted harness that turns coarse SMT verification failures into obligation-local repair using snapshots, checked SMT-local lemmas and theory-aware solver policies, evaluated on C and Rust/Verus tasks. The harness separates an untrusted agent that proposes specifications, decompositions and counterexamples from a checker that regenerates obligations, invokes configured solvers and validates unsafe witnesses by concrete replay. Program-point snapshots expose checked facts; local lemmas must themselves be entailed before use; theory policies select numeric, quantified, memory and floating-point encodings. The paper reports detailed ablations: removing local lemmas drops aggregate pass rate from 92.7% to 77.7%, removing policies to 88.2%, and removing both to 73.3%. Capability analysis identifies strong integer/recursion/control-flow performance and weaker floating-point, library semantics, layout and long proof routes.

The C suite uses a curated local subset, and score comparisons aggregate heterogeneous tasks. Solver-aware direct SMT edits are diagnostic and must be lifted back into source-level artifacts before acceptance.

    - what: Retrospective human-led analysis of Stellar SDEX exchangeV10 uses LLM-generated PVS abstractions, interactive theorem proving and SeaBMC symbolic C++ harnesses to recover rounding invariants and test a supposedly unreachable exception. Authors report 37 of 38 lemmas/typechecker obligations proved, establishing P1–P3 in their model. The remaining P4 obligation leads to an executable local exception witness; reachability through upstream production callers is left unresolved. Records the actual human/LLM/prover division of work, the distinction between generating and discharging type obligations, the asymmetry of the one-percent bound, exact harness assumptions and concrete exception inputs. Code structure and substantial informal invariants are important favorable inputs. §3.1: humans copied full source to ChatGPT 5 Pro, fed PVS errors back and repeated with Claude Opus 4 with qualitatively similar results. Invalid DIV, misplaced IMPORTING and invented libraries needed repairs. Models originally represented exceptions as unused booleans. Humans split theories into three files and supplied interface preconditions. This is not autonomous proof generation. §3.1 footnote4: passing the initial typechecker phase means name resolution and sound generation of type-correctness conditions (TCCs), not their discharge. TCC generation itself is distinct from a complete checked proof. §3.2: the introduction says 16 generated lemmas and 22 generated proof obligations were proved, but the detailed account reports 37/38 and one failed P4 obligation. Retain the detailed denominator; do not claim all 38 obligations or absence of exceptions were established. §§2–3/Figs3–4: P1 favors the offer remaining in the book. P2 bounds relative rounding error by 1% for a single matched offer. P3 permits path-payment asymmetry: error favoring the wheat seller can be unbounded, whereas the sheep-favoring case remains bounded; non-path exchanges exceeding the bound are rejected. These claims depend on input bounds and formalized preconditions. §3.2/Fig5: PVS assert/grind use Shostak reasoning; Yices2 handles nonlinear arithmetic. Type subranges and explicit bounds matter. The displayed Price uses uint32 fields although the source comment says int32_t; uint32 includes zero while the shown posint64 obligations require 100*n>0 and 100*d>0. The schematic includes ellipses and does not show all premises, so it alone neither proves positivity nor establishes unsoundness of the full artifact. §3.3: C++ unit proofs encode PVS type constraints as assumptions, call the implementation and assert corresponding properties; SeaHorn lowers harness and source to LLVM for SeaBMC. These are parallel property checks and a sanity check on relevance. The paper supplies no general relational refinement/equivalence theorem covering the PVS abstraction and all C++ operational semantics. §3.3/Fig6: price n,d are nondeterministic signed 32-bit integers with n>0,d>0. Four maxima are signed 64-bit; assumptions constrain maxWheatSend>=0, maxSheepSend>0 and maxWheatReceive>0. No positivity assumption is shown for maxSheepReceive. The postcondition is !res.wheatStays || (res.numSheepSend>0 && res.numWheatReceived>=0). §3.3/Fig7: a counterexample confirms local exception behavior for the stated harness domain. Developers believe upstream code excludes it; that caller claim is not proved. A failed PVS proof alone would not establish a code bug, and the witness does not establish an exploitable end-to-end production vulnerability. Algorithm2: graphical line14 asserts round=PPS_SEND implies Ss≠0; extracted text loses the negation. Prose p.237 calls it line15, but printed line15 zeroes amounts. The function declaration omits several variables used by its body, so the pseudocode is schematic rather than a complete executable specification. §4/Fig8: revised prompt asks for total mathematical functions, explicit bounds and exceptions converted to preconditions/subtypes. It explicitly excludes full C++ semantics and asks only to state lemmas. Reader inference: strengthening a precondition can remove the failing behavior from the model; callers must separately establish it. No memory, exception or arithmetic semantic correspondence follows from well-typed PVS alone. §4: claims of low manual effort, transfer to larger codebases and useful continuous integration are qualitative. No attempt counts, token costs, elapsed human/prover/model-checker times, resource limits, unroll bounds, solver configurations, controlled comparison or repeated-run variance are reported. §4: exchangeV10 did not change during the exercise. Maintenance/evolution benefits and automatic harness/proof updates are proposed opportunities, not measured longitudinal outcomes. Conclusion still says specification finalization and broader coverage are ongoing. Trusted components include human interpretation, abstraction/precondition selection, PVS and its proof/solver integration, source-to-LLVM compilation, SeaBMC and harness construction. No independent artifact execution, proof replay or upstream-call analysis was performed for this record. No Verus, Rust or TLA+ result is reported.
    - how much was read: most of it read
