- Interleaving static analysis and LLM prompting with applications to error specification inference (2025)
    - what: Interleaves EESI abstract-interpretation analysis with LLM queries to infer function error return values in C. Analyzes bottom-up in the call graph, asks the LLM about unavailable library functions or functions left unknown by analysis, and propagates the results as new facts. Prompts combine error-code conventions, abstract-domain/format examples, and known callee specifications; missing-library queries receive available specification context. Recursive SCCs use a fixed point. Requeries for consistency, excludes known success values and results exceeding return ranges, and tracks the origin, dependency path and LLM-call count of inferred facts. Journal extension studies error propagation, infallibility classification and nondeterministic outcomes.
    - number: 6 C programs; 2–288 KLOC
    - number: recall 52.55% to 77.83%; F1 0.612 to 0.804
    - number: precision 86.67% to 85.12%
    - number: 973 GPT-4o-mini requests; reported $0.29
    - limits: Inferred specifications are heuristic facts, not certified contracts: EESI approximations and LLM hallucinations remain after filters. LLM provenance tracks all supplied context because actual causal contribution is unknown. Six C benchmarks use manually supplied knowledge; ground truth is exhaustive only for small benchmarks and sampled for larger ones. Main effectiveness results use GPT-4, whereas cost and repeated-run analyses use GPT-4o-mini, so those numbers cannot be combined as one configuration. Fallibility and incorrect background facts remain substantial error sources; inference-path length and number of LLM calls do not reliably identify errors. No downstream bug-finding or distributed-correctness guarantee is established. Source PDF prints the exact requested DOI.

- LLM backend formal verification (2025)
    - title: Towards Automated Formal Verification of Backend Systems with LLMs
    - what: Translates functional Scala backend code, APIs and database tables into pure Lean 4 dependent-type representations, generates API/table theorems from documentation and inferred behavior, then uses LLM proof search with Lean feedback. Failed positive theorems trigger negation proving to produce concrete bug witnesses; unresolved cases are sent to humans. The pipeline has implementation formalization, theorem generation and proof search stages. Monads become explicit inductive return values; tables become function inputs/outputs. API requirements preserve control-flow paths, while table properties summarize all APIs touching a table. Qwen-Max performs formalization/theorem generation and DeepSeek-R1 proof search. Experiments use four Scala projects with 2–25 APIs and injected bug variants; dependency/formalization and theorem quality are manually checked across three runs.
    - number: Four projects, 2–25 APIs each; 45 APIs total and >250 generated API/table theorems
    - number: Dependency analysis and table formalization: 100% accuracy; API formalization 96.2%
    - number: API theorem formalization 95.1%; table theorem formalization 86.5%
    - number: Proof search: 100/168 API theorems (59.5%) and 72/125 table theorems (57.6%) proved
    - limits: Correctness guarantees apply only to successfully proved Lean statements and faithful formalization/specifications; a failed proof may be model weakness or a real bug. Scala projects are AI-generated, then human verified, and only four systems are evaluated. Negation proving can detect a bug only when the negated theorem is expressible and provable. Pure-functional translation abstracts effects and external runtime behavior, limiting claims about arbitrary production backends.

    - title: Extracting Formal Specifications from Documents Using LLMs for Automated Testing

A two-stage annotation-then-conversion pipeline improves coverage and accuracy over direct end-to-end prompting. Experts cross-validated ground truth; prompts first mark requirement-bearing sentences and then convert annotations into temporal logic, reducing oversimplification and hallucinated clauses. The paper quantifies false positives and studies a DeepSTL non-LLM conversion baseline. Documents average 2,966 words; each model runs three times and unique outputs are aggregated.

    - number: Best false-positive rate 14.5%; each LLM run three times
    - limits: Ground truth may be incomplete or contain expert errors; temporal formulas require interpretation of informal documents. LLMs oversimplify dense requirements and fabricate plausible but unsupported conditions. Dataset is limited to three open-source autonomous-system projects and temporal logic; extraction quality does not prove implementation correctness.

- LLM human-readable PFV proofs (2025)
    - title: Towards LLM-based Generation of Human-Readable Proofs in Polynomial Formal Verification
    - what: Argues for using LLMs to draft human-readable explanations of polynomial formal-verification proofs, illustrated with induction and BDD size examples, while insisting that a formal reasoning engine must validate every generated claim. The paper places PFV in a resource-bounded setting for adders/multipliers and describes induction prompts for two-variable and three-variable monomial sums and totally symmetric Boolean functions, where BDD size is linear or quadratic under stated structures. It deliberately probes a multiplication-function answer: ChatGPT first gives a misleading variable-ordering claim, then the author identifies the complexity error, showing why LLM prose cannot be trusted as proof. Proposed future directions include retrieval-augmented proof context, chain-of-thought and feedback from formal tools, and application to modern data structures.
    - number: adders over 1,000 bits cited as PFV case
    - number: f2/f3 induction examples
    - number: O(n²) symmetric-function BDD bound
    - limits: This is an invited position paper with examples, not a validated LLM benchmark. ChatGPT 4o outputs can hallucinate and even the paper's displayed answer contains an empirically plausible but complexity-theoretically incorrect claim about multiplication BDDs; all results require BDD/PFV engine checking. Examples concern well-studied Boolean functions and do not establish general proof synthesis, scalability, or correctness of generated text. No runtime, success-rate or human-study measurements are reported.

- LLM plan verification with NuSMV (2025)
    - title: Bridging LLM Planning Agents and Formal Methods: A Case Study in Plan Verification
    - what: Introduces an LLM pipeline that translates natural-language plans into NuSMV Kripke structures and LTL properties, then uses model checking to classify plan validity on a simplified PlanBench task. The method maps facts to Boolean variables, natural-language initial conditions to init, action preconditions/effects to guarded transitions, and a stage variable to enforce action sequence; goals become LTL reachability properties such as F(goal). NuSMV bounded model checking labels generated models valid (SAT), invalid (UNSAT) or unknown (parse/model failure). A one-shot GPT-4o/GPT-5 comparison reports formal-verification metrics against direct LLM judgments and analyzes unknown handling, syntax versus semantic fidelity and risk-sensitive precision/recall.
    - number: GPT-5 one-shot: 95.89% accuracy, 99.44% precision, 93.34% recall, 96.30% F1, 6.87% unknown, 47.08 time units
    - number: GPT-4o one-shot: 52.06% accuracy, 59.19% precision, 45.54% recall, 51.48% F1, 34.61% unknown
    - number: direct GPT-5 baseline: 99.59% accuracy/F1 with no unknowns, illustrating comparison mismatch
    - number: direct GPT-4o baseline: 80.37% accuracy, 79.95% F1
    - limits: The benchmark is simplified and reduced to binary validity; it does not explain invalidity reasons or test broad software/circuit settings. Unknown outputs are excluded from headline metrics, so treating them differently changes apparent reliability. Syntactically valid models can still misrepresent plan intent and pass verification; one-shot prompting, GPT-5 low reasoning effort and small qualitative semantic analysis limit generalization.

- MA-LoT: Model-Collaboration Lean-based Long Chain-of-Thought Reasoning enhances Formal Theorem Proving (2025)
    - what: Presents MA-LoT, a Lean4 proof-generation and verifier-guided correction framework, and LoT-Transfer Learning (LoT-TL), a three-stage SFT procedure intended to transfer general long natural-language reasoning to Lean without annotated Lean long-reasoning traces. A LoT-Solver model serves prover and corrector roles under different prompts, generating whole proofs, checking them with Lean, and repairing failures. The Goedel-based system reports 61.07% MiniF2F-test success with 16 initial attempts plus two rounds of 8 repairs, versus 55.33% baseline at 32 samples; the cumulative 63.93% result pools outputs across experimental models/settings. Builds LoT-ProveData: 54,465 formal statements/proofs with generated natural-language statements and proof comments, using DeepSeek-Prover-v1 and LeanWorkbook proofs plus Qwen2.5-72B annotation. LoT-CorrectionData has 64,912 incorrect/correct proof pairs and error messages. Train on 126K OpenO1-SFT-Pro general reasoning examples, then LoT-PD with a WITHOUT Long CoT system prompt and placeholder thought, then correction pairs. At inference, switch the prompt to WITH Long CoT and seed a header requiring mathematical analysis, tactics and final Lean code. Correction embeds the failed proof and real Lean diagnostics into the thought context before requesting analysis and revision. Appendix F clarifies correction training suppresses error analysis but retains a WITH Long CoT header containing the failed draft/messages; this differs from the main text’s simpler description. No reinforcement learning is part of this training pipeline.
    - limits: Success is reported Lean verification of supplied mathematical formal statements, not verification of the generated natural-language reasoning or statement faithfulness. Prompts prohibit sorry, but the PDF does not give a full admissions/axiom/statement-integrity audit or exact Lean/Mathlib versions. The name model collaboration refers to separated proof-writing and correction roles. §2.3 explicitly uses LoT-Solver as the base model for both; the paper does not establish that different model weights or multiple independent epistemic agents are necessary. The WITHOUT/WITH prompt change is trained on general long-reasoning SFT and domain code/comment data. No specially annotated Lean long-reasoning dataset is needed, but substantial synthetic Lean data and Qwen annotations are still used; this is not a data-free method. Main benchmark is MiniF2F-valid/test,244 statements per split. Imports are manually configured. DeepSeek-Prover-v1.5 used the valid split for training according to the paper’s footnote, so valid results are not held-out comparisons for that baseline. Baselines differ in model size, sampling method, context and search budgets. Whole-proof runs generally use4096 context and128 samples, Goedel and Table1 DeepSeek-V3 use32; tree-search figures use other budgets. The authors exclude DeepSeek RMaxTS because its smallest disclosed3200 budget is incomparable. The authors could not use vLLM and report that baseline results differ from original papers. Training is about1 GPU-day and evaluation11 GPU-days on a4×H100-96G cluster as stated, without a detailed wall-clock/batch breakdown. The reported corrector advantage combines feedback placement, learned correction behavior and explicit thought prompts. Prover-as-corrector ablation puts draft/errors in code comments; it does not isolate weights or prove that role separation itself causes the gains. No repeated-run variation, confidence intervals or training-data contamination audit is supplied. Scores and illustrative cases do not establish general long-reasoning scaling laws or transfer to systems verification. ProofNet adds only a small Goedel-versus-MA-LoT comparison,15.47% at16+16 versus12.15% at32 independent samples, with no broader model or project study. The paper claims more insightful/structured proofs and emergent self-reflection. Success rates and selected traces supply evidence of task performance; there is no formal or blinded human metric validating insight, cognitive mechanism or truthfulness of thoughts.

- Position: Formal Mathematical Reasoning—A New Frontier in AI (2025)
    - what: A position paper arguing that formal mathematics should complement language-model reasoning to supply checked reasoning steps, automatic training feedback and a path toward verified software/hardware generation. It organizes research challenges around data scarcity, faithful autoformalization, proof search, abstraction, retrieval, specification trust and proof maintenance. Its main original contribution is an agenda with separate levels for autoformalization, theorem proving, verified natural-language reasoning and verified system generation; it supplies no new proving algorithm, experiment or formal theorem. The paper separates producing a proof of a fixed formal statement from ensuring that the statement captures human intent. It proposes collecting human repairs and synchronizing informal/formal pairs, using semantic rather than string-match autoformalization metrics, comparing provers under explicit hardware/time budgets, growing libraries through useful subgoals and abstractions, and evaluating agents on projects and change histories. Software verification receives a distinct treatment: bespoke datatypes, repetitive case splits, large evolving codebases and specification management differ from isolated mathematical theorem proving. It discusses an alternative neural-only view and identifies self-verification and easy-to-hard generalization as unresolved prerequisites rather than demonstrated impossibilities.
    - limits: This is an explicitly selective position paper, not a systematic survey or controlled comparison. The claim that formal reasoning is indispensable and forecasts of lower verification cost are author positions, not results established by experiments in this paper. Formal proof checking establishes the exact encoded claim relative to the proof system, its assumptions and trusted implementation. The paper itself makes fidelity of generated specifications a key open problem; checker acceptance alone does not establish developer intent or correctness of the translation. The milestone levels are proposed organizing categories, with no validated thresholds, measured reliability requirements, human-cost study or proof that capability rises in the stated order. Theorem-proving level 2 explicitly includes human-built tactics without machine learning. Numbers about NuminaMath, Proof Pile, AlphaProof and seL4 are cited contextual examples, not new measurements. IMO 2025 claims are explicitly attributed to blogs/X without further details, and no such external evidence was fetched in this reading. The discussion of current systems is tied to the supplied 2025 proceedings version. Its model capability and benchmark-availability claims are historical author assessments, not a current exhaustive inventory. Arguments that informal scaling alone cannot resolve advanced mathematics and that neural-only self-verification remains weak draw on prior work; no impossibility theorem or decisive experiment rules out future neural-only systems. Software, hardware and mathematical verification are discussed broadly. Verus and AutoVerus appear as examples, but Rust memory/concurrency semantics, TLA+ specifications, distributed protocols and their fault assumptions are not evaluated. Formalizing or solving selected substeps while interleaving natural language does not by itself certify every unformalized claim or the full natural-language conclusion. This boundary follows from which parts are actually checked, not a new end-to-end guarantee shown here.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    -
    - record id: `position-formal-mathematical-reasoning-2025`
- Position: Vibe Coding Needs Vibe Reasoning: Improving Vibe Coding with Formal Verification (2025)
    - what: Advocates a side-car system that maintains specifications during conversational code development, selects lightweight verification tools, feeds actionable results to the coding model, and lets developers review constraints. Distinguishes verifier-feedback filtering (Type I), postprocessing/constrained generation (Type II), and collaborative specification maintenance (Type III). A small TypeScript prototype hooks Claude Code changes and instantiates four templates: exhaustive switches, discriminated unions, shared union aliases, and satisfies Record guards. Toy order-status examples show missing shipped UI handling and missing cancelled processing.
    - number: 4 specification templates
    - number: 2 bugs in the order-status toy example
    - number: no quantitative evaluation
    - limits: This is a position paper with a small proof of concept, not a quantitative effectiveness study. Verification is currently syntactic checks and compiler acceptance; broader formal tools, provenance, and application-specific specification maintenance remain proposed work. Its claims about inevitable decay and general bug absence are arguments, not experimentally established guarantees. Compiler checks apply only to the chosen templates and modeled type constraints.

- ProofAug: Efficient Neural Theorem Proving via Fine-grained Proof Structure Analysis (2025)
    - what: Postprocesses whole-proof LLM proposals by retaining a compatible hierarchy of proof blocks, replacing failures with temporary admissions, filling holes with Isabelle automation, and collapsing enclosing blocks when automation fails. Optional efficient recursive proving requests completions at failed original conjectures. Reports miniF2F improvements with fixed deepseek-math-7b-base, multiple prompting strategies and changed benchmark statements, plus a small Lean implementation. Exact printed pseudocode omits proof-replacement and rollback-state updates; implementation results remain author reports. Records observable-state equality, maximal compatible semi-proof procedure, finite-time ATP fallback, original-conjecture-only ERP tree, query-versus-verifier accounting, prompt and context details, all mixed-strategy rows, benchmark subtraction/typo curation and author rechecking limitations. Concrete printed Algorithm2 trace exposes retained sorry admissions and uninitialized rollback states; distinguishes these presentation findings from the unchecked code/results.
    - limits: §§2.1–2.2, pp3–5: ITP modeled by parsed transitions and state; completion initially defined by finish=True. Actual equality compares only displayed proof-state string, mode, available facts, error and finish. Equality of these observations is not proved equivalent to full kernel/proof-object equality. An admission-free final check is needed because sorry closes a goal without constructing its proof. §3.1 Algorithm1,p5: maximal compatible semi-proof preserves exact original steps outside replaced by/proof...qed blocks. Execute proposal; on error in proof(prove), replace step by sorry and retry; otherwise collapse innermost block, restore recorded pre-block state; error outside any block returns Null. Blocks and parsing depend on Isar syntax; no correctness, uniqueness or completeness theorem for MCSP is proved. §3.2 Algorithm2,p6: try builtin heuristics/Sledgehammer at holes. Failure collapses enclosing block and retries at coarser granularity, eventually the theorem alone. This searches retained structural choices without retraining the model. Finite-time failure of these particular tactics does not establish that the intermediate goal is mathematically unprovable or that no compatible proof exists. §3.3,pp6–7: optional ERP requests completion with theorem/informal draft, already accepted prefix and goal state when ATP first fails. If raw completion fails, replace failed tactics by ATP and retry; compare resulting observable state with the sorry-generated next state. Only failed conjectures of original MCSP generate new nodes; descendants precede ancestors, otherwise earlier blocks first. This is a restricted completion search, not an unrestricted recursive repair of all new subgoals. §4.1/AppendixH,pp7/16/18: Isabelle DSP-version miniF2F-test has244 statements and accompanying informal drafts. Authors identify wrong statements and later use curated variant with typo changes and nat-to-int changes for subtraction. Different formal propositions can produce different success rates; curated66.0% cannot be read as improvement on identical original statements. Only still-unproved cases are rerun on curated data. Existing FAIR upstream also fixes some errors; paper points to PR18. §4.2,pp7–8: Isabelle2022/PISA; model deepseek-math-7b-base on RTX3090/vLLM;12PISAinstances on XeonGold6326@2.90GHz; proof-step timeout10s/Sledgehammer120s. Temperature0.6,top-p0.95,response cap2048 or4096−prompt tokens if prompt>2048. Few-shot examples randomly sampled; authors write full proofs matching informal demonstrations, default1shot. Zero-shot means no demonstrations and can still include informal statement/draft. §4.2/AppendixD,pp7/14: heuristic list plus Sledgehammer; paper calls it8methods but displayed list repeats auto and has more entries. Added mod_simps; remove force,presburger,linarith versus DSP. Sledgehammer candidatesCVC4,vampire,verit,e,spass,z3,zipperposition;30seach/120stotal. Author reports DSP code usedcvc5 and about35stotal, and fixes this. No AFP/AoFcompiled library due to disk space for12instances, so no Symmetric_Polynomials.Vieta import. These changes affect baseline comparisons. §4.2/AppendixD,pp8/14: checked semi-proofs cached across samples to avoid re-verifying; authors acknowledge caching can reduce success because ATP outcomes vary. CPU and timeout nondeterminism affect reproducibility. Authors say obtained proofs reverified and supplied, while some reconstructed smt/metis proofs time out. No independent replay, admission/axiom audit, or per-proof replay-success count was performed for this record. §§4.3–4.5,pp8–9: same-setting DSP baseline is strongest local comparison, but prompting content also changes from sketches to full proofs. Query budget counts model requests; it does not measure tokens, ATP calls, repeated proof checking, elapsed CPU/GPUtime or total cost. Cross-paper16384versus1400comparisons also change models, training and environments; they do not isolate an efficiency factor. Figure3 also compares augmentation on/off within DSP,full-proof and zero-shot prompting families and shows gains in each, providing controlled evidence beyond the combined prompt-and-method headline comparison. §4.5/AppendicesE–F,pp9/15/17: authors explore multiple prompt recipes and report union of successes. Three few-shot sets and two zero-shot instructions differ; draft-off excludes both informal statement and proof. A mixed cumulative rate is a portfolio outcome, not performance of one fixed recipe selected without seeing test outcomes. No confidence intervals or controlled total-compute comparison reported. AppendixC,pp13–14: Lean version uses block indentation/context inference with Aesop,omega and hand heuristic try norm_num[*];try field_simp[*]at*;try ring_nf at*;try nlinarith. Kimina-Prover-Preview-Distill-1.5B pass@1reported44.3→50.4%onminiF2F-test. Detailed Lean version/hardware/sampling controls are not supplied in this appendix. Extension to other procedural systems via declarative wrappers/training is a proposal, not evaluated across all ITPs. AppendixA/§5,pp9/12–13: related-work distinctions include bottom-up structural repair versus top-down POETRY/LEGO, Coq PALM backtracking, retrieval/premise automation and expert iteration. Claims that top-down prompting hurts intrinsic model ability are author explanations rather than a separately controlled causal experiment. Informal mathematics/software-engineering applications and online/expert-iteration integration remain future work.

- ProofSeek (2025)
    - title: Neural Theorem Proving: Generating and Structuring Proofs for Formal Verification
    - what: Presents a three-module framework that turns natural-language statements into formal Isabelle proofs: statement generation, LLM proof generation and heuristic proof structuring. SFT teaches syntactic Isabelle, RL rewards proofs checked by PISA, and recursive proof search uses ATPs, ERP and backtracking. The proof representation is a state-transition system; each generated step is checked by ATPs, with ERP repairing failures and heuristic tactics/backtracking reorganizing proof trees. The framework targets both miniF2F-test mathematics and AWS S3 bucket policy verification, and curates an FVELER-derived training dataset. The authors emphasize whole-proof generation plus structured subproof reuse.
    - number: Two-stage SFT then RL training

    - number: AWS S3 policy used as application case; detailed success tables are in §4 of extraction
    - limits: Formal correctness is relative to Isabelle/PISA/ATP checking and the formalized statement; natural-language translation can misrepresent program intent. RL and heuristic search depend on finite budgets and solver availability. The supplied experiments report benchmark results but do not establish coverage of arbitrary software policies.

    - what: A loop around three parts: a general reasoning LLM that writes an informal proof, a small prover model that turns it into Lean code, and Lean that checks it and returns errors. - ShieldAgent: Shielding Agents via Verifiable Safety Policy Reasoning (2025)
    - what: An LLM guardrail converts natural-language policies into logical rules, refines their predicates, clusters relevant rules by action, assigns observed predicate values through tools and generated code, then thresholds a learned probabilistic rule model. Reports improved classification and conditional online policy compliance for web agents. The paper calls its probability comparison a dynamic safety guarantee, but supplies no invariant theorem establishing it. Separates action constraints from background state rules; uses GPT-4o policy extraction, verifiability refinement and redundancy pruning; learns soft Markov-logic weights rather than requiring every rule to hold. Gives a hybrid history/workflow memory, specialized search/check tools, an action-circuit training algorithm, attack-generated data and a fully worked Reddit bio example. Appendices expose translation, predicate-binding and probability inconsistencies that formal checking alone does not repair.
    - limits: Logical checking is conditional on correct policy extraction, refinement, merging, action retrieval and assignment of observed facts. GPT-4o performs policy translation and optional human experts may correct rules. No equivalence proof establishes fidelity to original policies or completeness of retrieved constraints. Rules have finite learned weights, explicitly allowing tradeoffs to reduce false positives. A positive likelihood margin is not a requirement that every policy holds. Negative rule weights are not explicitly excluded. Classification accuracy is an empirical property of the learned rule model, not a universal enforcement guarantee. Equation 5 compares probabilities with one action bit flipped and state predicates fixed. The source supplies neither environment transition dynamics nor a proved inductive invariant/control-barrier condition linking that comparison to safe future trajectories. Current-state likelihood improvement does not by itself prove temporal safety. Predicates are grounded by learned search/binary-check models, moderation APIs, external sources and generated code. Observability and truthful, complete interaction history are required. Specialized operations use fine-tuned InternVL2-2B; formal tools include Stormpy in the main text and Prover9/Mace4 in the worked appendix. LTL over finite traces is defined in Appendix C.3, but most displayed rules and the worked code are propositional. The paper does not specify an end-to-end implementation or correctness argument for temporal monitoring, finite-trace termination, solver unknowns, missing observations or an inability to assign every predicate. The training recipe allows real labels or simulated pseudo-labels but does not give sufficient actual training/test splits, model hyperparameters, number of optimization steps, specialized-tool training data or random seeds to independently reproduce all reported scores. Algorithm 1 loops until all predicates are assigned without an explicit fallback budget. Human annotators manually label safety and violated policies. Annotator count, disagreement resolution, agreement rates and a detailed labeling protocol are absent. Policies include empirically constructed constraints as well as platform documents. Benchmark attacks are targeted to extracted policy violations. Existing benchmarks are augmented with policies and safe/unsafe trajectories. Scores concern those augmented guardrail tasks, not the original benchmarks unchanged. AWM is the protected web agent; results do not establish safety of arbitrary agents or environments.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- STP: Self-play LLM Theorem Provers with Iterative Conjecturing and Proving (2025)
    - what: STP trains one LLM as both conjecturer and whole-proof prover. The conjecturer proposes related statements conditioned on a proved seed and either an extracted lemma or a fixed trivial lemma; the prover attempts new conjectures and remaining dataset statements. Verified, barely provable and heuristically elegant conjectures train the conjecturer, while correct nontrivial proofs train the prover. This supplies denser training signals than repeatedly attempting hard unproved statements. Lean training reaches 28.5% cumulative LeanWorkbook coverage, and the final model reports 65.0% miniF2F-test and 23.9% ProofNet-test pass@3200, plus 8/644 PutnamBench solves. Three stages: supervised initialization from proof libraries, iterative self-play, then final retraining from the base model on SFT data and selected generated proofs. Conjecturer SFT uses ordered shared-lemma/theoremX/theoremY tuples; self-play selects verified seed proofs, deduplicates statement–lemma pairs, caps frequent lemmas at 0.1 n and sometimes inserts a trivial lemma. K=32 proof samples estimate conjecture difficulty; keep empirical success in(0,1/4], require the seed lemma in a successful proof, deduplicate conjectures, and discard the bottom 20% of minimum-proof-length/statement-length elegance scores. Reweight conjectures toward unproved statements using mean-last-layer embeddings and negative cosine transport cost. Prover training keeps correct proofs from tasks with success below 1/2, uses a three-iteration replay buffer, inverse proof-count weights, length discount and Lean verification-time discount. Periodic refresh and final retraining counter forgetting/distribution shift; final data keeps at most 16 distinct proofs per task with empirical success≤1/4.
    - limits: Correctness is relative to the formal statements, loaded libraries and verifier configuration. Generated conjecture proofs are reported checker-accepted, but semantic equivalence to a natural-language problem and mathematical usefulness are separate. No artifact/checker execution or admissions/axiom audit was performed here. The barely-provable criterion uses 32 sampled proofs, not exact underlying success probabilities. Elegance is a textual length ratio; relevance/diversity uses shared lemmas and embedding proximity. These are heuristics, not guarantees of novelty, difficulty or scientific value. Lean uses DeepSeek-Prover-V 1.5-SFT already trained on public/proprietary proof data including LeanWorkbook, miniF2F-valid and ProofNet-valid. The 24-iteration ablation without valid-set STP training still inherits those base-model exposures; its stronger out-of-domain interpretation is qualified by footnote 7. Main Lean self-play also explicitly includes miniF2F-valid/ProofNet-valid. After 24 iterations their matching weights increase from 1 to 128. Held-out test results should be distinguished from this validation-targeted curriculum and from cumulative training-dataset coverage. Isabelle starts from Llemma-7 b, translates LeanWorkbook using DeepSeek-V 2.5, uses PISA and disables heavy tactics. This is a separate restricted-verifier experiment rather than an equal-strength cross-language comparison. The source argues expert iteration cannot improve indefinitely from a fixed difficulty dataset and motivates indefinite self-improvement. Finite 48-Lean/58-Isabelle-iteration experiments do not prove either an impossibility bound for expert iteration or unbounded STP progress. Training plots count generated proofs, not equal end-to-end wall time, tokens, CPU verification or retraining FLOPs. Conjectures are comparatively few; Lean verification time nevertheless became a bottleneck without an explicit time penalty. The 20-item audit samples only unproved LeanWorkbook statements:16 are labeled faithful formalizations and 7 labeled provable. This conditional small sample cannot estimate the whole-corpus falsehood rate or a numerical global maximum pass rate; the human labels themselves are not formal certificates. Whole-proof pass@k uses independent sampled outputs per statement. Tree-search budgets count steps or complete-proof generations and have different state/value-model costs; Table 1 explicitly says they are not fully comparable. The paper gives uncertainties for several benchmark rates but does not specify repeat counts or whether ± denotes standard deviation, standard error or another interval in the supplied text. Training-curve uncertainty and controlled multi-seed self-play runs are absent.
    - how much was read: most of it read

    - record id: `stp-self-play-llm-2025`

- Trustworthy agents LLM-FM position (2025)
    - title: Position: Trustworthy AI Agents Require the Integration of Large Language Models and Formal Methods
    - what: Argues for bidirectional LLM/formal-method integration: LLMs automate specification and proof work, while model checkers, theorem provers, SMT solvers, testing and runtime monitors constrain and certify LLM actions. The proposed six-stage pipeline starts from natural-language requirements, auto-formalizes them, runs model checking/theorem proving/SMT, tests edge cases, monitors executions and iteratively refines outputs. It sketches a Coq theorem-prover agent and a miniF2F even-number example. Sections 2–5 distinguish failure modes of LLM-only reasoning (hallucination, non-determinism, no traceability), FM-only workflows (specification effort, scalability and unpredictable environments), and hybrid trade-offs. The auto-formalization workflow creates a proof outline with placeholders, delegates algebraic gaps to external tools and revises failed proof steps. The model-checking discussion targets safety/liveness, deadlock and refinement; theorem proving targets machine-checkable proofs; SMT handles satisfiability; testing and monitoring cover behaviors outside proofs. Appendix A gives explicit Coq interaction steps and an Isabelle/HOL formalization attempt, making the proposal concrete but not an implemented benchmark. The implemented PAT prototype repairs GPT-4o car-lock modeling errors using model-checker traces: leavekey must require an open door, and locking receives explicit owner/key guards. Appendix A also gives Z3 meeting scheduling with missing unavailable-day constraints and a Dafny palindrome specification/implementation; these illustrate semantic alignment risks rather than measured success rates.
    - number: six pipeline stages: auto-formalization, model checking, theorem proving, SMT, testing, monitoring
    - number: Coq workflow has four steps from natural language to verified explanation
    - number: Appendix example uses sum of two even numbers
    - limits: This is a position/conceptual paper with case studies, not a controlled empirical evaluation. It assumes formal specifications and verifiers are available and sufficiently expressive; hybrid integration incurs representation mismatch and complexity. Formal methods can miss unmodeled or emergent behavior, while LLM-generated specifications/proofs still require verifier feedback and possibly human refinement. The Coq example is illustrative and does not establish agent success rates.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    -
    - record id: `trustworthy-agents-llm-fm-position-2025`

    - what: GPT-4 agents generate plans and translate natural-language constraints through LTL templates into PRISM properties. - Vulnerability Detection: From Formal Verification to Large Language Models and Hybrid Approaches: A Comprehensive Overview (2025)
    - what: # Reading: Vulnerability Detection, Formal Verification, LLMs and Hybrids (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Tihanyi, Bisztray, Ferrag, Cherif, Dubniczky, Jain and Cordeiro review formal verification, LLM vulnerability detection and pipelines combining them. Read the complete saved arXiv2503.10784v1,13March2025:15pages,five main sections,two figures,one22-row dataset table and59references,noappendices. All15renders were inspected through four contact sheets;Figure2/page4 was also inspected at2600-pixel page resolution. Saved official/inventory PDFs match. The contribution is a narrative review with two small motivating C examples and author-reported model/checker observations, not a new verifier, formal soundness theorem, systematic meta-analysis or reproduced benchmark suite.  Section1/Figure1 selects six categories: static, dynamic and interactive application-security testing,fuzzing,penetration testing and formal verification. The authors acknowledge other taxonomies. This is an organizing choice, not a disjoint exhaustive classification: formal methods can underlie static analysis and testing generation. They contrast learned pattern detection with symbolic guarantees, cite state explosion, language support and specification effort, and propose complementarity. Claims that LLMs lack “true semantic understanding,” will dominate development or decoder-only models inherently generalize better are author assessments without a controlled comparative experiment here. The review reports no search strings,databases,screening protocol,inclusion/exclusion process or quality assessment establishing exhaustive coverage.  Section2 provides its concrete original illustration. Figure2's left program initializes ten unsigned integers,then executes440 outer iterations (printed C expression220*2) and j=1,…,8,adding A[j]^2+8A[j] each time. Authors prompted ChatGPT-4o,o1 and DeepseekR1 with Deepthink to return vulnerable lines/CWE identifiers or an empty list;they report all flaggedCWE-190 overflow. They report ESBMC returned “VERIFICATION SUCCESSFUL” after0.73seconds on a “regular laptop.” A reader arithmetic check gives one inner pass9,737,136,total4,284,339,840,below the32-bit unsigned maximum4,294,967,295 by10,627,455. All summands are positive and each intermediate product is small,so under the intended32-bit unsigned semantics there is no accumulation wrap. The array access excludes indices0 and9;the conclusion depends on the actual printed loop bounds/types,not estimating from the constants. The paper calls2^32 the maximum limit;the maximum representable32-bit unsigned value is2^32−1. C does not universally require unsigned int to be32bits,so the target data model matters. This calculation is arithmetic over copied constants,not execution/import/compilation of author code.  The right program sums i=0,…,999,999,999 into long long. Reader formula gives499,999,999,500,000,000,below2^63−1;positive partial sums also fit a64-bit signed accumulator. Authors say an LLM can recognize safety whereas ESBMC must unroll10^9times. They measured46seconds for the first10^6iterations and extrapolate approximately12.7hours for the whole loop. Linear extrapolation gives46,000seconds≈12.78hours;the complete runtime and memory consumption were not measured here. This is a limitation of the described unrolling configuration,not all formal verification:an inductive invariant or arithmetic summary can reason about the loop without enumerating every iteration. Reference39 itself concerns invariant generation “without loop unrolling.” No exact laptop/CPU/RAM,checker command/version/options,unwinding assertions,target data model,solver limits,model snapshots,raw LLM outputs,request dates,trial counts,temperature/seeds or latency for the LLMs is supplied. The examples do not establish population error rates or a general speed ranking.  Section3.1 distinguishes encoder-only,encoder-decoder and decoder-only transformers;discusses CodeBERT,GraphCodeBERT,CodeT5,GPT/CodeLlama,prompting versus fine-tuning,and claims flexible generative repair versus efficient classification. Section3.2/Table1 catalogs22datasets with stated size,language,“#Unique Vuln.”,granularity and source. Counts range from Draper1,274k to Vulcorpus100;the prose says “over 100” but includes100. Granularities vary: function,file,contract,commit,website and repository. VulDeeLocator lists LLVM IR as its language/representation. These units and label policies prevent adding sizes as a number of distinct vulnerabilities or directly comparing corpus difficulty. “#Unique Vuln.” is a table label whose exact counting definition is not established here;do not silently reinterpret it as positive examples. Draper is explicitly cautioned as static-analyzer-labeled. SARD/Juliet inclusion and mixed/generated/real-world origins further require deduplication,provenance and split checks before any aggregate training benchmark.  The table includes Draper,SARD,SeVc,DiverseVul,FormAIv2,BigVul,PrimeVul,VulDeeLocator,JulietC/C++,CodeGadgets,JulietJava,FELLMVP,CVEfixes,ChatPHP,VCMatch,SVEN,Ponta2019,VulBench,Ullah2023,Smartbugs,MAGMA,Vulcorpus. Main text attributes model/dataset examples to ReGVD,VulBERTa and121M-parameter SecureFalcon,then imports an approximately73% fine-tuning-study proportion from[58]. These are cited literature claims,not experiments rerun in this review. The displayed ReGVD attribution[21] points to a broadly titled2024experimental study;this reading does not establish original provenance from that citation alone. The table prints sizes such as “12,k” and decimal commas;the record retains original entries rather than silently normalizing an uncertain digit or unit.  Section3.3 describes zero/few-shot prompting,context costs and inconsistency;its approximately54.5% balanced-accuracy claim has no directly attached study identification or experimental denominator/settings. Reference55 supports chain-of-thought broadly,not automatically a matched vulnerability-detection benchmark. Section3.4 emphasizes label scarcity,C/C++ dominance,underrepresented concurrency,aliasing/dataflow,long contexts,behavior-preserving transformations,false positives/negatives,ensembles,on-premise cost,distillation andLoRA. IDE/copilot,repository triage and smart-contract examples are broad application descriptions,not a measured deployment study here. Generalization claims require independent splits,contamination checks,metrics and matched task/model conditions;none is newly established by this review.  Section4 outlines state-space exploration,temporal properties,BDD/BMC history,state explosion and SAT/SMT bounded execution encodings. It cites ESBMC checks of ArmCCA components[57] andLSVerifier/open-sourceC[7],but gives no new property list,model artifacts,coverage or measurements for those studies. Statements that formal verification always yields a proof or a concrete counterexample,eliminates false positives or establishes safety “across all possible scenarios” need qualification. A sound result is relative to the formal model,property,environment/compiler semantics and trusted checking implementation;tools can return unknown,timeout or fail to prove. BMC success ordinarily excludes encoded counterexamples only within the checked bound,unless a separate completeness/unwinding/induction argument covers all executions. Abstract counterexamples may be spurious for the implementation until concretized;under-modeled environments can miss real bugs. Reader case:checking increments only through boundk can miss an assertion violation on incrementk+1. This is a logical limitation of bounded evidence,not an execution of ESBMC.  The introduction also says BMC “fails to address” SQLinjection/XSS. Those vulnerability names are not a general impossibility result for formal methods:security-relevant dataflow,sanitization,parsing and sink semantics must be represented in the model/property;tool/frontend support and tractability constrain a particular analysis. The paper does not show such a model or a formal limitation theorem. Likewise,a valid modeled counterexample is not by itself a demonstrated exploit in every deployment.  Section5.1 identifies LLM assistance in properties,invariants and constraints:hardware assertions[23],Pei etal.'s invariant generation[38],PropertyGPT[30],FVEL[28],CryptoFormalEval[6] andLEMUR[56]. It imports PropertyGPT's80% ground-truth-property recall and12zero-day bounty findings;its body labels the work“NDSS 2024”, while reference30 is an arXiv2024entry. The survey does not give the ground-truth denominator,matching criterion,contracts,seeds,proof bounds,or bug adjudication needed to independently evaluate those quantities. The exact primary version/venue must be checked before relying on that body citation. LEMUR is credited with a sound calculus conditional on a sound underlying verifier;this review supplies neither that calculus nor its proof and benchmark details. CryptoFormalEval is described as middleware for an LLM agent's repeated interaction with a protocol prover,which is directly relevant to protocol proof-agent evaluation,but this paper adds no fresh protocol/task outcomes.  The claim that formal checks ensure “only valid, useful properties” requires separating syntax,truth and adequacy. Compilation can establish well-formedness,not truth;proving a generated assertion does not establish that it expresses the human's desired security property or is strong enough. Reader cases:a verifier can prove True while missing an overflow,or a false precondition can make a desired theorem vacuous. An invariant must satisfy initiation,consecution and the required consequence,not simply look plausible. An LLM can be an untrusted proposer when independent sound checks cover every accepted step;that arrangement does not certify the human-to-specification translation,model completeness or unchecked natural-language reasoning.  Section5.2 describes ESBMC-AI's repair loop and claims evaluation on50,000vulnerable FormAI programs,substantial first-attempt repair and eventual iterative repair of the remainder. These are survey assertions attributed to[49];no result table,success counts,failed/timeout cases,attempt bound or source-version reconciliation substantiates universal eventual repair here. This reading neither accepts them as independently verified primary-study totals nor infers a100%repair rate. The section revisits PropertyGPT/CryptoFormalEval and argues for generate-check-revise workflows. Its phrase“formal analysis guarantees the fix’s correctness” is limited to the actual checked postconditions/model/bounds. A patch that deletes functionality or returns a constant can satisfy a memory-safety assertion while violating required behavior;functional contracts/refinement and adequate environmental assumptions are additional obligations. A verifier does not inevitably catch a weak property,missing specification,unknown result or unchecked output.  The final paragraphs advocate complementary workflows;there is no separate conclusion section,new experiment or theorem beyond the motivating illustration. All 59 bibliography entries were read,including invariant-generation and interactive-proof pointers. For this database,the paper is a useful citation map for generated specifications,invariants,protocol-prover feedback and repairs,and a concrete evaluation exercise in distinguishing bounded evidence,property adequacy,reported speed from extrapolated speed,and primary results from survey assertions. It provides no Verus,verifiedRust orTLA+artifact,no distributed-system implementation verification,and no new LLM proof-agent benchmark. No author-code execution/import,scientific-result loading,compiler/checker/solver/prover/model-checker run,or independent LLM experiment occurred in this reading;only source reading,renders and own arithmetic.
    - limits: Narrative/secondaryclaims;guaranteesmodel/property/bound/trustedcheckerrelative;generatedpropertytruthnotadequacy;32bitunsignedCtarget;unknown/checkertimeoutpossible;noindependentprimarytotalsorcheckerreproduction
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10784)

- Autoformalize Mathematical Statements by Symbolic Equivalence and Semantic Consistency (2024)
    - what: # Whole reading: Autoformalize Mathematical Statements by Symbolic Equivalence and Semantic Consistency (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The framework generates ten Isabelle statement candidates, ranks them using symbolic agreement and back-translation similarity, and selects results. SymEq checks premise and conclusion equivalence after numerical standardization or heuristic variable matching, then scores equivalence-class size. SemCo asks an LLM to informalize each candidate and computes BERT cosine similarity to the original problem. Log, linear and quadratic combinations weight normalized scores, with alpha=0.5 selected empirically. It is a candidate-selection method; it neither supplies a verified translation relation to natural language nor proves that a majority class expresses the intended problem. [Evidence: Q2, Q3, Q4, Q5, Q8 in quotes.json.]  The prose before Definition 1 requires noncontradictory premises, but Definition 1 says premises are not tautologies. Those conditions differ: False is not a tautology but is contradictory; True is noncontradictory but is a tautology. The proposed P implies False check can establish contradiction when a sound prover succeeds; failure or timeout does not establish satisfiability. Premise-equivalence plus conclusion-equivalence is stronger than equivalence of whole implications. The text also calls the true product statement symbolically equivalent to 4=4; that claim requires the later auxiliary-variable projection/simplification interpretation, not the literal componentwise definition alone. These are written-definition qualifications, not an implementation audit. [Evidence: Q3, Q4, Q13 in quotes.json.]  Numerical standardization introduces alpha=f(x) and compares residual-value relations, leaving original variables auxiliary. The quantifier/projection semantics must be specified: the printed free-variable equivalence in Appendix C is false if x,y,a,b remain independent (x=2/3,y=6,alpha=0,a=0 makes its left premise true and right premise false). Existentially quantifying auxiliaries makes that example work but can discard modeling structure and identify unrelated solved true problems through the same residual set. Non-numerical matching uses edit-distance weights and top-k maximum bipartite matchings; maximizing raw distance favors dissimilar names unless the weight is negated or converted to similarity. Finite heuristic matching and incomplete ATP search can miss genuine equivalences; no completeness theorem is provided. Appendix C Example 2 asks for five positive integers, but both displayed nat statements admit all five equal to zero, with n=0 in No.1. Then 4^0=1 is not divisible by 2. No.1 also retains an extra variable n and its sum assumption, so the displayed variable counts differ and the projection/elimination step needs specification. This is a manual boundary check, not a checked formal counterexample. [Evidence: Q3, Q13, Q4, Q20 in quotes.json.]  Back-translation is fallible model output, and embedding similarity is a numerical proxy rather than a proof of preserved meaning. The paper itself notes that notation changes can alter mathematical meaning while embeddings miss them. Its power example explicitly maps both powr and natural-exponent power back to the same word, which can hide the very distinction being checked. Shared model errors and correlated wrong candidates remain possible; consensus does not establish the correct domain, quantifiers, definitions or assumptions. [Evidence: Q4, Q12, Q14 in quotes.json.]  Section 3.3 and Algorithm 1 call normalization softmax, while the displayed formulas divide each score by the sum of scores without exponentials. For scores 1,2 these give 1/3,2/3; actual softmax gives about 0.269,0.731. This can change a combined ranking. Raw cosine scores need not all be positive and their sum can be zero, so the printed division/log combination needs positivity and nonzero-denominator conditions or a stated transform. The publication does not specify that safeguard. This identifies ambiguity in written mathematics, not which normalization author code executes. [Evidence: Q5, Q4 in quotes.json.]  MATH evaluation randomly selects 400 problems; miniF2F contains 488 problems with formal oracles. Five models are Mistral-7B,Llemma-34B,DeepSeek-v2,Codex completion API and GPT-4(version 0710), temperature 0.7, ten candidates and declared eight-shot examples. MATH candidates are manually labeled; miniF2F correctness is automated equivalence against the supplied oracle. n@k denotes whether the top n of k candidates includes a correct version. The log-probability baseline changes to prompted ranking for Codex/GPT-4; Naive filters provable candidates, and Cluster uses adaptive k-means on formal-statement BERT embeddings. The checker uses scala-isabelle, twelve listed Isabelle tactics including Sledgehammer timeout 300 s, and Z3/CVC5. The authors report eight examples, but Appendix F prints two generic instruction templates without an identifiable complete block of eight demonstration pairs. Their archive contents remain uninspected. [Evidence: Q6, Q5, Q14 in quotes.json.]  miniF2F scores measure agreement with a finite automatic oracle-equivalence procedure, which shares the SymEq machinery and can miss valid alternate encodings or inherit flawed oracles. They are not independently human-adjudicated semantic-correctness rates. Manual MATH labeling lacks a fully described annotator count, agreement/adjudication protocol and blinded treatment of methods in this publication. The paper itself discusses human errors and problematic formal oracles. Passing a prover for a generated statement establishes only that statement relative to its assumptions and trusted checking, not that the natural-language task was formalized faithfully. [Evidence: Q6, Q12, Q14, Q16, Q17 in quotes.json.]  Table 1 GPT-4 MATH1@10 is37.5% baseline,42.0% SymEq,39.5% SemCo; miniF2F is32.9%,41.1%,34.9%. Figure 5 log-comb gives45.3% and43.6%, improvements of7.8 and10.7percentage points over those baselines and3.3/2.5points over SymEq. Codex MATH log-comb reaches49.4%. These are reported empirical selection gains under the stated labeling procedure, not formal guarantees or evidence on distributed-system specifications. SymEq is not best in every Table 1 cell: Mistral MATH3@10 SemCo38.8 exceeds SymEq37.9. [Evidence: Q6, Q7, Q8 in quotes.json.]  Do not silently merge differing displayed values. Figure 5 Llemma MATH SymEq41.4/SemCo35.4 differ from Table 1 41.1/36.5; miniF2F SymEq31.2 differs from32.0. Figure 5 Mistral MATH SymEq26.7 to log-comb42.6 is15.9percentage points (about59.6%relative), not the RQ2 prose22.6% when read as that comparison. Table 2 geometry SymEq28.1 exceeds SemCo25.1, so the narrative about geometry favoring semantic sensitivity is a hypothesis, not a demonstrated category gain. The paper does not resolve these table/figure/text discrepancies. Figure 5 miniF2F labels its pink series SemCo but prints 7.5,20.5,27.6,29.7,34.8, versus Table 1 SemCo 8.8,27.2,27.6,33.2,34.9. Figure 5 MATH Codex SemCo is 42.6 versus Table 1 44.9. Figure 4, captioned GPT-4 MATH log-comb, has visually estimated endpoints near 46.6–46.7 and a peak near 47.5. These disagree with Table 1 semantic/symbolic endpoints 39.5/42.0 and Figure 5 log-comb 45.3. At alpha=0 the log combination ranks only by semantic score. Appendix D Figure 7 also uses a 45.5–48 range. No undocumented split, model or correction is inferred to reconcile these displays. [Evidence: Q6, Q7, Q8 in quotes.json.]  RQ3 prints sigma=sum over n=2..k-1 of n times(q_n-q_(n-1)) plus(1-q_k), with q_n=n@k. Under ordinary sequential inspection of up to k candidates, expected checks are sum over n=1..k of n times(q_n-q_(n-1)) plus k(1-q_k), q_0=0. The printed formula gives0 if the first candidate is always correct,0 if only the kth is correct, and1 if none is correct, instead of1,k,k. Thus it is not the stated ordinary average number of formal statements labeled without an additional unreported cost convention. Table 3 reports17.7–21.6% MATH and8.4–21.9% miniF2F relative savings for log-comb; these are derived scores, not measured human-time reductions. No author-code recalculation performed. [Evidence: Q9 in quotes.json.]  At k=10 there are at most45unordered candidate pairs before transitivity shortcuts, plus ten informalizations. The full checker versus auto/simp-only GPT-4 ablation reports 1@10 both 41.1%, 2@10 48.1 versus 47.5, and 3@10 49.3 versus 48.7. Its values match miniF2F in Table 1, but Figure 6 and the RQ4 paragraph do not explicitly name the dataset. The prose lists2.13vs2.33equivalences while saying the normal checker proves more, without ordering these two values clearly. Then, the two statements are called symbolically equivalent if the two logical > equivalences, i.e., P1 ≡ P2 and Q1 ≡ Q2, both hold. > The two logical equivalences induced by the symbolic equivalence can be determined through existing > automated theorem provers (ATPs). Additionally, the validity of the premises can also be checked by > replacing the conclusion Q with a contradictory result (e.g., 0 = 1) and verifying P → Q.     - limits: The framework generates ten Isabelle statement candidates, ranks them using symbolic agreement and back-translation similarity, and selects results. SymEq checks premise and conclusion equivalence after numerical standardization or heuristic variable matching, then scores equivalence-class size. SemCo asks an LLM to informalize each candidate and computes BERT cosine similarity to the original problem. Log, linear and quadratic combinations weight normalized scores, with alpha=0.5 selected empirically. It is a candidate-selection method; it neither supplies a verified translation relation to natural language nor proves that a majority class expresses the intended problem. MATH evaluation randomly selects 400 problems; miniF2F contains 488 problems with formal oracles. Five models are Mistral-7B,Llemma-34B,DeepSeek-v2,Codex completion API and GPT-4(version 0710), temperature 0.7, ten candidates and declared eight-shot examples. MATH candidates are manually labeled; miniF2F correctness is automated equivalence against the supplied oracle. n@k denotes whether the top n of k candidates includes a correct version. The log-probability baseline changes to prompted ranking for Codex/GPT-4; Naive filters provable candidates, and Cluster uses adaptive k-means on formal-statement BERT embeddings. The checker uses scala-isabelle, twelve listed Isabelle tactics including Sledgehammer timeout 300 s, and Z3/CVC5. The authors report eight examples, but Appendix F prints two generic instruction templates without an identifiable complete block of eight demonstration pairs. Their archive contents remain uninspected. miniF2F scores measure agreement with a finite automatic oracle-equivalence procedure, which shares the SymEq machinery and can miss valid alternate encodings or inherit flawed oracles. They are not independently human-adjudicated semantic-correctness rates. Manual MATH labeling lacks a fully described annotator count, agreement/adjudication protocol and blinded treatment of methods in this publication. The paper itself discusses human errors and problematic formal oracles. Passing a prover for a generated statement establishes only that statement relative to its assumptions and trusted checking, not that the natural-language task was formalized faithfully. RQ3 prints sigma=sum over n=2..k-1 of n times(q_n-q_(n-1)) plus(1-q_k), with q_n=n@k. Under ordinary sequential inspection of up to k candidates, expected checks are sum over n=1..k of n times(q_n-q_(n-1)) plus k(1-q_k), q_0=0. The printed formula gives0 if the first candidate is always correct,0 if only the kth is correct, and1 if none is correct, instead of1,k,k. Thus it is not the stated ordinary average number of formal statements labeled without an additional unreported cost convention. Table 3 reports17.7–21.6% MATH and8.4–21.9% miniF2F relative savings for log-comb; these are derived scores, not measured human-time reductions. No author-code recalculation performed. Example 6 contrasts human labels on two cardinality encodings; Example 7 highlights positive-integer versus nat-domain oracle issues; Example 12 uses a real parameter2*n to represent even integers, which does not itself enforce integrality. These illustrate that equivalence search, domain checks, library grounding and human review must be separated when evaluating generated specifications. - Autoformalizing Euclidean Geometry (2024)
    - what: Introduces LeanEuclid, 173 manually formalized Euclidean geometry statement/proof/diagram examples, and a Lean implementation of a variant of system E. A symbolic SMT engine fills implicit diagrammatic proof gaps and evaluates statement equivalence as E3. January2024 GPT-4/GPT-4V few-shot experiments report up to21.0% solver-proved statement equivalence and23.1% proof acceptance across the143-item evaluation set. SMT answers are admitted through an explicitly unsound axiom rather than reconstructed as Lean proof terms, so results require trusting the translation and SMT solvers as well as the Lean kernel. Separates explicit construction steps, chosen by humans/models, from non-construction diagrammatic/metric/transfer reasoning handled by SMT refutation. euclid_apply instantiates a rule, proves missing premises with SMT, and adds conclusions/witnesses; euclid_finish/assert discharge goals. E3 checks both implication directions plus contradiction detection; approximate comparison requires matching numbers/types of bound variables, selects top-n renamings by string similarity, and counts four directions of pre/postcondition obligations, adding preconditions to postcondition comparisons only after precondition equivalence. Dataset contains all48 Elements BookI propositions and125 UniGeo examples (25 each in five categories); texts are manually augmented/abridged. Appendices give a full axiom catalogue, a missing-case reconstruction for Elements24, formalization comparisons, exact prompts, partial semantic outcomes, and before/after repair scripts.
    - limits: Correctness is relative to the chosen geometric axioms and Lean-to-SMT encoding. AppendixC explicitly introduces an unsound axiom to admit successful SMT queries; there is no end-to-end kernel certification or SMT proof reconstruction. The reader did not audit or execute the released implementation. SystemE soundness/completeness is attributed to Avigad etal for its original axioms. This implementation adds construction, diagrammatic and transfer rules, strengthens one construction rule, and uses SMT real-number theories. The supplied paper does not mechanize a soundness proof of the modified implementation. The diagrammatic engine uses diagrammatic,metric,transfer rules. Equivalence checking additionally uses nine simple construction rules. Limited construction/instantiation makes E3 incomplete; failure to establish equivalence is not proof of inequivalence. Logical equivalence of closed true theorems can be weaker than faithful correspondence of hypotheses,objects and informal meaning. The paper itself reports a logically equivalent but unfaithful quadrilateral/parallelism example; success should not be read as comprehensive intent validation. Approximate equivalence is a bounded heuristic over variable unifications and clause counts, not a semantic distance with a correctness theorem. It excludes formulas with differing variable counts/types and depends on chosen clauses and names. Evaluation uses gpt-4-1106-preview and gpt-4-1106-vision-preview in January2024, one domain, manually prepared prompts and a small curated test split. No significance intervals or multiple-run variance are reported. Proof autoformalization receives a correct formal statement and informal proof, whereas statement autoformalization is evaluated separately. Proof success cannot establish an end-to-end informal-statement-to-correct-proof rate. Manual Elements repairs use ground-truth proofs and a minimum-change preference; Levenshtein ratios measure textual alteration, not time,semantic effort,independent automation success or proof validity. Data-contamination improbability is the authors inference from LeanEuclid being unpublished at experiment time. Models may have seen Elements/UniGeo or other formalizations; no pretraining audit is supplied. No distributed systems,Verus,Rust or TLA+ evaluation occurs. Reuse of domain rules and semantic equivalence checks in those settings is a reader hypothesis, not established transfer.

- ESBMC-AI self-healing software (2024)
    - title: A New Era in Software Security: Towards Self-Healing Software via Large Language Models and Formal Verification
    - what: Presents ESBMC-AI, a counterexample-guided repair loop in which ESBMC finds a bounded C vulnerability and emits a trace/stack trace, an LLM proposes a patch, and ESBMC verifies the patch again. The paper evaluates GPT-4o-based repair on FormAI vulnerability samples and measures both verification outcomes and manual semantic correctness. The framework has User Chat and Fix Code modes, converts ESBMC counterexamples into a human-readable prompt, and uses a 10-second ESBMC timeout with overflow, memory-leak, stack-trace, one-unwind and multi-property options. Because program equivalence is undecidable, the authors manually inspect 1,337 samples from common categories and use lines-of-code and cyclomatic-complexity changes as additional patch-impact indicators. Table III separates verified-safe, verified-failed and unknown outcomes for eight vulnerability classes; examples show that adding one to an upper bound can leave the same violation, while arithmetic-overflow repairs may require a second iteration. A misleading MD5/password example demonstrates that LLM-only diagnosis follows names and semantics poorly, whereas the BMC trace identifies the actual multiplication overflow.
    - number: Table III samples: lower-bound array 182, fscanf 241, scanf 175, division by zero 133, NULL dereference 229, arithmetic add overflow 73, forgotten memory 187, upper-bound array 117
    - number: single-iteration accuracy: 95.60% lower-bound array, 91.29% fscanf, 90.40% scanf, 86.47% division by zero, 80.35% NULL dereference, 70.27% add overflow, 48.66% forgotten memory, 41.03% upper-bound array
    - number: second-iteration arithmetic example reaches 88% on the same samples
    - number: FormAI corpus described as 50,000 randomly selected C programs; manual subset 1,337
    - limits: BMC only checks bounded executions and depends on ESBMC modeling and timeout; a successful verification is not a proof of whole-program semantic equivalence. LLM patches can compile yet preserve or introduce bugs, and the off-the-shelf model is nondeterministic and not explainable. The study manually verifies a subset rather than all 30,000 candidate samples, uses human-designed prompts and GPT-4o, and notes that forgotten-memory failures may require external headers/files absent from the prompt. Results therefore measure one model, one prompt workflow and selected FormAI categories, not general self-healing correctness.
    - how much was read: most of it read

    - record id: `esbmc-ai-self-healing-software-2024`
- Graph2Tac: Online Representation Learning of Formal Math Concepts (2024)
    - what: Studies online representation learning for Coq proof search. The online k-nearest-neighbor solver learns from recent tactic scripts; Graph2Tac is a graph neural network whose definition task computes hierarchical embeddings for new definitions. On a package-disjoint benchmark of unseen Coq developments, online k-NN improves from 15.0% to 25.8% solved, Graph2Tac with online definition updates from 17.4% to 26.1%, and a time-scaled combination solves 33.2%. The paper argues that online information matters most when test theorems have many dependencies unseen during training. Represents Coq's kernel environment as one reversible, hash-shared graph; proof-state and definition subgraphs stop expansion at global definition nodes and are topologically ordered. Graph2Tac prunes each graph to 1024 nodes, uses an eight-hop message-passing GNN with 128-dimensional embeddings, predicts base tactics and local/global definition or hypothesis arguments, and computes new definition embeddings from their graphs in dependency order. The definition loss is one minus cosine similarity and the tactic/argument loss is cross-entropy, combined as 1000 Ldef + Ltactic. Inference uses beam width 256. Three variants separate anonymous/name-aware embeddings and no-definition-task controls. Tactician turns single-tactic predictions into whole proofs with iterative-deepening Dijkstra-style search. Online k-NN searches the 1000 most recent tactic steps; offline k-NN searches training proofs with LSH forests. The artifact includes code, models, benchmark data, commits and analysis notebooks.
    - limits: Evaluation uses 120 jointly installable Opam packages selected by a SAT solver for Coq v8.11, covering mathematics, compiler/programming-language formalization, separation logic and HoTT. Package-version compatibility does not imply logical consistency. The paper does not specifically evaluate transfer to Verus/Rust/TLA+ agents or distributed-protocol specification tasks. The reported main results exclude hott and tlc. The paper found inconsistent axioms in three packages; G2T-Anon-Update used tlc's skip_axiom (False) for 192/500 five-minute theorems (38.4%), so including them would distort pass rates. Comparisons with CoqGym and prior neural systems are explicitly informal and non-conclusive because projects, Coq versions, theorem counts, time limits, hardware, parallelism and solver implementations differ. The paper states that ‘more work is needed to compare’ its methods fairly. The one-model, one-package-split evaluation is variable; the authors say excluding hott and tlc heavily influences final results and that the split and training instability increase variability. No multiple-seed confidence intervals are reported. Graph2Tac cannot predict arbitrary term arguments and uses a fixed tactic vocabulary after excluding tactics occurring fewer than six times; the transformer can generate arbitrary term arguments but has no online learning. The graph is pruned to 1024 nodes, so claims about representations concern the retained closure, not the entire 250-million-node universe. Definition embeddings are trained with manually tuned settings, and training had periodic instability and checkpoint restarts. The online k-NN's advantage depends on recency and same-file proof availability; the ablation calls it ‘slightly brittle’ because proof ordering outside the current file is arbitrary. Model-called pass rates are affected by how many tactic suggestions each call returns and by search speed; aggregate solvers time-share one CPU and are simulations, not concurrent runs with extra compute. The paper reports the artifact as fully reproducible, but this reading did not execute the code, Coq benchmark or provided models.
    - how much was read: most of it read

    - record id: `graph2tac-online-representation-learning-2024`
- Learning Formal Mathematics From Intrinsic Motivation (2024)
    - what: MINIMO jointly trains a randomly initialized character Transformer to conjecture typed propositions and prove them in Peano dependent type theory. Type-directed constrained decoding guarantees well-formed conjectures; MCTS policy/value proof search and hindsight relabeling provide self-generated training data. Completion engine recursively constructs type inhabitants through Peano typing/unification; general inhabitation is undecidable so timeout/rejection remains. Difficulty is proof log-likelihood, discretized by recent-batch percentiles (10% hard, 50% trivial, remaining easy). MCTS provides successful paths and hindsight intermediate propositions; cleanup removes irrelevant steps and duplicate goals. Appendix B compares likelihood with MCTS expansions; Appendix C explains why arithmetic provable-conjecture fraction can drop while useful learning increases. Appendix D lists the exact axiom sets and Appendix E includes generated induction proof. Appendix F outlines, but does not implement, Peano/Lean proof reconstruction.
    - limits: Formal environment is Peano, a minimal dependent type theory with finite action space; conjecture token budget K=150 and rejection <10% reported. Correctness is machine-checked by Peano for found proofs; experiments are small (three domains, five iterations, three seeds). Fixed axiom/definition library prevents deep cumulative theory; larger libraries make action enumeration slow; no Verus/Rust/TLA+ implementation. Important scope discrepancy: main text says 35 Kleene statements, but Appendix E.2 explicitly says 30 and lists 30 statements (including a duplicated 9a/9b formula). Preserve this inconsistency; do not assert a reconciled denominator. Groups axiomatization explicitly includes commutativity op_comm, so these experiments concern commutative groups despite broader prose label. 8.45M-parameter character Transformer (8 layers/heads, 512 hidden, context 1024), 2000 AdamW steps per iteration; each run 8–16 GPU hours, 288 GPU hours total for main results. Formal correctness is relative to Peano implementation and stated axioms. No independently replayed proofs or external supplement; Natural Number Game statements are translated to Peano, not checked in Lean.

- LLMLIFT verified lifting (2024)
    - title: Verified Code Transpilation with LLMs
    - what: Introduces LLMLIFT, an LLM-based verified-lifting workflow that generates a DSL program summary and loop invariants in Python IR, verifies equivalence to side-effect-free source code with SMT, then rewrites the verified IR into concrete DSL syntax. The workflow uses oracle-guided inductive synthesis: prompts provide DSL operator semantics in Python, the LLM proposes summaries and invariants, a parser rejects unsupported syntax, and cvc5/Z3 check functional equivalence for all states. Four implementations target Spark/MapReduce, Domino programmable switches, TACO tensor algebra and TensIR. Two-phase summary-then-invariant generation is stronger than joint generation; smaller Llama3/Mistral models fail the complex tensor suite due to syntax adherence/context.

Correctness is relative to the encoded IR semantics, rewrite rules and theorem-prover model; code performance is inherited from target DSLs rather than optimized by LLMLIFT. The verifier supplies Boolean feedback, and the authors identify semantic filtering/counterexamples as future improvements.

- PropertyGPT (2024)
    - title: PropertyGPT: LLM-driven Formal Verification of Smart Contracts through Retrieval-Augmented Property Generation
    - what: PropertyGPT retrieves human-written Certora properties by embedding their associated code, prompts GPT-4-turbo to generate Solidity-like PSL properties for new contracts, repairs candidates with compiler/static feedback, ranks them by code/property similarity, and verifies them with source-level symbolic execution, modular verification and bounded model checking. The PSL intermediate language expresses invariants, function pre/postconditions and scenario rules in Solidity-like syntax. A conservative code-similarity threshold retrieves roughly 10–20 references; generated properties are iteratively repaired (up to nine attempts in the implementation), checked for inclusion of the target function, then ranked using four raw/summary embedding similarities with learned weights α=.134, β=.556, γ=.141, η=.168. The prover uses strongest-postcondition symbolic execution and Z3, over-approximates unknown on-chain calls, and bounds model checking to depth three (five loop iterations). The dataset contains 623 properties from 23 Certora projects; tests include 90 held-out properties, 13 CVEs, 24 attack incidents and four bounty projects.
    - number: 623 properties from 23 projects
    - number: 90 held-out properties
    - number: recall 0.80, precision 0.64, F1 0.71
    - number: 9/13 CVEs
    - limits: Certora CVL and generated PSL properties lack an automated equivalence checker; three authors manually judged semantic equivalence. On-chain calls are assumed to succeed with symbolic return data, and nonlinear functions use uninterpreted approximations. Bounded checking can miss deeper behavior, runtime context is not modeled, and top-K ranking/generation depends on GPT-4-turbo and a hand-curated knowledge base. The reported zero-day findings require manual confirmation and responsible-disclosure fixes.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://doi.org/10.14722/ndss.2025.241357)
