- Subgoal-based Demonstration Learning for Formal Theorem Proving (2024)
    - what: Introduces subgoal-based demonstration learning for LLM formal theorem proving. It iteratively turns informal proofs into subgoal sequences that are checked and repaired against Isabelle, then trains a discrete graph diffusion model to select and order in-context demonstrations. On miniF2F with Isabelle, the full method reports 48.0% cumulative validation pass rate and 45.5% test pass rate; without diffusion it reports 44.1% test, and without subgoals 40.6%. The method reaches 38.5% test pass rate with 20 LLM calls, versus 100 calls used for the comparable DSP reference setting. A valid subgoal sequence must be reachable from the theorem statement and able to reach a passing proof state. Iterative refinement starts from manually written subgoals, asks an LLM to produce them, recursively calls an Isabelle-backed Verify-and-Correct routine for each segment, and retains only verified or fallback pairs. Demonstration reorganization treats selection and ordering as a Hamiltonian-path-like graph problem; successful randomized organizations supervise a discrete diffusion model. A GNN denoiser encodes Ada embeddings of the target and each demonstration, and inference samples 200 candidate organizations, scores them, picks the highest-scoring candidate, and appends examples until the context limit. The paper supplies recursive algorithms, prompt templates, failure/success traces, ordering and count ablations, leakage checks and Sledgehammer-cost analysis.
    - limits: Formal-sketch validity is defined as no “sorry”/“oops” cheating keyword and Isabelle authentication of the generated proof. The reported cumulative validation rate includes organizations discovered during the diffusion training-data collection, so it is not a held-out estimate in the same sense as the test rate. The training data has no separate miniF2F training split: successful randomized organizations from miniF2F-valid are used to train the diffusion model. Test statements are separate, but examples, prompts and problem names can be web-visible; the paper reports a leakage audit and randomized-name experiment rather than proving no contamination. Subgoal reachability is operational: an LLM fills or repairs the formal sketch and Isabelle accepts or rejects it. A failed verification can trigger a correction/decomposition, but after the finite call budget the algorithm returns the original pair, so not every retained demonstration is guaranteed to contain a newly repaired valid subgoal in that path. Algorithm3 describes a10-call budget for Verify-and-Correct,counting one call for verification and one for correction. Reader observation:the pseudocode can spend a correction call after a failed verification has exhausted the last remaining unit,so a strict10-call bound is not established by the shown loop. No budget-exhaustion frequency or full recursive-cost distribution is reported. The main LLM is GPT-3.5-turbo-0301 at temperature 0 and 1024 max tokens. The diffusion model uses 137 collected successful organization contexts from 116 statements, split 81 for training and 56 for validation, with three repeated experiments. Small data and model/version choices constrain generality. miniF2F contains high-school mathematics in Isabelle, HOL-Light and Lean, but this study evaluates Isabelle only. No Verus, Rust, TLA+, concurrency, distributed systems or software specifications are tested. Ordering and selection results are benchmark-specific. Shuffling an organization drops solved problems from 94 to 39 in a 20-attempt test, but this is not a causal decomposition of all prompt effects; the model also changes selected examples, order, and uses a finite context. Figures 16–18 show malformed or failed Isabelle outputs from reordered demonstrations, including an incorrect use of mod_mult_self2, an unsuitable conjecture, and an unresolved factorial equation. The paper reports them as qualitative cases, not formal error rates. The authors’ statement that the approach can extend to software verification is future-work motivation; the experiments establish only mathematical theorem-proving behavior.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    -
    - record id: `subgoal-based-demonstration-learning-2024`
- Synthetic Programming Elicitation for Text-to-Code in Very Low-Resource Programming and Formal Languages (2024)
    - what: SPEAC makes an LLM generate a familiar intermediate language, repairs that output with compiler checks and weighted MAX-SMT, then translates it into a language poorly represented in training data. Eudoxus instantiates this with a subset of Python that describes UCLID5 transition systems. It prunes unsupported syntax, replaces inconsistent syntax-tree nodes with holes, infers repairs from a satisfying model and asks the LLM to fill remaining holes. The evaluation measures compiler acceptance and a single author’s manual semantic scores on 33 textbook tasks. Preserves how the intermediate language was manually designed, exact pruning/constraint/repair contracts and minimality conditions, the traffic-light input ownership mistake, type and timer concerns, every result-table cell and denominator, three dataset roles, baseline and call settings, manual scoring, timing components and limits of the semantic claim. Distinguishes satisfying static constraints from preserving intended behavior or proving the generated transition system.
    - limits: §3–§5: Requires a target language T, familiar parent P, child subset C and compiler f:C→T. Here T is UCLID5, P Python and C an abstract Module subclass with declaration/init/next methods. The language design and syntax-directed translator are supplied by people; the paper does not prove compiler semantic preservation or automatically derive a correct modeling language. §5.1: Synthetic elicitation is a manual lightweight grounded-theory analysis of LLM outputs, not a human-subject study. People label output fragments, group codes into concepts/categories and design C. The object-oriented category is chosen for ease of translation; no experiment establishes universal LLM naturalness or superiority over every alternative child language. §5.1, p.6: GPT-3.5-turbo-0125 and GPT-4-turbo-2024-04-09 each generate ten samples on each of 33 curated regression tasks: 660 direct-UCLID5 attempts, none parses. This is a result for those models/tasks, not a universal inability theorem. Imaginary FormalVerificationLibrary prompts elicit from-scratch Z3, procedural API and object-oriented subclass styles. §5.2/Figure 4: Initial prompt specifies parent, task, unique code block and child description, ending with an affirmative assistant prefix. Repair prompt asks to replace every ?? and repeats the task. The templates are partial; the PDF does not include every concrete benchmark prompt or the complete child grammar. §5.3: Error-tolerant parsing first builds a parent-language AST, then removes constructs outside C. This initial pruning may lose program intent. Static checks are encoded as soft clauses per node; MAX-SMT keeps a satisfiable weighted subset and rejected clauses identify nodes to replace with holes. Hard clauses and algebraic data types constrain legal structure. §5.3, p.7: Minimum node-edit guarantee is conditional on uniform weights and exactly one clause per node. Actual depth-proportional weights favor edits near leaves and explicitly break syntactic minimality guarantees. Largest consistent subtree therefore does not imply globally fewest edits in the implementation or smallest semantic change. §5.4: A satisfying model can infer a hole’s type, e.g. integer from integer-literal assignment; unresolved holes return to the LLM. Satisfying static constraints establishes a well-formed intermediate program, not equivalence to its original candidate, natural-language task satisfaction or a verified safety/liveness property. §3/Figure 1: Compiler-accepted traffic-light output declares pedestrian with var and initializes it false, although the task requires a nondeterministic input from another subsystem. Authors explicitly identify var→input as a semantic correction. Inference for distributed modeling: changing an environment input into locally controlled state can remove executions and make a property misleadingly easy to satisfy. Figure 1 lines 13–15 and Figure 5: The shown state-zero branch resets count to zero immediately before checking count<60 and incrementing it. Under the sequential step semantics suggested by the excerpt, that prevents this branch reaching 60. This is an apparent concern in a partial excerpt, not a confirmed full-model counterexample; the complete generated model and execution were not inspected. Authors discuss the pedestrian mistake, not this timer concern. Figures 5–6: Repairs replace BitVector declarations with int to match integer literal use, and replace Boolean()/False values in declarations with bool. Changing a bounded bitvector to integer changes wraparound/range behavior; static consistency alone does not preserve a bounded-state interpretation. Final snippets also show declarations/init separately and contain ellipses. §3/Figure 5: Figure 5 caption identifies (a) initial output and (b) repair; overview prose reverses these references. Figure 6 likewise contains partial outputs. Preserve the caption/code reading rather than treating the displayed snippets as a complete executable proof artifact. §6: Python implementation uses tree-sitter and Z3 MAX-SMT; local solver machine is a 2.3 GHz quad-core Intel Core i7 with 32 GB RAM, LLMs through OpenAI Python API. Repository is https://github.com/FedericoAureliano/eudoxus. This read covers the PDF, not repository code or a reproduced run. §6/Table 1: Text says allow repair loop five times; caption says limited to five LLM calls per benchmark. Self-repair baseline uses five iterations starting from zero-shot. Exact initial-call versus repair-count convention is not reconciled in the PDF; use the caption’s reported cap without silently adding an initial sixth call. §6: Three datasets have distinct roles: 33 curated developer regression tests with hand-written descriptions for language design; 317 repository regression tests self-summarized by the LLM for fine-tuning; 33 textbook exercises/examples for end-to-end evaluation. Textbooks are Baier/Katoen 2008, Lee/Seshia 2016 and Huth/Ryan 2004. No explicit split-overlap/contamination audit is given. §6: Fine-tunes GPT3t for 284 steps with learning-rate multiplier .16; no GPT4t fine-tune is reported. Baselines include one/three examples, with/without chain of thought, each on two models; two compiler-feedback self-repair variants; and fine-tuning. That is eleven baseline variants plus two Eudoxus variants, thirteen Table 1 rows; prose saying all eleven approaches is a counting slip. Abstract/§6: Abstract mentions retrieval baselines, but the specified baseline is repository few-shot prompting; no operational retrieval pipeline, retriever-quality measurement or retrieval ablation is described. Do not infer a separate implemented RAG system from that phrase. §6.1: Parse is shorthand for passing all compiler checks, including static typing, rather than grammar alone. Semantic assessment is one author’s manual reasoning plus hand-written specifications, performed only for compiled outputs. Scores run from 1 entirely wrong to 5 fully correct; ≥4 means authors believe useful, allowing minor errors. This is not an independently machine-checked end-to-end correctness result. Table 1: GPT3t Eudoxus parses 28/33=84.8%; GPT4t 24/33=72.7%. Combined 52/66=78.8% explains approximately 78% prose. Fine-tuning Table 1 is 2/33=6.1%, inconsistent with introduction 9.1%; preserve the discrepancy. Best GPT4t few-shot parses 4/33=12.1%. Percentages here are derived from printed counts. Table 1: Fully correct score-5 outputs are GPT4t 11/33 and GPT3t 9/33, not 24/33 or 28/33. Useful score≥4 outputs are 19/33 and 14/33 respectively. These derived unconditional denominators differ from the table’s conditional semantic denominators of 24 and 28 compiled outputs. §6.1: Across both Eudoxus variants, 30/33 unique tasks have at least one compiled output; across all eleven baseline variants, only 6/33 do. Eudoxus has 33/52 compiled outputs scoring ≥4, covering 24 unique tasks. Four unique baseline-useful tasks are also useful under Eudoxus. These unions are not a single model’s success rate or 33 verified specifications. §6.1: Four benchmarks hit iteration cap and three suffer implementation syntax/output-extraction/printing bugs. Their scope across model variants is unclear: the two table rows together have fourteen non-compiling outputs. Do not assign the seven reported cases independently to both models or invent an overlap explanation. §6.1, p.10: Reported GPT3t mean repair time .9 s (SD .6), LLM time 7.2 s (SD 4.6); GPT4t repair 1.8 s (SD 2.0), LLM 35.1 s (SD 24.7). These components support that LLM calls dominate reported local repair time. They are not total project compute, API dollars, token counts or a precisely specified aggregate wall-clock distribution. §6.1–§6.2: Authors explicitly caution that few baseline outputs compile, making conclusions about semantic performance difficult. No ablation separates child-language design, pruning, model-based repairs and repeated LLM calls; no broad budget-matched repeated-sampling comparison is reported. §6.2/Checklist: Only one target language, two LLMs and one semantic rater. No inter-rater agreement, semantic-score confidence intervals or statistical-significance claim; checklist item 7 explicitly calls this a case study. Generalization to other languages/models remains open. Checklist items 3–8: No theoretical results claimed. Code/data are said to be in supplemental material/GitHub; not executed here. Checklist says temperature settings are reported, but no numerical temperature is supplied in the PDF’s experimental description. Solver/compiler versions, exact seeds, repeated-run counts and solver timeouts are not disclosed there. Checklist items 10–15: Broader impacts answer No because work targets compiler checks; existing UCLID5 license stated as 3-clause BSD, textbook examples cited, new code/documentation claimed public. Human-subject and IRB questions marked NA. These are disclosure statements, not independent license or artifact verification. Whole paper: No generated distributed protocol, Verus/verified Rust proof, TLA+ specification, termination argument, concurrency refinement proof or validated natural-language specification oracle is evaluated. Formal tools repair syntax/types; UCLID5’s verification capabilities do not by themselves make generated models faithful to the task.

- TSL+LLM pipeline (2024)
    - title: Combining LLM Code Generation with Formal Specifications and Reactive Program Synthesis
    - what: The paper splits generation between an LLM and correct-by-construction Temporal Stream Logic (TSL) synthesis. GPT-4 converts structured natural-language assumptions/guarantees into TSL; a reactive synthesizer generates the control structure; an LLM fills data/predicate functions and either wraps the synthesized code or uses it as prompt seed for a complete implementation. The authors evaluate five small reactive tasks (Ball, Game of Life, Vending Machine, Space Invaders, Rotating Cube), a scaling arbiter with 10/20/30 conditions, and Conway's Game of Life prompt seeding. The TSL pipeline's arbiter success is 61.5%, 45.4%, and 16.7% for 10, 20, and 30 conditions versus 0% for LLM-only; it uses 22, 31, and 40 average lines of verified synthesized code. Prompt seeding reaches 0.80 success on Game of Life at 15 trials versus GPT-4's 0.40. Small tasks show mixed success and many unverified integration lines; failures include unsynthesizable TSL generated by few-shot prompting.
    - number: 5 small reactive tasks, 5/10/15-trial success rates
    - number: arbiter (10/20/30 conditions): TSL 61.5%/45.4%/16.7%, LLM-only 0%
    - number: arbiter synthesized verified lines: 22/31/40
    - number: Game of Life prompt-seeding success 0.80 vs GPT-4 0.40 at 15 tries
    - limits: The LLM must correctly express the natural-language requirements in TSL; an invalid or unsynthesizable specification is rejected. TSL separates temporal control from data/predicate implementation, but integration and function terms remain unverified LLM code. The study is small, uses GPT-4 few-shot prompting, and treats repeated trials/success as the metric; it does not establish correctness of natural-language interpretation or distributed-system safety. Authors state that the simple benchmarks do not always expose the pipeline's benefit and that complex unknown TSL syntax causes weak performance.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Can Large Language Models Reason about Program Invariants? (2023)
    - what: Fine-tunes 430M, 1B, and 5B-parameter code language models to predict 18 classes of Java program invariants at function entry/exit and loop points. A scratchpad prompt predicts invariants at successive program points and then the target; the paper interprets this as learning in an abstract invariant space. On Code Contests Java programs, the best models reach about 86% precision and recall and outperform Daikon when Daikon receives at most five traces. The dataset is generated by instrumenting Java submissions with dummy loop calls, running Daikon on 200 available inputs, and splitting by problem to prevent near-duplicate leakage. The authors report 1,600,158 train, 86,346 validation and 24,509 test examples. Evaluation uses Jaccard similarity, precision, recall and F1 against 200-trace Daikon targets; models use greedy decoding, 20,000 steps, batch 128 on 64 TPU-v4 cores. Table 3 shows Daikon F1 .721 with one trace, .857 with five, .868 with six and 1.00 with 200. For ordered 5B scratchpad forward prediction, F1 is .858 in both combined and separated training; combined shuffled 5B scratchpad reaches .863. Oracle reaches .867 forward and up to .877 backward across settings. GPT-4 zero-shot is weak (listed pilot precision .44, recall .384 on paper listings and no ground-truth invariants for ten random test prompts). Case listings show model-discovered valid invariants omitted by Daikon, but also spurious predictions and dependence on model scale. The paper is empirical invariant prediction, not proof: Daikon targets are treated as ground truth although dynamic analysis can miss valid invariants or produce false positives. The oracle receives the Daikon target invariants at program points preceding its target and therefore uses dynamic information, unlike direct and scratchpad inference. Backward prediction also changes the dataset: it excludes the final program point and introduces class invariants, yielding 1,284,492 training, 61,431 validation and 21,576 test examples; forward/backward scores thus have different target and example populations.
    - number: {'model_parameters': ['430M', '1B', '5B'], 'train_examples': 1600158, 'validation_examples': 86346, 'test_examples': 24509, 'traces_per_program': 200, 'invariant_types': 18, 'daikon_f1_1_trace': 0.721, 'daikon_f1_5_traces': 0.857, 'daikon_f1_200_traces': 1.0, 'best_reported_precision_recall': 'about 0.86 each', 'training_steps': 20000, 'tpu_cores': 64, 'backward_train_examples': 1284492, 'backward_validation_examples': 61431, 'backward_test_examples': 21576}
    - limits: An invariant is a Boolean predicate true whenever execution reaches a specified program point; the task is undecidable in general. Ground truth is the set Daikon emits from 200 inputs; the authors explicitly note this can miss invariants and overfit finite traces. The dataset consists of Java competitive-programming submissions with available inputs, not distributed protocols, Rust/Verus code or TLA+ specifications. Direct and scratchpad inference is static with respect to the target program and uses greedy decoding, but training labels come from dynamic traces. Oracle inference receives intermediate Daikon target invariants as additional dynamic information and is not a purely static comparator. The paper reports offline experiments and does not establish semantic correctness of model outputs beyond comparison with Daikon/manual inspection.

- PRISM proof repair infrastructure (2023)
    - title: Proof Repair Infrastructure for Supervised Models: Building a Large Proof Repair Dataset
    - what: Introduces the initial PRISM Coq proof-repair dataset and its extraction infrastructure, with roughly 200 unique changes at publication. Defines proof repair with known repaired theorem statements and kernel-checking metrics; explains build, serialization, command alignment and project-history obstacles. It proposes infrastructure and evaluation practices, not a trained proof-repair model. Inputs include old theorem/proof, new theorem, changed dependencies, project state, raw text/ASTs/identifiers and intermediate goals/hypotheses. Partially applying an aligned pair of Git commits while leaving out one proof edit produces a broken proof plus supervised repair target. Across-project and chronological splits test different generalizations but no fixed split is committed. SwiM selects/clones cached opam environments; bwrap remaps cloned switches because binaries hard-code paths. strace recovers I/Q/R dependency flags from actual builds. SerAPI supplies states, while a heuristic parser and Vernacular queries work around extensible syntax, unqualified names, missing definition/conjecture queries, nested proofs and version drift. Command alignment solves a thresholded normalized-Levenshtein bipartite assignment over changed commands; it ignores proof bodies when comparing command text, supports moves across files/reordering, and uses t=0.4 to reject poor matches. Supplementary material lists target/candidate projects and a concrete hoare-tut exgcd.v repair replacing cutrewrite with replace, showing compact commit/diff/environment encoding of a much larger state.
    - number: initial release: a few Coq projects, roughly 200 unique changes
    - number: main text expansion target: 60 opam projects; appendix lists an additional 224 candidate projects
    - number: supplement Table 1 reports 171,775 proofs and 2,984,471 sentences; Table 2 reports 335,223 proofs and 6,078,063 sentences, each approximate one-snapshot inventory rather than repair counts
    - number: build attempts: about 68% failure across Coq versions 8.9–8.15 (seven major versions)
    - limits: The primary task assumes the new theorem and dependencies have already been repaired; successful checking establishes that stated theorem, not that a changed specification captures intent. Specification/definition repair lacks a satisfactory metric; exact equality is conservative and BLEU-like distances are inadequate. Initial release has a few projects and roughly 200 changes, whereas 60-project expansion and later large-scale coverage are planned. Main text and supplementary Table 1 use inconsistent wording about whether all 60 projects are already included; appendix prose explicitly calls repositories current and pending. Supplementary proof/sentence totals describe approximations from one snapshot per repository and are not repair-instance counts. Name resolution relies on handcrafted scopes and assumptions about shadowing; conjecture tracking assumes Show Conjectures order and proof-boundary behavior; grammar extensions can defeat serialization. Roughly 68% build-failure rate counts attempts across seven Coq versions 8.9–8.15, so it is not a rate of irreparably broken projects. No trained ML model, proof-repair accuracy, fixed benchmark split, or model comparison is reported.
    - how much was read: most of it read

    - record id: `prism-proof-repair-infrastructure-2023`
- HyperTree Proof Search for Neural Theorem Proving (2022)
    - what: Introduces HyperTree Proof Search (HTPS), an online proof-search/training method for encoder-decoder policy and critic models. It expands a proof hypergraph by sampling tactics, requires all child subgoals, estimates unresolved leaves, and trains from proof-search outcomes. Evariste improves held-out Metamath/Lean results and adapts to a synthetic Equations environment; reported improvements are benchmark pass rates, not proof semantic judgments beyond each checker. Selection recursively builds partial proof trees rather than one MCTS path; expansion classifies solved/invalid/unsolved nodes; back-propagation multiplies child values under an independence assumption. Policy samples minimal proofs and critic targets solved=1, invalid=0, internal best-action values. Distributed asynchronous provers stream samples into finite queues while trainers refresh models. Online training differs from expert iteration and transductive evaluation intentionally includes statements seen during training. Ablations show minimal-proof filtering, soft critics and stochastic search parameters matter. Supplement findings: Appendix C defines solved/invalid/expandable hypergraph nodes, PUCT and prior-regularized selection, virtual counts, cycle removal and topological multiplicative backup. The implementation discards a tactic if a simulation encounters a cycle and restarts from the root; the authors explicitly acknowledge this may remove valid proofs. Search failure or an invalid node therefore need not mean the mathematical statement is false. Algorithms 1–2 are procedural descriptions, not a completeness theorem. The recovered appendix specifies 12 encoder and 6 decoder layers; encoder dimension 1600, decoder dimension 1024 for Lean/Metamath and 512 for Equations; 600M versus 440M parameters. MASS pretraining uses mathematical arXiv LaTeX, around 6B tokens/40GB. Fine-tuning uses Adam, inverse-square-root scheduling, dropout 0.2 and layer dropout 0.1; online learning fixes 3e-5 after warm-up and alternates supervised and search-generated objectives. Default Metamath/Equations experiments use 16 trainers+32 provers/48 V100 GPUs, with larger experiments excepted. Figure 7 shows asynchronous prover/trainers/controller messaging. These are source-reported settings, not independently exercised. Decoding caps are 512/128/32 tokens for Metamath/Lean/Equations. Per-proof randomized settings are tactic samples {8,16,32,48}, temperature U[0.8,2], length penalty U[0,1.2], expansion budget log-U[1000,10000], depth penalty {0.8,0.9,0.95,1}, exploration log-U[0.01,100]. Evaluation fixes expansions to 5000 Lean/10000 Metamath. Lean subgoal splitting respects shared metavariables and contiguous goal groups. Every tactic proof term is kernel checked, replacing metavariables by lambda abstractions. Version commits are printed in D.5, with Lean tag v3.3.0. This closes the earlier missing-details reading gap. Appendix E.4 reports that early approximate numerical checking let the model obtain 100% apparent accuracy and prove arbitrary statements. Figure 9 gives a false proof of 2=3 via injectivity of exp and underflow of exp(-exp(exp(2))) and exp(-exp(exp(3))) to 0.0. Figure 10 gives a false proof of 0≠0 using cos(pi/2)≈6.123e-17 and repeated square roots to exceed a tolerance. The reported fix restricts automatic numerical checking to exact rational expressions; transcendental inequalities require rules. This documents earlier checker vulnerabilities, not a claim that the reported final Lean/Metamath runs accepted those falsehoods. No independent soundness audit of the Equations checker was done. Equations goals use expression trees and assumption-bearing rewrite/assertion rules. Random walks yield easy, transformation-only proofs; random graph generation adds assertion use and shared subproofs. Identities contains 160 hand-created out-of-domain expressions. Lean export is possible only for rules also present in Lean; 1702 distinct Mathlib statements were converted, with overlapping type categories. Figure 11 supplies two synthetic Lean examples. This is constrained generation/export, not verified translation of arbitrary Equations rules. Table 9 gives selected examples among 144 identities, ordered by first solution, with size and depth for the first and smallest proof found during online training. For sin(2pi+x)=sin(x), size is 353→69 and depth 62→16; for tanh(x)+tanh(y)=sinh(x+y)/(cosh(x)cosh(y)), size 162→144 and depth 95→91. The label Best means smallest found in the reported search, not globally optimal. The caption’s 144 solved identities and inherited main-text 91.3% are retained as separately reported counts rather than silently equated. Figure 12 shows a cleaned proof that 7 does not divide 2^n+1 using modular arithmetic and induction. Figure 13 explicitly says its imo_2001_p6 proof is valid for an erroneous miniF2F statement: natural-number subtraction interprets b+d-a+c as max(b+d-a,0)+c, making the problem easier; the proof fails for the corrected statement. Checker acceptance establishes the formal statement, not that the benchmark formalization captures the intended IMO problem. Rendered Table 6 prints Transformation [74,18,9,8,109], Assertion [90,11,9,0,110], Total [171,29,18,11,219] in Basic/Exponential/Trigonometry/Hyperbolic/All order. Several displayed sums disagree (e.g. Basic 74+90=164, not 171; Hyperbolic 8+0=8, not 11). Table 9 prints cos(x)^2=1+cos(2x)/2 and cosh(x)^2=1+cosh(2x)/2 without grouping the constant in the numerator; at x=0 these printed identities fail. These are presentation discrepancies, not evidence that false printed identities were accepted by the implementation. Source values are preserved without silently repairing them.
    - limits: HTPS treats child-goal solvability as independent when multiplying values; this is a search heuristic,not a theorem about goal probabilities. Policy/critic predictions guide search and do not establish correctness; final proof validity comes from Metamath/Lean/Equations environments. Online training statements can be seen during training. Lean miniF2F-valid58.6% is cumulative pass rate on statements used in online training; test41.0% pass@64 is the cleaner held-out comparison. Metamath valid82.6% is cumulative; test72.4% pass@32 is held out. Equations is a restricted synthetic expression environment. Its training data is generated on the fly, and random-generator statements are solved easily; Identities is the harder online split. The paper does not establish transfer to Verus,Rust,TLA+ or distributed systems. All evaluations are run once; the paper says repeated evaluations would be too costly and reports no error bars. Compute is large:Lean uses32 trainers/200 provers and A100 days in Table2;Metamath uses128 trainers/256 provers and V100s. Code for overall distributed training is tied to internal infrastructure and difficult to open-source. Compared with GPT-f,reported pass rates use different training budgets and protocols. The study is a 2022 benchmark snapshot; no current model comparison or human study. Appendix C defines solved/invalid/expandable hypergraph nodes, PUCT and prior-regularized selection, virtual counts, cycle removal and topological multiplicative backup. Search failure or an invalid node therefore need not mean the mathematical statement is false. Algorithms 1–2 are procedural descriptions, not a completeness theorem. Appendix E.4 reports that early approximate numerical checking let the model obtain 100% apparent accuracy and prove arbitrary statements. Figure 9 gives a false proof of 2=3 via injectivity of exp and underflow of exp(-exp(exp(2))) and exp(-exp(exp(3))) to 0.0. Figure 10 gives a false proof of 0≠0 using cos(pi/2)≈6.123e-17 and repeated square roots to exceed a tolerance. The reported fix restricts automatic numerical checking to exact rational expressions; transcendental inequalities require rules. This documents earlier checker vulnerabilities, not a claim that the reported final Lean/Metamath runs accepted those falsehoods. No independent soundness audit of the Equations checker was done. Figure 12 shows a cleaned proof that 7 does not divide 2^n+1 using modular arithmetic and induction. Figure 13 explicitly says its imo_2001_p6 proof is valid for an erroneous miniF2F statement: natural-number subtraction interprets b+d-a+c as max(b+d-a,0)+c, making the problem easier; the proof fails for the corrected statement. Checker acceptance establishes the formal statement, not that the benchmark formalization captures the intended IMO problem. - Learning to Find Proofs and Theorems by Learning to Refine Search Strategies: The Case of Loop Invariant Synthesis (2022)
    - what: Proposes self-training AlphaZero-style teacher and solver agents that refine generic nondeterministic programs for automated theorem proving without human proof examples. Demonstrates Looprl on loop-invariant synthesis: abductive strategy code defines a search space, a teacher generates constrained diverse Code2Inv problems, and a solver learns greedy action choices. On 132 single-loop C programs, trained policy solves 61.5% without backtracking versus 39.7% using an untrained-teacher dataset and 18.4% random actions; MCTS with the generic strategy solves all benchmarks but is not the learned no-search metric. Defines choose/event nondeterministic strategy operators, conditional generative teachers, abductive reasoning and bounded event rewards. A strategy induces an MDP whose choice points are actions; AlphaZero/MCTS policy and value heads refine both teacher and solver. Looprl compiles an OCaml DSL to RL environments, provides graph-format UI/debugging, integer-linear-arithmetic abduction, and parallel Gumbel AlphaZero. Teacher constraints include invariant shape, conditionals, usefulness of guard and equalities; solver reward penalizes proof steps. A complete run uses 20 teacher +20 solver iterations, 1.6M-parameter graph transformers, 50K solver training problems, and one16-hour RTX3080 run. Supplement findings: Appendix A expands the solver strategy to disjunctions of at most three atomic candidates, optional candidate strengthening, lazy constant metavariables with global constraints, and conjectures from preserved linear combinations, relaxed guards and unmodified initial variables. Conjecturing costs -0.3, abduction -0.2, each counted at most four times, with successful reward at least zero. Figure 7 provides all 94 lines of the strategy and a pending stack encoding. This makes the hand-designed search space and shaped reward explicit; the neural policy chooses within it. Appendix B/Table 3 lists 15 sampled constraint types; penalties are -0.5 except the last four at -0.2, and successful reward is at least -0.5. Table 4 hard constraints require correctness, nontrivial/nonredundant formulas, satisfiable invariants, entering the loop, and a restricted loop-terminates test. The paper explicitly says that test rules out only a subset of nontermination cases. It must not be described as a general termination guarantee. Constraint combinations and correlations are partly hardcoded; formula/program templates and parameter-abduction shape the generated distribution. Table 5 gives 12 final transformations, including irrelevant guards/formulas, commuting or shuffling instructions, comparison normalization, moving assumptions and weakening the final assertion. An application is cancelled if it violates a hard constraint or increases soft violations. Table 6 gives Code2Inv problems 3,7,93,110 and explanatory invariants: disjunctive x<5 or y≤z; x-y≤10; 3i=x+y with i≤n; i-s=1 with i≤n+1 or s=0. Table 7 prints three generated programs/invariants, with final transformations disabled for clarity and invariants hidden before solver training. They are illustrations, not additional held-out measurements. Appendix F uses Fourier–Motzkin elimination on negated CNF clauses in linear integer arithmetic. For a single clause, negations of derived consequences suggest sufficient assumptions; contradiction can prove validity. For multiple conjuncts, the implementation returns the union of clause-local suggestions rather than all conjunction combinations. F.2 explicitly withdraws the guarantee that any suggestion implies the entire input formula. Subsequent proof checks and recursive refinement matter; treating every abduction result as a complete proof would overstate the method. Table 8 specifies teacher 20×8000 generation episodes, 800 validation episodes/iteration, max 60 steps, 64 MCTS simulations; solver 20×20000 episodes, 5000 validation/iteration, max 12 steps, 32 simulations. Both use 600 asynchronous/parallel episodes, 128-dimensional embeddings, four attention heads, 6 state/3 action/1 combiner blocks, state/action encoding limits 80/12, 16 identifier IDs and 256-node MCTS cap. Teacher/solver dropout is 0.05/0.1, batch size 400/300, learning-rate maximum 5e-4/3e-4, maximum epochs 6/1. The last five teacher iterations and 10000 extra noiseless generated problems supply solver problems. The 20000 solver episodes per iteration are distinct from the inherited 50000-problem training pool. Appendix H uses separate Dynamic Graph Transformers for state probe and each action, a combiner for softmax action scores, and a value head for outcome/event predictions. AST Polish-order tokens retain syntactic edges, data/control-dependence edges and numerical comparison edges. Identifier IDs are randomized per choice point; overflow names share a flagged ID. H.2 describes binary constant encoding generally, but Table 8 sets const-emb-size=0 for both reported agents, disabling that binary value encoding. Numerical comparison edges remain enabled. Search exceeding state/action/step limits fails or discards actions; no unrestricted completeness claim follows. Figures 8–9 expose policy probabilities, outcome/event estimates and MCTS statistics. The solver example gives prior 0.81 for abduct x>0 and predicted value 0.46 versus described true shaped reward 0.4; teacher screenshot predicts success 0.97 and soft-constraint violation risk 0.31. These are individual UI examples, not calibration experiments. Figure 7 as printed has inconsistent/unbound names in prove_preserved (suggs/assum/action) and proved_invs versus invs_proved; it is explanatory pseudocode, not a standalone executable listing. No author code was inspected to resolve the differences.
    - limits: The proof domain is Code2Inv:132 C programs with one loop, linear integer arithmetic and selected conditionals/assumptions/nondeterministic tests. The authors leave other domains for future work; general theorem-proving claims are a design vision, not demonstrated coverage. The learned result evaluates greedy highest-policy actions with no search/backtracking. It measures ability to generate an invariant of minimal size; MCTS can solve all benchmark problems, so no-search success is the reported challenge. An invariant is accepted through the benchmark/verifier pipeline; the paper does not establish semantic robustness beyond those checks. Teacher generation uses manually defined constraints and validity-preserving transformations. Constraints can be incompatible; rewards are shaped so success beats failure. Generated problem distribution and benchmark generalization depend on strategy design, not an unrestricted theorem distribution. Training protocol uses two random seeds for error intervals; one complete run takes about16h on specified hardware. No comparison across many independent training runs, hardware settings or random datasets is reported. The approach formalizes expert strategy fragments and learns choices; it does not produce Lean/Coq/Rust/Verus proof terms or TLA+ specifications in this paper. Appendix B/Table 3 lists 15 sampled constraint types; penalties are -0.5 except the last four at -0.2, and successful reward is at least -0.5. Table 4 hard constraints require correctness, nontrivial/nonredundant formulas, satisfiable invariants, entering the loop, and a restricted loop-terminates test. The paper explicitly says that test rules out only a subset of nontermination cases. It must not be described as a general termination guarantee. Constraint combinations and correlations are partly hardcoded; formula/program templates and parameter-abduction shape the generated distribution. Appendix F uses Fourier–Motzkin elimination on negated CNF clauses in linear integer arithmetic. For a single clause, negations of derived consequences suggest sufficient assumptions; contradiction can prove validity. For multiple conjuncts, the implementation returns the union of clause-local suggestions rather than all conjunction combinations. F.2 explicitly withdraws the guarantee that any suggestion implies the entire input formula. Subsequent proof checks and recursive refinement matter; treating every abduction result as a complete proof would overstate the method. Appendix H uses separate Dynamic Graph Transformers for state probe and each action, a combiner for softmax action scores, and a value head for outcome/event predictions. AST Polish-order tokens retain syntactic edges, data/control-dependence edges and numerical comparison edges. Identifier IDs are randomized per choice point; overflow names share a flagged ID. H.2 describes binary constant encoding generally, but Table 8 sets const-emb-size=0 for both reported agents, disabling that binary value encoding. Numerical comparison edges remain enabled. Search exceeding state/action/step limits fails or discards actions; no unrestricted completeness claim follows.

- NaturalProver: Grounded Mathematical Proof Generation with Language Models (2022)
    - what: # NaturalProver: supplementary reading and updated record (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  The official 16-page prose appendix supplies training/search settings, additional ablations, qualitative proofs, the complete rating schema and agreement analysis. Its most consequential additions are low agreement on correctness/usefulness, a correctness rating that includes nearly correct proofs, oracle selection in the best-of-ten next-step experiment, and a qualification of the table’s GPT-3 13B label. The earlier main-paper findings remain human-rated natural-language proof results.  The full main paper was read in formal268. This task read Appendix A–G completely, including Tables10–25, Figures3–5, Equations7–11 and embedded screenshots; it does not claim a fresh full reread of the first15 pages.  ## D1: source scope and inheritance  The official NeurIPS2022 publisher page links a supplemental ZIP containing a 16-page appendix PDF and a 31-page combined PDF. The new reading covers all Appendix A–G prose, Tables 10–25, Figures 3–5, and Equations 7–11. The original main-paper account from formal268 is preserved with attribution to that earlier full 15-page read. Only the two prose PDF members were extracted; code/data/stored-evaluation members were not opened. Cited annotation guidelines, IRB letter and consent template are not separate documents in the publisher ZIP directory listing; the external training video was not watched.  Source anchors: Q15, Q30 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  ## D2: core contribution retained  NaturalProver conditions natural-language proof generation on reference titles, trains title-to-content reconstruction, and optionally searches proof steps using reference coverage and likelihood. The main-paper 32% full-proof correctness/45% usefulness and 43% next-step correctness/51% usefulness are human ratings, with provided gold-proof references constituting privileged assistance. No formal theorem-proving guarantee is added by the appendix.  Source anchors: Q0, Q2, Q3, Q5, Q7, Q9, Q24, Q25 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  ## D3: dataset and split claims  Appendix C describes about 20k ProofWiki theorems, 20k proofs, 12k definitions and 1k other pages; about 33k reference pages and 14.5k theorem/proof examples. It inherits splits designed so dev/test theorems are not mentioned in training. Reader inference: this statement does not certify absence from the base language model’s pretraining data, nor does it forbid analogous training proofs; Tables 21–22 explicitly illustrate adaptation of close training analogues.  Source anchors: Q24, S13, Q21, Q22 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  ## D4: search scoring and compute  Appendix D formalizes expand/score/select over full proofs or proof-step segments. The value combines unique in-context reference-title count and log probability; each term is divided by its maximum absolute value across candidates. Greedy, full-proof sampling and stepwise stochastic beam have stated token-step costs T, NT and NTK. Terminated proofs stop expanding; search returns the highest-valued proof once the beam terminates. These are search descriptions, not validity proofs or runtime benchmarks.  Source anchors: Q24, Q25 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  ## D5: decoding hyperparameters  Appendix E supplies ten next-step candidates per prefix: one at temperature0, three each at .3/.5/.7. Beam size9 is split equally across alpha .1/.5/1.0 clusters; final selection uses alpha .75. Alternatives are reported as tried. Full-proof sampling uses temperature .3 selected from .1/.3/.5/.7 by GLEU+Ref-F1 on core dev. Reference inclusion and likelihood do not check the logical use of a reference.  Source anchors: Q25, Q26, S5, S14 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  ## D6: training and generation configuration  All GPT-3 variants are Curie API fine-tunes for four epochs, batch64. Other GPT-2/J/Neo variants use one Quadro RTX8000. Prompts are truncated to1024 tokens; full generation allows1020; next-step proof history900 and output120. Proof-step boundaries are inferred automatically and contiguous lines merged. Retrieval uses the previously trained joint ProofWiki retriever, unchanged, returning top20 references.  Source anchors: Q25, S3, S4 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  ## D7: model-size qualification  Tables10–11 label GPT-3 as13B, but Appendix E states these are Curie fine-tunes whose evaluation-harness performance lies between GPT-3 6.7B and13B and whose further model details are not public. Record13B as a table label rather than a confirmed Curie parameter count. The main-paper account is otherwise retained.  Source anchors: Q15, Q25, S2 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  Exact quotation (S2, Appendices D/E, Equations 10–11; full PDF physical/printed page 26; supplement PDF page 11):  > All GPT-3 models (including NATURALPROVER models) are fine-tuned > instances of the Curie engine, the second largest model available through the OpenAI API at the > time of writing.3 > The model’s performance on the EleutherAI evaluation harness4 > is between the > 6.7B and 13B variants of the autoregressive transformer language model GPT-3 from [5],5 > though > further details of the Curie model are not publicly available.  ## D8: model scale and inference compute measurements  Table10 reports GLEU/Ref-F1/Halluc of24.85/61.42/11.07 for GPT-Neo125M,32.06/65.22/6.76 for GPT-2 774M,39.14/79.23/3.51 for GPT-J6B and42.39/89.29/1.90 for GPT-3 table-labelled13B. Table11’s GPT-2 ten-sample reranking reaches Ref-F1 89.30 versus GPT-3 greedy89.29, but GLEU remains32.65 versus42.39. This closes reference matching, not proof correctness or the whole quality gap.  Source anchors: Q15 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  ## D9: constraint tradeoffs  Table12 varies alpha0 to1: Ref-F1 increases88.40 to93.60 while GLEU moves42.79 to41.90. Table13 compares LM-only stepwise stochastic beam to stepwise++: Ref-F1 84.21 to94.15 and Halluc4.60 to2.00, but GLEU41.0 to40.4. The appendix opening incorrectly names Table13 as the alpha ablation; the printed alpha table is12.  Source anchors: Q15 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  ## D10: oracle best-of-ten next-step evaluation  Table14 samples ten candidates under stochastic decoding and chooses the candidate maximizing the sum of all reported evaluation metrics with hallucination negated. Temperature1.0 yields GLEU62.12/Ref-F1 86.87 versus greedy47.87/65.50. Reader inference: this uses reference-dependent evaluation information for selection, so it measures an oracle best-of-ten opportunity rather than a deployable reranker or ten-sample human correctness improvement.  Source anchors: Q16, S1 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  Exact quotation (S1, Appendices A.2/B.1.1, Tables 14–15; full PDF physical/printed page 17; supplement PDF page 2):  > For stochastic decoding, > 10 candidates are sampled. We compute metrics using the candidate with the highest sum of all > metrics (with Hallucination negated).  ## D11: human study procedure  Appendix F.2 adds30 minutes training plus one hour moderated work/Q&A; annotators can continue for two weeks. Each is assigned25 theorems with five proofs each and may complete as many tasks as desired. The MediaWiki interface shows qualification, theorem/gold proof, generated proof, step history and questions. Payment is1 dollar plus .4 dollar per step/task,40 dollars for session attendance, minimum20 dollars/hour, total about5000 dollars. UW IRB approval and consent are source-reported, not independently checked.  Source anchors: Q26, Q28, S11 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  ## D12: correctness rating boundary  Table24 defines correctness4 as correct or nearly correct and logically coherent, while5 is correct and flows logically. Together with the main paper’s >=4 threshold, reported correct includes nearly correct human-rated proofs. The prose F.1 calls step correctness yes/no, but the actual schema also permits cannot determine (including dependence on an invalid prior step) and meaningless step (e.g. QED). Usefulness is distinct from correctness.  Source anchors: Q4, Q26, Q27, S8, S9 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  Exact quotation (S8, Appendix F, Table 24; full PDF physical/printed page 28; supplement PDF page 13):  > 4: The proof is correct or nearly correct and logically coherent. > ◦ 5: The proof is correct and flows logically.  ## D13: agreement and annotator concentration  On core dev proofs with at least two annotations, reported overall Fleiss kappa is .24. The authors report fair-to-moderate agreement on coarse error types and relatively low agreement on high-level correctness/usefulness. Figure4 additionally shows step usefulness with negative kappa. Source diversity: largest contributor supplies20% of proofs/18% of steps; the top3–4 supply about half. These observations qualify the human-rated main result; source diversity does not establish rating validity.  Source anchors: Q26, Q29, S6, S7 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  Exact quotation (S6, Appendices E/F.1–F.4; full PDF physical/printed page 27; supplement PDF page 12):  > Inter-annotator agreement. We compute inter-annotator agreement using proofs in the core dev set > that get an evaluation from two or more annotators. Overall, the annotators achieved fair agreement > (Fleiss kappa κ = 0.24). The level of agreement for each evaluation question is shown in Figure 4. > Fair to moderate agreement is reached for identifying coarse-grained error types, while the high-level > questions (i.e. correctness, usefulness) have relatively low agreement.  ## D14: full human results and denominators  Table25 reports full-proof sample counts90/88/90/92 for GPT-3/NP-retrieve/NP/NP++; step counts802/727/654/466 and665 for NP next-step. Mean overall correctness is1.94/2.49/2.41/2.68; usefulness1.80/2.34/2.43/2.75. NP++ step correctness35.41 and usefulness46.57 remain below NP next-step42.86/51.43. Full-proof and next-step evaluations differ in conditioning and samples; their percentages are not interchangeable.  Source anchors: Q29 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  ## D15: fine errors and remaining failures  NP++ still has15.24% invalid deployment,10.30% invalid justification,9.44% invalid equation and21.89% invalid derivation; coarse reference/equation rates23.61/28.54%. NP-retrieve’s equation errors37.55% exceed GPT-3’s32.54%, even though its reference errors fall. NP++ skipped-step rate4.51% exceeds NP’s2.29%. Coarse rates denote any member error and must not be summed across finer rows. No repeated-run uncertainty for these percentages is supplied.  Source anchors: Q29 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  ## D16: reference errors illustrated  Tables15–16 separate citing the right reference from using it correctly. Examples wrongly define geometric mean as(a²+b²)/2 instead of sqrt(ab), infer P(S) subset S instead of P(S) subset P(P(S)), and assert an all-points derivative property from a point-of-inflection definition. The -1<0 proof reaches a useful conclusion with an invalidly applied reference. Reader inference: lexical coverage alone cannot establish sound reasoning.  Source anchors: Q16, Q17 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  ## D17: equation failures illustrated  Table17 shows even elementary false equalities24=2³ and26=2², a trace-of-sum derivation summing over matrices incorrectly, and a discrete-uniform moment-generating proof using Pr(X=k)=n/(n+k). Several equation errors coincide with reference-deployment/justification errors. The label identifies source-rated example failures; this reading does not run a checker.  Source anchors: Q18 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  ## D18: successful and partial qualitative examples  Tables18–22 show supplied-reference stepwise++ restoring the T2-implies-T1 link for metric-space perfect normality; a direct ordered-pair proof for complex-number equality; retrieved irreducibility/connectedness references proving compact-complement connectedness; and adaptation of training proofs for real-valued pointwise addition and cosine-by-cases. Table23’s n-variable complex triangle-inequality induction has one hallucinated-reference and one repetition error and is described as otherwise correct. Claims of generalization and correctness here are the authors’ qualitative interpretation, not an independent proof audit.  Source anchors: Q19, Q20, Q21, Q22, Q23, S12 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  ## D19: printed notation and scope limits  Table16’s point-of-inflection theorem concerns f double-prime(xi)=0, but its explanatory caption prints f(xi)=0, and the generated statement describes f(eta) as a derivative while writing f(eta). Preserve this as a source notation inconsistency rather than silently repairing the quote. Tables19–23 contain raster screenshots whose equations are missing from text extraction; rendered pages were individually read. Video/guidelines/consent/IRB supporting documents were not independently inspected.  Source anchors: Q17, Q19, Q20, Q21, Q22, Q23, Q26 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  ## D20: relevance to verification and agents: reader inference  Reference reconstruction, retrieval and stepwise candidate search can inform specification/proof assistants. The appendix strengthens the reason to distinguish natural-language plausibility, lexical reference coverage, human usefulness, human correctness with low agreement, and machine-checked validity. Verus/Rust contracts, TLA+ specifications and distributed-system safety need checks of the intended state model and proof obligations; this paper evaluates none of those systems and no distributed protocol. Appendix G explicitly warns that outputs may be fallacious or misleading.  Source anchors: Q9, Q24, Q25, Q26, Q27, Q29, Q30, S10 in [quotes.json](quotes.json), with exact character/LF/page pointers.  Exact quotation (S10, Appendix G and EOF; full PDF physical/printed page 31; supplement PDF page 16):  > Our system may produce proofs of mathematical theorems that are fallacious or misleading, which > may have negative impact if deployed in real educational environments. We kindly remind potential > users that our system and models are experimental, and their outputs should be interpreted critically.  The complete numeric Tables10–14 and25 are transcribed in [evaluation_tables.json](evaluation_tables.json). Acquisition, archive scope and hashes are recorded separately. No author implementation or stored evaluation/dataset member was opened; no model, compiler, checker, solver, prover or model checker was run.  Reference titles alone enter the context; a separate reconstruction objective teaches their content. Stepwise++ samples multiple temperatures and keeps clusters with different likelihood/reference-coverage weights; this does not check logical validity. Retrieval uses top-20 titles and avoids constrained decoding because irrelevant references would create noisy constraints. Human evaluation marks reference deployment/justification/hallucination/self-loop, equation/derivation, reasoning/repetition/skipped steps, language and symbolic errors; overall 0–5 correctness uses ≥4, usefulness ≥3. ProofWiki gold proofs can themselves contain mistakes (Figure 1).
    - limits: ProofWiki proofs are community-contributed natural language/LaTeX; core evaluation selects 100 validation and 100 test theorems chosen for human comprehensibility, an easier slice. Correctness/usefulness are university-student judgments, not machine-checked; references and constraints are lexical. Human-provided references are titles from the gold proof and serve as privileged assistance/upper bound; retrieved-reference full proof correctness is 24%, vanilla fine-tuned GPT-3 13%. Annotators see theorem and gold proof before generated proof, can skip unfamiliar theorems, and evaluate model text stepwise; claims are human ratings, not formal theorem proving. Official prose appendix now fully read. Correctness4 includes nearly correct proofs; >=4 ratings are not machine certificates. Human agreement kappa.24 overall, low for correctness/usefulness. Table14 uses oracle metric-based candidate selection. Curie exact parameter count is not established despite13B table label. Separately linked video/supporting study documents unread. D3–D20.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Passport (2022)
    - title: Passport: Improving Automated Formal Verification Using Identifiers
    - what: Enhances Coq proof-synthesis models with identifier information through category vocabularies, subword sequence modeling and fully qualified path elaboration. Passport augments ASTactic, Tac and Tok models while retaining proof-assistant-guided search. Identifier categories cover global definitions, type constructors and local variables. Frequent names receive category-specific vocabulary indices; rare names are represented with subwords; paths encode module context. CoqGym evaluation trains on 97 projects/57,719 theorems and tests 26 projects/10,782 theorems with a ten-minute timeout. Ablations quantify category, subword and path effects, and repeated training measures nondeterministic variance and ensemble diversity.
    - number: Passport enhancements: ASTactic-like +29% (304 theorems), Tac +14% (136), Tok +33% (318)
    - number: Combined enhanced tools prove 38% more; enhanced+base union 45% more than base union
    - number: Identifier categories jointly prove 64% more theorem union than baseline
    - number: 20 runs average 1,279 theorems (11.9%), spread 0.4%; union 1,564 (14.5%), +22%
    - limits: Results are CoqGym-specific and depend on ASTactic/TacTok architectures, fixed ten-minute search timeout and vocabulary thresholds. Identifier usefulness is corpus/style dependent; subword encodings can overfit and local-variable gains are marginal alone. The authors could not reproduce original ASTactic results exactly and report hardware/framework nondeterminism. Proof synthesis remains bounded search, not completeness.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Design2Vec hardware verification (2021)
    - title: Learning Semantic Representations to Verify Hardware Designs
    - what: Introduces Design2Vec, a GNN representation of Verilog RTL control/data-flow graphs that predicts coverage and generates tests from simulation data, targeting scalable industrial hardware verification. Design2Vec embeds RTL token sequences and node attributes with an LSTM/word2vec plus GCN, and adds RTL IPA-GNN propagation for concurrent, non-terminating semantics. A differentiable gradient search over test parameters targets individual cover points. The paper defines cover-point/test data generation, compares semantic GNNs to black-box MLP and random/Vizier baselines, and tests transfer/generalization across IBEX v1, security-enhanced IBEX v2, and Google's TPU.
    - number: TPU coverage prediction exceeds 90% accuracy
    - number: up to 50% and average 20% improvement over black-box baselines on real designs
    - number: up to 88 fewer simulator calls for TPU and 40 fewer for IBEX versus random/black-box search
    - number: hard-point study: Design2Vec averages 20 fewer tests than Vizier and up to 40 fewer
    - limits: The approach learns from simulation labels rather than proving reachability or functional correctness; designs are restricted to synthesizable behavioral RTL and considered graphs without loops. Test parameters define input distributions and coverage is branch-oriented. TPU code/data are proprietary, so only open IBEX designs are reproducible; medians over random seeds are reported without error bars. The authors position the learned abstraction as a proxy and acknowledge it inherits best-effort simulation limits.
    - how much was read: most of it read

    - record id: `design2vec-hardware-verification-2021`
- Pattern-trained Transformer circuit synthesis (2021)
    - title: Neural Circuit Synthesis from Specification Patterns
    - what: Trains hierarchical Transformers to map decomposed LTL assumptions/guarantees to AIGER hardware circuits. To overcome scarce paired data, the authors mine 2099 assumption/guarantee patterns from 346 SYNTCOMP benchmarks, generate 200,000 specifications, and synthesize circuits with classical tools for supervision. Beam-search predictions are model-checked, allowing semantically correct circuits different from the training target. The dataset filters to at most five inputs/outputs and syntax-tree size ≤25, renames atoms uniformly, and includes realizable and unrealizable formulas. Tree positional encodings preserve syntax paths; the decoder emits a realizability token and AIGER sequence. Verification is easier than synthesis (PSPACE versus 2-EXPTIME), so candidate circuits are checked post hoc. The model generalizes to held-out generated formulas, filtered SYNTCOMP instances, classical-tool timeouts and an out-of-distribution Smart Home benchmark; it also correctly handles a prioritizing arbiter requiring input-dependent behavior.
    - number: 2099 patterns mined from 346 benchmarks; 200,000 generated specifications
    - number: Beam-16 semantic accuracy: 79.9% generated test, 66.8% filtered SYNTCOMP (145 formulas), 30.1% classical timeouts, 40.0% restricted Smart Home (44.8% at beam 8); full Smart Home 11.1%
    - number: Beam-16 semantic/syntactic: realizable 70.7% (52.6% syntactic), unrealizable 86.7% (39.0% syntactic); beam-1 realizability-token accuracy 91.4%
    - number: Beam-1/4/8/16 generated semantic accuracy 53.6/70.4/75.8/79.9%; SYNTCOMP 51.9/60.0/63.6/66.8%
    - limits: Training distribution is pattern-composed formulas with ≤5 inputs/outputs and AST size ≤25, so large or novel temporal structures are out of distribution. Circuits are generated by classical tools and evaluation measures semantic satisfaction on bounded benchmark sets; neural outputs are not guaranteed correct without model checking. Accuracy falls with circuit size, full Smart Home performance is much lower than restricted subset, and classical synthesis timeout labels may have unknown realizability. The approach predicts circuits, not a proof-producing synthesis certificate.
    - how much was read: most of it read

    - record id: `pattern-trained-transformer-circuit-synthesis-2021`
- CLN2INV (2020)
    - title: CLN2INV: Learning Loop Invariants with Continuous Logic Networks
    - what: It trains continuous logic networks on execution traces to propose loop invariants (facts that hold at every pass through a loop). It extracts SMT formulas from them. Z3 then checks the three conditions that make an invariant inductive: it holds at the start, each step preserves it, and it implies the goal.
    - number: solves all 124 valid tasks in one hour each; LoopInvGen 104, Code2Inv 90
    - number: average time 1.07 s vs 45.11 s and 266.71 s; 31.77 Z3 calls vs 3,605.43 and 16.62
    - number: without learning still solves 91 tasks
    - number: 12 extra author-built harder tasks solved; degree three to five polynomial representations fail
    - limits: It gives partial correctness of the source program only (if it finishes, the result is right). It does not handle nested loops. The trace bounds (50 iterations, width 10) limit what is learned. They do not limit the proof. The result that training reaches the global optimum holds only for linear equalities. Nine invalid Code2Inv tasks were removed.

- CoqGym (2019)
    - title: Learning to Prove Theorems via Interacting with Proof Assistants
    - what: The complete five-page official PMLR supplement is newly read: A.1 project compilation, A.2 proof-tree reconstruction, A.3 synthetic proof extraction and the full Figure A, then every production of grammar B across three pages. The original 11-page CoqGym/ASTactic main-paper record and evaluation are explicitly inherited. The supplement explains collection bias, the discarded multi-goal traces and why synthetic proofs are conditional proofs in augmented contexts; it supplies no additional success-rate experiment or system soundness theorem. Serializes kernel terms/runtime proof states through modified Coq and SerAPI while leaving the checking core untouched; represents proof trees and complete environments. Project-disjoint train/validation/test split has 43,844/13,875/13,137 proofs. Generates 1–4-step synthetic intermediate-goal proofs, but ASTactic training does not use them. Encoder embeds goal and premises with child-sum TreeLSTM; GRU decoder grows Ltac AST depth-first, attends over premises and predicts grammar productions. Argument generation selects available premise identifiers, classifies integers 1–4, or randomly selects quantified goal variables. Teacher forcing and cross-entropy train individual tactics; beam-search tactics feed DFS with duplicate-state backtracking. Combines learned decomposition with built-in automation or hammer at each goal.
    - number: about 71K proofs from 123 projects
    - limits: Compilation success and the manually installed dependency subset select the collected projects.123projects/3061files are the collected corpus, not ecosystem coverage. Tree reconstruction uses one disappearing goal per step. Tactics that affect multiple goals/goal selectors are discarded, with reported loss<1%; the denominator is described as data without a detailed sampling/count audit. Synthetic theorem contexts may include generalized descendant obligations H3/H4. This shortens a conditional proof; it does not establish an unconditional shorter proof of the original theorem or independent novel mathematical evidence. The grammar starts at tactic_expr, permits listed atomic tactics, identifiers and small integers1–4, and lists fixed hint databases. It has no arbitrary Ltac sequencing, custom tactics or arbitrary Coq-term synthesis; restriction is deliberate. Inherited main-paper limits still apply: at most10environmental premises plus local context; teacher-forced supervised tactic training; synthetic proofs not used by the reported ASTactic; search/tactic/time budgets; no modern LLM, distributed protocol, Rust/Verus or TLA+ experiment. Only printed grammar/proof examples were read. No repository, Coq implementation, dataset, generated proof corpus or solver/prover execution was inspected or run.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Optimizing the Cost-Quality Tradeoff of Agentic Theorem Provers in Lean (2026)
    - what: A Lean-proving agent with a cheap controller: after each failed proof attempt, a logistic-regression model predicts the chance and cost of the next attempt and decides whether to retry the current target or restart from a new lemma breakdown.
    - number: On 43 held-out PutnamBench problems, average cost falls 28.9% at equal solve rate, or +7.9% accuracy at equal cost (area under cost-quality curve)
    - number: High-accuracy point: baseline solves 44.0% for 28.5 M SFLOPs vs agent 43.6% for 20.4 M SFLOPs
    - number: An oracle that knows true success odds cuts cost 62.0%
    - number: Lemma-decomposition pipeline with the 8B prover solves 48 of 85 problems vs 23 for whole-proof pass@256
    - limits: Small benchmark: 85 PutnamBench problems already solvable by a stronger prover, split 42 train / 43 test; the router only chooses attempt or stop; far from the oracle router; all about math competition proofs, not systems code.
